Chapter 1

Milnor §1, pp. 1–9: smooth manifolds, regular values, the fundamental theorem of algebra

V1.1

Inverse function theorem, manifold form

stronger
proved, not quotedany class C^n with n ≥ 1complete, not finite-dimensional

Milnor §1, Inverse Function Theorem · p. 4

For a smooth $f : U \to \mathbb R^k$ with $U$ open in $\mathbb R^k$, Milnor states on p. 4 that if the derivative $df_x : \mathbb R^k \to \mathbb R^k$ is nonsingular then $f$ carries every sufficiently small open set $U'$ about $x$ diffeomorphically onto the open set $f(U')$. He gives no proof, referring to Apostol and to Dieudonné, and warns that $f$ may fail to be injective in the large even if $df_x$ is nonsingular everywhere. The manifold version is never stated: on p. 7 he simply says that at a regular point $f$ carries a neighbourhood of $x$ in $M$ diffeomorphically onto an open subset of $N$.

One of the few results in the book quoted from the literature rather than proved.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ and $N$ be manifolds without boundary over the real or complex scalars, $x \in M$, and let $f$ be of class $C^n$ with $n \ge 1$ on a neighbourhood of $x$, with $df_x$ invertible as a continuous linear map. Then $f$ is a $C^n$ local diffeomorphism at $x$: it restricts to a $C^n$ diffeomorphism from an open $U \ni x$ onto an open $V \ni f(x)$. The model space of $M$ is assumed complete but not finite-dimensional, and equality of dimensions is replaced by invertibility of $df_x$. A second form assumes $f$ of class $C^n$ everywhere, which is Milnor's hypothesis.

Milnor/Chapter1/InverseFunction.lean, line 119  ·  Milnor.isLocalDiffeomorphAt_of_isInvertible_mfderiv

theorem isLocalDiffeomorphAt_of_isInvertible_mfderiv
    (hf : ∀ᶠ y in 𝓝 x, ContMDiffAt I J n f y) (hn : n ≠ 0)
    (h : (mfderiv I J f x).IsInvertible) :
    IsLocalDiffeomorphAt I J n f x := …

Milnor/Chapter1/InverseFunction.lean, line 254  ·  Milnor.ContMDiff.isLocalDiffeomorphAt_of_isInvertible_mfderiv

theorem ContMDiff.isLocalDiffeomorphAt_of_isInvertible_mfderiv
    (hf : ContMDiff I J n f) (hn : n ≠ 0) (h : (mfderiv I J f x).IsInvertible) :
    IsLocalDiffeomorphAt I J n f x := …

What differs

  • Milnor quotes the Euclidean case from Apostol and Dieudonné; the formal statement is proved, and it is the manifold case, which he uses from p. 7 on without stating it.
  • Milnor's $f$ is smooth; the formal statement allows real or complex scalars and class $C^n$ for any $n \ge 1$, the local inverse being of the same class.
  • Equal dimensions are replaced by invertibility of $df_x$ as a continuous linear map, and the model space of $M$ need only be complete, not finite-dimensional.
  • The hypothesis is that $f$ is $C^n$ on a neighbourhood of $x$, not merely at $x$: for $n = \infty$ the weaker form gives no single open set on which $f$ is $C^\infty$.
V1.2

Regular points and regular values

restated
surjective, not nonsingularno dimension hypothesis

Milnor §1, p. 7

For a smooth $f : M \to N$ between manifolds of the same dimension, Milnor calls $x \in M$ a regular point when $df_x$ is nonsingular, and $y \in N$ a regular value when $f^{-1}(y)$ contains only regular points; otherwise $x$ is a critical point and $f(x)$ a critical value, and every $y \in N$ is one or the other according as $f^{-1}(y)$ does or does not meet the critical set. A footnote promises that the equal-dimension restriction will be dropped in §2, where (p. 10) a regular point is one at which $df_x$ is onto.

The two definitions, §1 by nonsingularity and §2 by surjectivity, agree exactly when $\dim M = \dim N$.

Proved in Lean

The formal definitions take the §2 form from the start: $x$ is a regular point when $df_x : TM_x \to TN_{f(x)}$ is surjective and a critical point otherwise; $y$ is a regular value when every $x$ with $f(x) = y$ is a regular point, and a critical value when it is the image of a critical point. No relation between the dimensions enters. That the two definitions agree is a separate statement, proved when both model spaces are finite-dimensional of the same rank; and a point outside the image of $f$ is recorded as a regular value.

Milnor/Chapter1/RegularValue.lean, line 65  ·  Milnor.IsRegularPoint

def IsRegularPoint (f : M → N) (x : M) : Prop := …

Milnor/Chapter1/RegularValue.lean, line 74  ·  Milnor.IsRegularValue

def IsRegularValue (f : M → N) (y : N) : Prop := …

Milnor/Chapter1/RegularValue.lean, line 103  ·  Milnor.IsRegularPoint.isInvertible_mfderiv

theorem IsRegularPoint.isInvertible_mfderiv (hdim : finrank 𝕜 E = finrank 𝕜 F)
    (h : IsRegularPoint I J f x) : (mfderiv I J f x).IsInvertible := …

Milnor/Chapter1/RegularValue.lean, line 92  ·  Milnor.IsRegularValue.of_notMem_range

theorem IsRegularValue.of_notMem_range (hy : y ∉ range f) : IsRegularValue I J f y := …

What differs

  • The formal definition of a regular point is Milnor's §2 one, used from §1 onward, so no equality of dimensions is built into the definition.
  • The equivalence with Milnor's §1 definition is a theorem, needing both model spaces finite-dimensional of equal rank; it is what turns surjectivity back into invertibility.
  • That a point outside the image of $f$ is vacuously a regular value is stated on its own, because the counting argument of p. 9 applies to such points.
V1.3

A regular fibre over a compact manifold is finite

stronger
points of $N$ closedany class C^n with n ≥ 1

Milnor §1, p. 8

Milnor observes, in italics and without a label, that if $M$ is compact and $y \in N$ is a regular value then $f^{-1}(y)$ is a finite set, possibly empty. His reason is that $f^{-1}(y)$ is a closed subset of the compact $M$, hence compact, and discrete because $f$ is injective near each of its points.

He treats it as an immediate consequence of the inverse function theorem, in one sentence of proof.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ and $N$ be manifolds without boundary over the real or complex scalars, modelled on finite-dimensional spaces of equal dimension, with $M$ compact and points of $N$ closed, and let $f$ be of class $C^n$ for some $n \ge 1$. If $y$ is a regular value of $f$ then $f^{-1}(y)$ is finite. The hypothesis on $N$ is exactly the $T_1$ axiom, needed so that $f^{-1}(y)$ is closed.

Milnor/Chapter1/RegularValue.lean, line 133  ·  Milnor.IsRegularValue.finite_preimage

theorem IsRegularValue.finite_preimage [CompactSpace M] [T1Space N]
    (hdim : finrank 𝕜 E = finrank 𝕜 F) (hf : ContMDiff I J n f) (hn : n ≠ 0)
    (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) : (f ⁻¹' {y}).Finite := …

What differs

  • The formal statement names the hypothesis that points of $N$ are closed; Milnor's manifolds lie in Euclidean space, so this is automatic for him and he uses it silently.
  • Milnor's $f$ is smooth; the formal statement needs only class $C^n$ for some $n \ge 1$, and allows complex as well as real scalars.
  • Neither statement assumes $M$ or $N$ connected, nonempty, or second countable.
V1.4

Local constancy of the fibre count

stronger
no restriction on the nearby $y'$$M$ and $N$ Hausdorff

Milnor §1, p. 8

For $f : M \to N$ with $M$ compact and $y$ a regular value, Milnor writes $\#f^{-1}(y)$ for the number of preimages and says it is locally constant as a function of $y$, adding the parenthesis that $y$ is to range only over regular values. The explanation he gives is a neighbourhood $V$ of $y$ with $\#f^{-1}(y') = \#f^{-1}(y)$ for every $y' \in V$, built as the intersection of the images of disjoint neighbourhoods $U_1, \dots, U_k$ of the preimages, minus the image of the rest of $M$.

His bracketed construction of $V$ puts no condition on $y'$, so his own proof gives the equality at every $y'$ in $V$.

Proved in Lean

With $M$ compact, $M$ and $N$ Hausdorff manifolds without boundary modelled on finite-dimensional spaces of equal dimension, $f$ of class $C^n$ for some $n \ge 1$, and $y$ a regular value, there is a neighbourhood of $y$ on which $\#f^{-1}(y')$ equals $\#f^{-1}(y)$ for every point $y'$, regular or not. A companion statement adds that every such $y'$ is again a regular value, which is what Chapter 4 uses.

Milnor/Chapter1/RegularValue.lean, line 164  ·  Milnor.IsRegularValue.eventually_ncard_preimage_eq

theorem IsRegularValue.eventually_ncard_preimage_eq [CompactSpace M] [T2Space M] [T2Space N]

    (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) :
    ∀ᶠ y' in 𝓝 y, (f ⁻¹' {y'}).ncard = (f ⁻¹' {y}).ncard := …

Milnor/Chapter1/RegularValue.lean, line 242  ·  Milnor.IsRegularValue.eventually_isRegularValue_and_ncard

theorem IsRegularValue.eventually_isRegularValue_and_ncard [CompactSpace M] [T2Space M]
    [T2Space N] (hdim : finrank 𝕜 E = finrank 𝕜 F) (hf : ContMDiff I J n f) (hn : n ≠ 0)
    (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) :
    ∀ᶠ y' in 𝓝 y, IsRegularValue I J f y' ∧ (f ⁻¹' {y'}).ncard = (f ⁻¹' {y}).ncard := …

What differs

  • Milnor's parenthesis restricts the argument of $\#f^{-1}$ to regular values; the formal conclusion holds for every $y'$ in the neighbourhood, which is what his construction of $V$ actually delivers.
  • Hausdorffness of $M$ (to separate the finitely many preimages) and of $N$ (so that the image of the complement of the $U_i$ is closed) are hypotheses here and silent in the book.
  • The companion statement, that the nearby $y'$ are themselves regular values, is not in the book; it is proved from the local inverses that the same argument produces.
V1.5

The counting principle behind the FTA

restated
a proof step made a theoremarbitrary $M$ and $N$

Milnor §1, p. 9

In the last paragraph of the proof of the fundamental theorem of algebra Milnor argues that the regular values of $f$, being the sphere minus finitely many points, form a connected set, so the locally constant function $\#f^{-1}(y)$ is constant there; since it cannot vanish identically it vanishes nowhere, and $f$ is onto. He states no theorem: the argument is written for the particular map $f : S^2 \to S^2$ coming from the polynomial.

Nothing in the paragraph uses polynomials or spheres beyond compactness, equal dimension and connectedness of the set of regular values.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be compact, $M$ and $N$ Hausdorff manifolds without boundary modelled on finite-dimensional spaces of equal dimension, and $f$ of class $C^n$ for some $n \ge 1$. The fibre count is constant on any preconnected set of regular values; hence if $R$ is a preconnected set of regular values meeting the image of $f$, then $R$ lies in the image. If the whole set of regular values is preconnected and meets the image, $f$ is surjective, critical values being in the image by definition.

Milnor/Chapter1/Counting.lean, line 60  ·  Milnor.subset_range_of_isPreconnected

    (hdim : finrank 𝕜 E = finrank 𝕜 F) (hf : ContMDiff I J n f) (hn : n ≠ 0)
    {R : Set N} (hR : R ⊆ regularValues I J f) (hconn : IsPreconnected R)
    (hne : ∃ y₀ ∈ R, y₀ ∈ range f) : R ⊆ range f := …

Milnor/Chapter1/Counting.lean, line 45  ·  Milnor.ncard_preimage_eq_of_isPreconnected

theorem ncard_preimage_eq_of_isPreconnected [CompactSpace M] [T2Space M] [T2Space N]
    (hdim : finrank 𝕜 E = finrank 𝕜 F) (hf : ContMDiff I J n f) (hn : n ≠ 0)
    {R : Set N} (hR : R ⊆ regularValues I J f) (hconn : IsPreconnected R)
    {y y' : N} (hy : y ∈ R) (hy' : y' ∈ R) :
    (f ⁻¹' {y}).ncard = (f ⁻¹' {y'}).ncard := …

What differs

  • Milnor states nothing here: the reasoning is three sentences inside the proof for $f : S^2 \to S^2$, and the formal version isolates it as a theorem about arbitrary $M$ and $N$.
  • The formal statement comes in a local form, for any preconnected set $R$ of regular values meeting the image, and a global form, which is the sentence Milnor uses.
  • The last step, that critical values are in the image by definition, is left implicit in the book and is discharged explicitly in the global form.
  • As in V1.3 and V1.4, $M$ and $N$ are required Hausdorff and $f$ only of class $C^n$ with $n \ge 1$.
V1.6

The fundamental theorem of algebra

as in the book

Milnor §1, The fundamental theorem of algebra · p. 8

Every nonconstant complex polynomial $P(z)$ has a zero. Milnor proves it by passing from the plane to the unit sphere $S^2 \subset \mathbb R^3$: stereographic projection $h_+$ from the north pole turns $P$ into a map $f : S^2 \to S^2$ fixing the north pole, which is smooth there because in the chart $h_-$ it becomes $z^n$ divided by the polynomial with conjugated and reversed coefficients. The critical points are the zeros of $P'$, finite in number, so the regular values form a connected set, and the counting argument makes $f$ onto.

The proof is offered as the first application of regular values, at the end of §1.

Proved in Lean

Every complex polynomial of positive degree has a root; for polynomials over $\mathbb C$ this is Milnor's nonconstancy hypothesis verbatim. The compact surface is the Riemann sphere $\mathbb C \cup \{\infty\}$ with the charts $z$ and $1/z$, rather than $S^2$ with stereographic projection; the two are diffeomorphic, a fact neither needed nor proved. In that presentation the map read in the chart $1/z$ at both ends is $w^n$ over the reversed polynomial, with the original coefficients and no conjugation, and $\infty$ is simply placed among the critical values instead of being shown regular.

Milnor/Chapter1/FTA.lean, line 284  ·  Milnor.fundamental_theorem_of_algebra

theorem fundamental_theorem_of_algebra (P : Polynomial ℂ) (hP : 0 < P.degree) :
    ∃ z : ℂ, P.IsRoot z := …

Chapter 2

Milnor §2, pp. 10–15: Sard–Brown, manifolds with boundary, the Brouwer fixed point theorem

V2.1

Slice submanifolds of an abstract manifold

not in the book
replaces subsets of $\mathbb R^k$dimension carried by a model space $Z$

Milnor §2, p. 11

The book has no notion of submanifold, because a manifold is by definition a subset of $\mathbb R^k$ that is locally diffeomorphic to an open subset of $\mathbb R^m$. In the proof of Lemma 1 Milnor produces a diffeomorphism $F$ of a neighbourhood of $x$ onto an open set under which $f^{-1}(y)$ becomes a coordinate hyperplane, and concludes at once that $f^{-1}(y)$ is a smooth manifold.

Being a manifold is a property of a subset of $\mathbb R^k$ for him, so no structure ever has to be built.

Proved in Lean

A slice chart for $S \subseteq M$ is a diffeomorphism $\varphi$ of an open set $U \subseteq M$ onto an open subset of $Z \times W$ with $p \in S \iff \varphi(p) \in Z \times \{0\}$ for $p \in U$; $S$ is a slice submanifold modelled on $Z$ when every point of $S$ lies in such a $U$. The formal statement is that such an $S$, with the subspace topology and the charts $p \mapsto \mathrm{pr}_Z \varphi(p)$ induced by all its slice charts, is a smooth manifold without boundary modelled on $Z$, and that the inclusion $S \to M$ is smooth. Nothing is assumed finite-dimensional: $Z$ and $W$ are arbitrary real normed spaces and the ambient $M$ may itself have boundary.

Milnor/Chapter2/Submanifold.lean, line 116  ·  Milnor.IsSliceSubmanifold

def IsSliceSubmanifold (S : Set M) : Prop := …

Milnor/Chapter2/Submanifold.lean, line 107  ·  Milnor.SliceChart

structure SliceChart (S : Set M) where

Milnor/Chapter2/Submanifold.lean, line 236  ·  Milnor.IsSliceSubmanifold.isManifold

theorem isManifold : letI := hS.chartedSpace; IsManifold 𝓘(ℝ, Z) ∞ S := …

Milnor/Chapter2/Submanifold.lean, line 247  ·  Milnor.IsSliceSubmanifold.contMDiff_val

theorem contMDiff_val : letI := hS.chartedSpace; ContMDiff 𝓘(ℝ, Z) I ∞ (Subtype.val : S → M) := …

What differs

  • For a subset of $\mathbb R^k$ the straightening chart of Lemma 1 already is the definition of a manifold, so Milnor's proof ends there; for an abstract $M$ the atlas has to be built.
  • The definition takes exactly what Milnor's proof produces, a subset covered by straightening diffeomorphisms, and every later "is a manifold" in the formal text reduces to exhibiting such charts.
  • Milnor's dimension $m - n$ becomes a hypothesis on the model space $Z$, and the atlas consists of the charts induced by all slice charts, not only the chosen ones.
V2.2

Lemma 1: the preimage of a regular value

hypotheses explicit
dimension carried by a model space $Z$$m \ge n$ becomes the existence of $Z$boundaryless models named

Milnor §2, Lemma 1 · p. 11

If $f : M \to N$ is a smooth map between manifolds of dimensions $m \ge n$ and $y \in N$ is a regular value, then $f^{-1}(y) \subseteq M$ is a smooth manifold of dimension $m - n$. The proof completes $f$ by a linear map $L : \mathbb R^k \to \mathbb R^{m-n}$ nonsingular on the null space of $df_x$, inverts $F = (f, L)$ near $x$, and observes that $f^{-1}(y)$ becomes the hyperplane $y \times \mathbb R^{m-n}$.

That $M$ and $N$ are finite-dimensional and have no boundary is part of his definition of a manifold, so he states neither.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$, $N$ be $C^\infty$ manifolds without boundary modelled on finite-dimensional real spaces $E$, $F$, let $f : M \to N$ be smooth, and let $y$ be a regular value, meaning $df_x$ is surjective at every $x$ with $f(x) = y$. Let $Z$ be a finite-dimensional real space with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$. Then $f^{-1}(y)$ is a slice submanifold of $M$ modelled on $Z$, hence by V2.1 a manifold of dimension $\dim M - \dim N$ with smooth inclusion. A refined form records that the slice chart at $x$ can be taken with second component $p \mapsto \psi(f(p)) - \psi(y)$, where $\psi$ is the extended chart of $N$ at $y$.

Milnor/Chapter2/LevelSet.lean, line 251  ·  Milnor.isSliceSubmanifold_preimage

    (hf : ContMDiff I J ∞ f) {y : N} (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) :
    IsSliceSubmanifold I Z F (f ⁻¹' {y}) := fun x hx =>

theorem exists_sliceChart_preimage {Z : Type*} [NormedAddCommGroup Z] [NormedSpace ℝ Z]
    [FiniteDimensional ℝ Z] (hZ : finrank ℝ Z + finrank ℝ F = finrank ℝ E)
    (hf : ContMDiff I J ∞ f) {y : N} (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) {x : M} (hx : x ∈ f ⁻¹' {y}) :
    ∃ σ : SliceChart I Z F (f ⁻¹' {y}), x ∈ σ.φ.source ∧
      ∀ p ∈ σ.φ.source, (σ.φ p).2 = extChartAt J y (f p) - extChartAt J y y := …

What differs

  • The dimension count $m - n$ is carried by a model space $Z$ with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$; Milnor's hypothesis $m \ge n$ is precisely the existence of such a $Z$.
  • Finite-dimensionality of $E$ and $F$ and boundarylessness of both models are named, being part of Milnor's definition of a manifold rather than of his statement.
  • The conclusion is that $f^{-1}(y)$ is a slice submanifold in the sense of V2.1; the smooth structure and the smooth inclusion come from there.
  • The refined form, which names the second component of the chart, is not in the book; Lemma 3 is proved from it.
V2.3

Lemma 2: the tangent space of a level set

restated
elementwise, not an equality of spacessecond clause is V2.12

Milnor §2, Lemma 2 · p. 11

For a regular value $y$ of $f : M \to N$ and $M' = f^{-1}(y)$, the null space of $df_x : TM_x \to TN_y$ is precisely the tangent space $TM'_x \subseteq TM_x$; hence $df_x$ carries the orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ in $TM_x$ isomorphically onto $TN_y$.

He proves it from the square $M' \to M \to N$: $df_x$ kills $TM'_x$ because $f$ is constant on $M'$, and a dimension count finishes both claims.

Proved in Lean

In the setting of V2.2, give $f^{-1}(y)$ the structure of V2.1 and let $\iota : f^{-1}(y) \to M$ be the inclusion. For $p \in f^{-1}(y)$ and $v \in T_pM$: $v$ lies in the image of $d\iota_p$ if and only if $df_p(v) = 0$. Two separate statements supply the halves: $df_p \circ d\iota_p = 0$, because $f \circ \iota$ is constant, and $d\iota_p$ is injective for any slice submanifold, because $\mathrm{pr}_Z \circ \varphi \circ \iota$ is the induced chart at $p$ and has derivative the identity there.

Milnor/Chapter2/LevelSet.lean, line 325  ·  Milnor.mem_range_mfderiv_val_iff

    (hf : ContMDiff I J ∞ f) {y : N} (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) (p : f ⁻¹' {y}) (v : E) :

What differs

  • The tangent spaces of $M$ and of $f^{-1}(y)$ are modelled on different spaces, $E$ and $Z$, so the formal statement compares vectors instead of asserting $\operatorname{im} d\iota_p = \ker df_p$.
  • Milnor's second sentence, that $df_x$ maps the orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ isomorphically onto $TN_y$, is a row of its own, V2.12: it needs an inner product on the model space, which an abstract $M$ does not carry.
  • The injectivity of $d\iota_p$, which Milnor gets free from $TM'_x \subseteq TM_x$, is proved for every slice submanifold and is what makes the dimension count available.
V2.4

Manifolds with boundary: the definition

restated
$B$ prescribed, not derivedwell-definedness not proved$X$ sits in an ambient manifold $M$

Milnor §2, Definition · p. 12

With $\mathbb H^m = \{x \in \mathbb R^m : x_m \ge 0\}$ and $\partial \mathbb H^m = \mathbb R^{m-1} \times 0$, a subset $X \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ is a smooth $m$-manifold with boundary if each of its points has a neighbourhood in $X$ diffeomorphic to an open subset of $\mathbb H^m$; $\partial X$ is the set of points corresponding to $\partial \mathbb H^m$ under such a diffeomorphism. He then records that $\partial X$ is well defined and is a smooth $(m-1)$-manifold, that $X - \partial X$ is an $m$-manifold, and that $TX_x$ is $m$-dimensional even at a boundary point.

He calls the well-definedness of $\partial X$ "not hard to show" and gives no proof.

Proved in Lean

The half-space is $H(Z) = \{(t,z) \in \mathbb R \times Z : t \ge 0\}$, made into a model with corners whose image has interior $\{t > 0\}$ and frontier $\{t = 0\}$. For subsets $X$, $B$ of an ambient manifold $M$, a half-slice chart is a diffeomorphism $\Phi$ of an open $U \subseteq M$ onto an open subset of $\mathbb R \times Z$ with $p \in X \iff \Phi_1(p) \ge 0$ and $p \in B \iff \Phi_1(p) = 0$ for all $p \in U$, and $X$ is a half-slice submanifold with boundary $B$ when every point of $X$ lies in such a $U$. The formal statement is that such an $X$ is a smooth manifold modelled on $H(Z)$, that its boundary in Mathlib's sense is exactly $X \cap B$, and that the inclusion $X \to M$ is smooth.

Milnor/Chapter2/Boundary.lean, line 150  ·  Milnor.IsHalfSliceSubmanifold

def IsHalfSliceSubmanifold (X B : Set M) : Prop := …

Milnor/Chapter2/Boundary.lean, line 140  ·  Milnor.HalfSliceChart

structure HalfSliceChart (X B : Set M) where

Milnor/Chapter2/Boundary.lean, line 296  ·  Milnor.IsHalfSliceSubmanifold.isManifold

theorem isManifold : letI := hX.chartedSpace; IsManifold (halfSpaceModel Z) ∞ X := …

Milnor/Chapter2/Boundary.lean, line 305  ·  Milnor.IsHalfSliceSubmanifold.boundary_eq

theorem boundary_eq : letI := hX.chartedSpace; (halfSpaceModel Z).boundary X = {q | q.1 ∈ B} := …

What differs

  • Milnor reads $\partial X$ off the charts and then remarks that it is well defined; the formal definition prescribes $B$ in advance and demands that every chart send exactly $B$ to $\{t = 0\}$.
  • So the invariance argument behind the well-definedness is never carried out; every manifold with boundary in the formal text is built with charts respecting a given $B$, which suffices downstream.
  • The distinguished coordinate is written first, $\{t \ge 0\} \subseteq \mathbb R \times Z$, where Milnor writes it last in $\mathbb H^m$.
  • $X$ is always a subset of an ambient manifold $M$, never an abstract manifold with boundary standing on its own; $M$ itself is allowed to have boundary.
V2.5

Lemma 3: the region where $g \ge 0$

hypotheses explicit
dimension carried by a model space $Z$boundary prescribed as in V2.4

Milnor §2, Lemma 3 · p. 12

Let $M$ be a manifold without boundary and $g : M \to \mathbb R$ a smooth function with $0$ as a regular value. Then the set of $x \in M$ with $g(x) \ge 0$ is a smooth manifold with boundary equal to $g^{-1}(0)$.

He gives no proof, saying only that it is just like the proof of Lemma 1.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a $C^\infty$ manifold without boundary modelled on a finite-dimensional real space $E$, let $Z$ be a finite-dimensional real space with $\dim Z + 1 = \dim E$, and let $g : M \to \mathbb R$ be smooth with $0$ a regular value. Then $\{g \ge 0\}$ is a half-slice submanifold over $Z$ with boundary $\{g = 0\}$, hence by V2.4 a manifold with boundary modelled on the half-space over $Z$.

Milnor/Chapter2/WithBoundary.lean, line 96  ·  Milnor.isHalfSliceSubmanifold_of_regularValue

theorem isHalfSliceSubmanifold_of_regularValue {Z : Type*} [NormedAddCommGroup Z]
    [NormedSpace ℝ Z] [FiniteDimensional ℝ Z] (hZ : finrank ℝ Z + 1 = finrank ℝ E)
    {g : M → ℝ} (hg : ContMDiff I 𝓘(ℝ, ℝ) ∞ g) (h0 : IsRegularValue I 𝓘(ℝ, ℝ) g 0) :
    IsHalfSliceSubmanifold I Z {p | 0 ≤ g p} {p | g p = 0} := …

What differs

  • The dimension of the boundary is carried by a model space $Z$ with $\dim Z + 1 = \dim E$, which in particular forces $\dim M \ge 1$; Milnor leaves the count implicit.
  • The conclusion is stated with the half-slice charts of V2.4, with $B = \{g = 0\}$ prescribed in advance rather than read off the charts.
  • Milnor's "just like the proof of Lemma 1" is made literal: at a boundary point the slice chart of V2.2 for $g$ has second component $g$, and swapping the factors puts $g$ first.
  • Regularity of $0$ is used only where $g(x) = 0$; at points with $g(x) > 0$ any chart of $M$, straightened by a linear isomorphism and translated, is a half-slice chart.
V2.6

Lemma 4: level sets in a manifold with boundary

narrower
$X$ presented as $\{g \ge 0\}$$y$ regular for $f$ on all of $M$transversality replaces $f|\partial X$

Milnor §2, Lemma 4 · p. 13

Let $f : X \to N$ be smooth from an $m$-manifold with boundary to an $n$-manifold, with $m > n$. If $y \in N$ is a regular value both of $f$ and of the restriction $f|_{\partial X}$, then $f^{-1}(y) \subseteq X$ is a smooth $(m-n)$-manifold with boundary, and $\partial(f^{-1}(y))$ is exactly $f^{-1}(y) \cap \partial X$.

In the local model $f : \mathbb H^m \to \mathbb R^n$ he uses the regularity of $f|_{\partial \mathbb H^m}$ only to know that the null space of $df_x$ is not contained in $\mathbb R^{m-1} \times 0$.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$, $N$ be manifolds without boundary modelled on finite-dimensional $E$, $F$; let $Z$, $Z'$ be finite-dimensional with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$ and $\dim Z' + 1 = \dim Z$; let $f : M \to N$ be smooth with $y$ a regular value of $f$ on all of $M$, and $g : M \to \mathbb R$ smooth. Assume that at every $x$ with $f(x) = y$ and $g(x) = 0$ there is a $v \in T_xM$ with $df_x(v) = 0$ and $dg_x(v) \ne 0$. Then, inside the manifold $f^{-1}(y)$ of V2.2, the set $\{g \ge 0\}$ is a half-slice submanifold over $Z'$ with boundary $\{g = 0\}$: that is, $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g \ge 0\}$ is a manifold with boundary $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}$. No regularity of $0$ for $g$ is assumed anywhere.

Milnor/Chapter2/WithBoundary.lean, line 194  ·  Milnor.isHalfSliceSubmanifold_levelSet

What differs

  • Milnor's $X$ is an arbitrary $m$-manifold with boundary; the formal $X$ is $\{g \ge 0\}$ inside a boundaryless $M$ on which $f$ is defined, which covers every later use but not his statement.
  • His hypothesis that $y$ is regular for $f|_{\partial X}$ becomes the transversality condition $df_x(v) = 0$, $dg_x(v) \ne 0$, which is what his proof extracts from it.
  • $y$ must be a regular value of $f$ on all of $M$, not only on $X$ as in the book; this holds in every application in the formal text.
  • Milnor's $m > n$ is the existence of $Z'$ with $\dim Z' + 1 = \dim Z$; unlike Lemma 3 this statement needs no regular value of $g$, since transversality already forces $dg_x \ne 0$ where it matters.
V2.7

The unit disk, with boundary the sphere

hypotheses explicit
ambient dimension at least $1$inner product space, not $\mathbb R^m$

Milnor §2, Example · p. 12

The unit disk $\mathbb D^m$, consisting of the $x \in \mathbb R^m$ with $1 - \sum x_i^2 \ge 0$, is a smooth manifold with boundary equal to $S^{m-1}$.

It is offered as the example produced by Lemma 3, with no verification of the regular value.

Proved in Lean

For a finite-dimensional real inner product space $E$ and a finite-dimensional $Z$ with $\dim Z + 1 = \dim E$, the closed unit ball of $E$ is a half-slice submanifold over $Z$ with boundary the unit sphere. The case recorded is that for every $m \ge 0$ the closed unit ball of $\mathbb R^{m+1}$ is a manifold with boundary $S^m$, modelled on the half-space over $\mathbb R^m$. Both are Lemma 3 for $g(x) = 1 - \|x\|^2$, whose derivative $v \mapsto -2\langle x, v\rangle$ is shown to be surjective wherever $\|x\| = 1$.

V2.8

Corestriction to a submanifold

not in the book
silent step of Lemma 5values in $S$ at every point

Milnor §2, p. 13

The book has no such statement: for subsets of $\mathbb R^k$, a smooth map with values in $S$ is a smooth map into $S$ by definition, so the question never arises. Milnor uses the fact silently in Lemma 5, where a map $f : X \to \partial X$ is treated as a map into the manifold $\partial X$ so that a regular value of it may be chosen.

Proved in Lean

Let $S$ be a slice submanifold of $M$ in the sense of V2.1, let $M'$ be a $C^\infty$ manifold, and let $F : M' \to M$ be smooth with $F(x) \in S$ for every $x$. Then $F$ is smooth as a map $M' \to S$. Read in the chart of $S$ at $F(x)$ induced by a slice chart $\varphi$, the map is $\mathrm{pr}_Z \circ \varphi \circ F$, a composite of smooth maps.

What differs

  • Smoothness of a map into $S \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ means smoothness into $\mathbb R^k$ for Milnor, so corestriction is invisible; for an abstract $S$ with a constructed atlas it has to be proved.
  • The formal statement requires the values to lie in $S$ at every point of $M'$, and is used in V2.10 to turn the retraction of the ball into a map into the sphere.
V2.9

Lemma 6: the retraction formula, verified

stronger
no finite dimension assumedstated on the open set $\Omega$$g$ need not map the disk to itself

Milnor §2, Lemma 6 · p. 14

In the proof of Lemma 6 Milnor writes the retraction explicitly: for a smooth $g : \mathbb D^n \to \mathbb D^n$ without fixed points, $f(x) = x + tu$ with $u = (x - g(x))/\|x - g(x)\|$ and $t = -x \cdot u + \sqrt{1 - x \cdot x + (x \cdot u)^2}$, the expression under the square root being strictly positive, and asserts that $f$ is a smooth map $\mathbb D^n \to S^{n-1}$ with $f(x) = x$ for $x \in S^{n-1}$.

He calls this an explicit computation and leaves the verification to the reader.

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ be a real inner product space and $g : E \to E$ any map; write $u$, $\Delta = 1 - \|x\|^2 + \langle x, u\rangle^2$, $t = -\langle x, u\rangle + \sqrt{\Delta}$ and $f(x) = x + tu$ as above, and let $\Omega = \{x : x \ne g(x),\ \Delta(x) > 0\}$. Then $\|f(x)\| = 1$ for every $x \in \Omega$; if $x \in \Omega$ with $\|x\| = 1$ and $\|g(x)\| \le 1$ then $f(x) = x$; $f$ is smooth on $\Omega$ whenever $g$ is smooth, and $\Omega$ is open whenever $g$ is continuous. Finally, if $g$ carries the closed unit ball into itself and has no fixed point there, the whole ball lies in $\Omega$.

Milnor/Chapter2/Retraction.lean, line 50  ·  Milnor.Retraction.norm_retr

theorem norm_retr {x : E} (hx : x ∈ good P) : ‖retr P x‖ = 1 := …

Milnor/Chapter2/Retraction.lean, line 67  ·  Milnor.Retraction.retr_eq_self

theorem retr_eq_self {x : E} (hx : x ∈ good P) (hx1 : ‖x‖ = 1) (hP : ‖P x‖ ≤ 1) :
    retr P x = x := …

Milnor/Chapter2/Retraction.lean, line 131  ·  Milnor.Retraction.contDiffOn_retr

What differs

  • The formal statements hold in any real inner product space, with no finite-dimensionality, for an arbitrary $g : E \to E$, and on the whole open set $\Omega$ where the formulas make sense.
  • Positivity of the radicand, which Milnor asserts, is the separate claim that the ball lies in $\Omega$: there $\Delta \le 0$ forces $\|x\| = 1$ and $\langle x, g(x)\rangle = 1$, hence $g(x) = x$.
  • That $f$ fixes the sphere needs only $\|x\| = 1$ and $\|g(x)\| \le 1$, through Cauchy–Schwarz; smoothness of $f$ needs $g$ smooth, and openness of $\Omega$ only $g$ continuous.
V2.10

Lemmas 5 and 6 for the disk: a smooth fixed point

narrower
Lemma 5 only for the disk$g$ smooth on all of $E$cutoff avoids boundary domains

Milnor §2, Lemma 6 · p. 14

Any smooth map $g : \mathbb D^n \to \mathbb D^n$ has a fixed point. It follows from Lemma 5 (p. 13), that a compact manifold with boundary admits no smooth map to its boundary leaving the boundary pointwise fixed: a fixed-point-free $g$ would produce the retraction $f$, contradicting the special case that the identity of $S^{n-1}$ extends to no smooth map $\mathbb D^n \to S^{n-1}$.

Lemma 5 is proved, following Hirsch, by choosing a regular value $y \in \partial X$ of $f$: then $f^{-1}(y)$ is a compact $1$-manifold with the single boundary point $y$, while such a manifold has an even number of boundary points.

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional real inner product space with closed unit ball $\mathbb D$. Every $g : E \to E$ that is smooth on all of $E$ and carries $\mathbb D$ into itself has a fixed point in $\mathbb D$. The proof is Milnor's: the retraction of V2.9, extended over $E$ by a smooth cutoff that is the identity on $\mathbb D$ and takes values in a slightly larger ball still inside $\Omega$, and corestricted to the sphere by V2.8, has a regular value $y$ by Brown's corollary; the transversality condition of V2.6 holds at $y$ because the retraction fixes the sphere; so by V2.6 the level set over $y$ inside $\mathbb D$ is a compact $1$-manifold whose only boundary point is $y$.

Milnor/Chapter2/Brouwer.lean, line 92  ·  Milnor.exists_fixedPoint_of_contDiff

What differs

  • Milnor's Lemma 5 holds for any compact manifold with boundary; only the case of the disk is formalized, inside this proof, and nothing in the book needs more.
  • Milnor's $g$ is smooth on $\mathbb D^n$, which for him means smooth on a neighbourhood; the formal $g$ is assumed smooth on all of $E$, and V2.11 shows that nothing is lost for the book.
  • The retraction is extended over $E$ by a smooth cutoff so that no manifold with boundary is ever a domain, and it is regarded as a map into the sphere through V2.8.
  • $\mathbb R^n$ is replaced by a finite-dimensional real inner product space, and the degenerate case $\dim E = 0$ is disposed of separately, the ball being then a single point.
V2.11

Brouwer's fixed point theorem

restated
$G$ continuous only on $\mathbb D$smooth approximation, not polynomialinner product space, not $\mathbb R^n$

Milnor §2, Brouwer Fixed Point Theorem · p. 14

Any continuous function $G : \mathbb D^n \to \mathbb D^n$ has a fixed point. The theorem is reduced to Lemma 6 by approximation: if $\|G(x) - x\|$ has minimum $\mu > 0$ on $\mathbb D^n$, the Weierstrass theorem supplies a polynomial map $P_1$ with $\|P_1 - G\| < \varepsilon$ there, and $P = P_1/(1 + \varepsilon)$ is a smooth self-map of $\mathbb D^n$ whose fixed point contradicts the minimum once $\varepsilon$ is small.

He points out that this pattern, proving a statement about continuous maps by approximating them with smooth ones, recurs later (§8, Problem 4).

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional real inner product space with closed unit ball $\mathbb D$. Every $G : E \to E$ that is continuous on $\mathbb D$ and carries $\mathbb D$ into itself has a fixed point in $\mathbb D$. The proof is Milnor's, with $\varepsilon = \mu/2$ and the rescaling by $c = 1 + \mu/2$, except that $P_1$ is smooth rather than polynomial: each coordinate $\langle b_i, G\rangle$ in an orthonormal basis is approximated within $\varepsilon/(N+1)$ by Stone–Weierstrass applied to the subalgebra of continuous functions on $\mathbb D$ that extend to smooth functions on $E$.

    ∃ x ∈ closedBall (0 : E) 1, G x = x := …

  • $\mathbb R^n$ is replaced by a finite-dimensional real inner product space, of which $\mathbb R^n$ is the case in the book.
  • The polynomial approximation from the Weierstrass theorem is replaced by an approximation by maps smooth on all of $E$; the final estimate and the rescaling by $1 + \mu/2$ are Milnor's.
V2.12

Lemma 2, second clause: the normal space

hypotheses explicit
inner product on the source's modeltangent space realised inside $E$bundled as a linear isomorphism

Milnor §2, Lemma 2 · p. 11

The second sentence of Lemma 2: for a regular value $y$ of $f : M \to N$ and $M' = f^{-1}(y)$, once the null space of $df_x$ is known to be the tangent space $TM'_x$, it follows that $df_x$ maps the orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ in $TM_x$ isomorphically onto $TN_y$. Milnor states it in one clause and leaves the dimension count to the reader. "Orthogonal complement" needs no comment from him: his manifolds are subsets of $\mathbb R^k$, so every tangent space is a linear subspace of a Euclidean space and comes with its inner product.

The first clause is V2.3, and in the book the two are one lemma with one two-line proof.

Proved in Lean

In the situation of V2.2 and V2.3 — $M$ and $N$ smooth manifolds without boundary over boundaryless models with corners, $f$ of class $C^\infty$, $y$ a regular value, and the model spaces satisfying $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$ — write $T \subseteq E$ for the tangent space of $f^{-1}(y)$ at $p$, realised inside the model space $E \cong T_pM$ as the range of the derivative of the inclusion. Then $df_p$ is injective on the orthogonal complement $T^\perp$; every vector of $TN_y$ is $df_p(v)$ for some $v \in T^\perp$; and the two halves are bundled into a continuous linear isomorphism $T^\perp \to TN_y$ whose underlying map is $df_p$ itself. The model space $E$ of the source carries an inner product, in which $T^\perp$ is taken; the target needs none.

def tangentSubspace (hZ : finrank ℝ Z + finrank ℝ F = finrank ℝ E) (hf : ContMDiff I J ∞ f)
    {y : N} (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) (p : f ⁻¹' {y}) : Submodule ℝ E := …

What differs

  • The one hypothesis Milnor does not state is an inner product on the model space $E$ of the source, in place of the bare norm of V2.2 and V2.3. He has it for nothing, his manifolds being subsets of $\mathbb R^k$; the target $N$ needs none.
  • "The orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ in $TM_x$" becomes the orthogonal complement of the subspace $T \subseteq E$ of V2.3, the range of the derivative of the inclusion read in the model space, because the tangent spaces of $M$ and of $f^{-1}(y)$ are modelled on different spaces.
  • The dimension count is written out: at a regular value $df_p$ is onto, so $T = \ker df_p$ has dimension $\dim E - \dim F$ and $\dim T^\perp = \dim F$; injectivity on $T^\perp$ is the first clause together with $T \cap T^\perp = 0$, and surjectivity follows because an injective map between spaces of equal finite dimension is onto.
  • Injectivity and surjectivity are also available separately and elementwise, which is the form the proofs use; Milnor asserts only the isomorphism.

Chapter 3

Milnor §3, pp. 16–19: the proof of Sard’s theorem

V3.1

Sard's theorem

stronger
abstract spaces, any Haar measuredegenerate dimensions split off

Milnor §3, Theorem of Sard · p. 16

Let $f : U \to \mathbb R^p$ be smooth with $U$ open in $\mathbb R^n$, and let $C$ be the set of $x \in U$ at which the rank of $df_x$ is less than $p$. Then $f(C) \subseteq \mathbb R^p$ has measure zero. Milnor notes that the cases $n \le p$ are comparatively easy but gives one proof covering all of them, by induction on $n$ in three steps, the third by counting cubes.

He restates the theorem here, having announced it in §2 (p. 10); a footnote credits the proof to Pontryagin, simplified by assuming $f$ infinitely differentiable.

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ and $F$ be finite-dimensional real normed spaces, $U \subseteq E$ open, and $f$ smooth on $U$, and let $C$ be the set of points of $U$ at which $df_x$ fails to be surjective. Then $f(C)$ is null for every Haar measure on $F$, taken with its Borel sets. The two degenerate cases the book passes over are split off: if $\dim F = 0$ then $C$ is empty, and if $\dim E = 0$ and $\dim F > 0$ then $f(C)$ has at most one point.

Milnor/Chapter3/Sard.lean, line 112  ·  Milnor.sard

theorem sard (μ : Measure F) [μ.IsAddHaarMeasure] {U : Set E} (hU : IsOpen U) {f : E → F}
    (hf : ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ f U) : μ (f '' Sard.critSet f U) = 0 := …

Milnor/Chapter3/Basic.lean, line 51  ·  Milnor.Sard.critSet

def critSet (f : E → F) (U : Set E) : Set E := …

Milnor/Chapter3/Sard.lean, line 49  ·  Milnor.Sard.sardStatement

theorem sardStatement : ∀ n : ℕ, SardStatement.{u} n := …

What differs

  • Milnor's $\mathbb R^n \to \mathbb R^p$ with Lebesgue measure becomes arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed spaces with an arbitrary Haar measure on the target.
  • The generality is forced by the proof rather than sought: the induction on $\dim E$ passes to hyperplanes of $E$, which are not literally $\mathbb R^{n-1}$.
  • Milnor's condition $\mathrm{rank}\,df_x < p$ appears as non-surjectivity of $df_x$, the same condition; $E$ and $F$ must lie in one universe of the type theory, a formality.
  • The case $\dim F = 0$ must be excluded by hand, since a Haar measure on a zero-dimensional space is a point mass and not the zero measure; $\dim E = 0$ is handled separately.
V3.2

Regular values are dense in Euclidean space

stronger
abstract target spaceno measure in the statement

Milnor §2, p. 10

Immediately after stating Sard's theorem for a smooth $f : U \to \mathbb R^n$ with $U$ open in $\mathbb R^m$, Milnor remarks that a set of measure zero contains no nonempty open set, so the complement $\mathbb R^n \setminus f(C)$ is everywhere dense. A footnote credits the density statement to Arthur B. Brown in 1935, rediscovered by Dubovickiĭ and by Thom.

A remark, not a numbered result; §3 proves only the measure-zero half.

Proved in Lean

In the same setting as the formal Sard theorem — $E$ and $F$ finite-dimensional real normed spaces, $U \subseteq E$ open, $f$ smooth on $U$, and $C$ the set where $df_x$ is not surjective — the complement of $f(C)$ is dense in $F$. No measure appears in the statement: the proof chooses the Haar measure attached to a basis of $F$ and uses that a null set has empty interior, which is Milnor's one-line reason.

Milnor/Chapter3/Sard.lean, line 118  ·  Milnor.dense_compl_image_critSet

theorem dense_compl_image_critSet {U : Set E} (hU : IsOpen U) {f : E → F}
    (hf : ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ f U) : Dense (f '' Sard.critSet f U)ᶜ := …

What differs

  • Milnor's remark is about $\mathbb R^n$; the formal statement is about an arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed space $F$, the Haar measure being chosen inside the proof.
  • The conclusion is purely topological, so the target needs no measure structure in the statement, unlike the Sard theorem it is deduced from.
V3.3

Brown's theorem: regular values are dense

hypotheses explicit
second countability of $M$ assumed

Milnor §2, Corollary (A. B. Brown) · p. 10

For a smooth $f : M \to N$ from an $m$-manifold to an $n$-manifold, Milnor calls $x$ critical when $df_x : TM_x \to TN_{f(x)}$ has rank less than $n$, and calls $N \setminus f(C)$ the set of regular values. Since $M$ is covered by countably many neighbourhoods each diffeomorphic to an open subset of $\mathbb R^m$, he concludes that the set of regular values of $f$ is everywhere dense in $N$.

The countable cover is asserted rather than assumed: for his manifolds, subsets of Euclidean space, it comes for free.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ and $N$ be smooth manifolds without boundary, modelled on finite-dimensional real normed spaces, with $M$ second countable, and let $f : M \to N$ be smooth. Then the set of $y \in N$ all of whose preimages are regular points is dense in $N$. No separation axiom on $M$ or $N$ is needed, and $N$ carries no measure: the proof reads $f$ in countably many charts of $M$ and in one chart of $N$ meeting the given open set, and applies the Euclidean statement there.

Milnor/Chapter2/Brown.lean, line 134  ·  Milnor.dense_regularValues

theorem dense_regularValues [SecondCountableTopology M] (hf : ContMDiff I J ∞ f) :
    Dense (regularValues I J f) := …

What differs

  • Milnor's countable cover by chart neighbourhoods becomes a hypothesis: $M$ is assumed second countable, which is automatic for the subsets of $\mathbb R^k$ he works with.
  • The target carries no measure; the measure lives on the model space of a chart of $N$, so the conclusion is stated purely as density of a subset of $N$.

Chapter 4

Milnor §4, pp. 20–25: smooth homotopy and isotopy, the degree modulo 2

V4.1

Lemma 4 for the cylinder's two ends

narrower
$X$ cut out by $g_0 \ge 0, g_1 \ge 0$two ends, disjoint zero setstransversality replaces $f|\partial X$

Milnor §2, Lemma 4 · p. 13

Take a smooth $f : X \to N$ from an $m$-manifold with boundary to an $n$-manifold, $m > n$, and let $y$ be regular for $f$ and also for what $f$ does on $\partial X$. Then $f^{-1}(y)$, sitting in $X$, is a smooth $(m-n)$-manifold whose boundary is what $\partial X$ cuts out of it, namely $f^{-1}(y) \cap \partial X$. In §4 this is used on the cylinder $X = M \times [0,1]$.

He states it for an abstract manifold with boundary and leaves to a footnote on p. 20 the fact that $M \times [0,1]$ is one, bounded by two copies of $M$.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ and $N$ be smooth manifolds without boundary, $f : M \to N$ smooth, $y$ a regular value, and let $Z$, $Z'$ be finite-dimensional with $\dim Z + \dim N = \dim M$ and $\dim Z' + 1 = \dim Z$. Let $g_0, g_1 : M \to \mathbb R$ be smooth, and suppose that at each point of $f^{-1}(y)$ where $g_i$ vanishes some tangent vector is killed by $df$ but not by $dg_i$, and that $g_0$ and $g_1$ never vanish at the same point of $f^{-1}(y)$. Then, inside the manifold $f^{-1}(y)$, the set $\{g_0 \ge 0,\ g_1 \ge 0\}$ is a manifold with boundary $\{g_0 = 0\} \cup \{g_1 = 0\}$. No compactness is assumed.

Milnor/Chapter4/Cylinder.lean, line 46  ·  Milnor.isHalfSliceSubmanifold_levelSet_two

What differs

  • Milnor's $X$ is any manifold with boundary; the formal one is $\{g_0 \ge 0\} \cap \{g_1 \ge 0\}$ inside a boundaryless $M$, with $g_0, g_1$ never zero together on $f^{-1}(y)$.
  • Regularity of $y$ for $f|\partial X$ becomes a transversality condition: at each zero of $g_i$ on $f^{-1}(y)$ some vector is killed by $df$ but not by $dg_i$.
  • The dimension hypotheses $\dim Z + \dim N = \dim M$ and $\dim Z' + 1 = \dim Z$ carry Milnor's $m > n$; the cylinder is the case $g_0 = t$, $g_1 = 1 - t$ on $M \times \mathbb R$.
  • Because being a manifold with boundary is local, the two ends are handled one at a time and glued, which is why disjointness of the zero sets is enough.
V4.2

Regularity at the ends of the cylinder

not in the book
step of a proof, not a statementneeds $\dim M = \dim N$hypothesis on $F$ goes unused

Milnor §4, p. 21

The book has no statement here. In the proof of the Homotopy Lemma Milnor asserts at once that $F^{-1}(y)$ is a compact $1$-manifold whose boundary is $f^{-1}(y) \times \{0\} \cup g^{-1}(y) \times \{1\}$. The hypothesis of Lemma 4 that $y$ be regular for $F$ restricted to $\partial(M \times [0,1])$, which is where regularity for the two end maps is used, is never checked.

It is a step inside a proof rather than a statement, and the book does not isolate it.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ and $N$ be manifolds without boundary of the same dimension, $M$ modelled on $E$, and let $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$ be smooth. If $y$ is a regular value of $F$ and of the end map $F(\cdot, c)$, then at each $(x, c)$ with $F(x, c) = y$ there is a vector $v = (v_1, v_2) \in E \times \mathbb R$ with $dF_{(x,c)}v = 0$ and $v_2 \ne 0$. This is precisely the transversality hypothesis of V4.1 for $g_0 = t$ at $c = 0$ and for $g_1 = 1 - t$ at $c = 1$.

  • Nothing in the book corresponds to this: it is the unstated verification that Lemma 4 may be applied to the cylinder, isolated here as a lemma.
  • Equality of dimensions is what makes it work: the kernel of $dF_{(x,c)}$ is nontrivial by counting, and meets $T_xM \times 0$ only in $0$ because $d(F(\cdot,c))_x$ is injective.
  • The formal statement assumes $y$ regular for $F$, but the proof appeals only to regularity of the end map $F(\cdot, c)$; that hypothesis is never used.
V4.3

The Homotopy Lemma

restated
homotopy on $M \times \mathbb R$Hausdorff and second countable on $M$even sum, not a congruence

Milnor §4, Homotopy Lemma · p. 21

Take two maps $f, g : M \to N$ that are smoothly homotopic, the two manifolds having equal dimension and $M$ being compact with empty boundary. Then at any $y \in N$ that is regular for each of them, the counts $\#f^{-1}(y)$ and $\#g^{-1}(y)$ have the same parity.

A smooth homotopy for him is a smooth map on $M \times [0,1]$, a manifold with boundary two copies of $M$ by the footnote on p. 20.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a compact, Hausdorff, second countable smooth manifold without boundary and $N$ a Hausdorff smooth manifold without boundary of the same dimension. Let $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$ be smooth, $f = F(\cdot, 0)$, $g = F(\cdot, 1)$. If $y$ is a regular value of $f$ and of $g$, then $\#f^{-1}(y) + \#g^{-1}(y)$ is even.

Milnor/Chapter4/Homotopy.lean, line 199  ·  Milnor.homotopy_lemma

    (hy1 : IsRegularValue I J (fun x => F' (x, 1)) y) :
    Even (((fun x => F' (x, 0)) ⁻¹' {y}).ncard + ((fun x => F' (x, 1)) ⁻¹' {y}).ncard) := …

What differs

  • A smooth homotopy is taken to be a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$, not on $M \times [0,1]$, so its domain has no boundary and Lemma 4 and Brown's theorem apply to it directly.
  • Milnor's homotopies extend to this form after reparametrizing $t$, and the level set is cut down to $M \times [0,1]$ by hand, so the two notions of smoothly homotopic agree.
  • $M$ is assumed Hausdorff and second countable and $N$ Hausdorff; second countability is used only for Brown's theorem applied to $F$ on the noncompact $M \times \mathbb R$.
  • The conclusion is the evenness of $\#f^{-1}(y) + \#g^{-1}(y)$ rather than a congruence; both fibres are finite by compactness and regularity.
V4.4

The Homogeneity Lemma

narrower
$N$ boundaryless, all points interiorisotopy over $\mathbb R$, not $[0,1]$Hausdorff on $N$

Milnor §4, Homogeneity Lemma · p. 22

Given any two points $y$, $z$ in the interior of a smooth connected manifold $N$, some diffeomorphism $h$ of $N$ takes $y$ to $z$ and is smoothly isotopic to the identity. Smoothly isotopic in the sense defined on p. 21: joined to the identity by a smooth homotopy on $N \times [0,1]$ every stage of which is again a diffeomorphism.

He proves it by the time-one flow of a compactly supported vector field on a chart ball, remarking that for $N = \mathbb S^n$ a rotation suffices.

Proved in Lean

Let $N$ be a Hausdorff, preconnected smooth manifold without boundary. For any $y, z \in N$ there are a diffeomorphism $h$ of $N$ with $h(y) = z$ and a smooth map $H : N \times \mathbb R \to N$ with $H(\cdot, 0) = \mathrm{id}$, $H(\cdot, 1) = h$, and every stage $H(\cdot, t)$ a diffeomorphism of $N$.

Milnor/Chapter4/Homogeneity.lean, line 261  ·  Milnor.isotopic_of_preconnected

theorem isotopic_of_preconnected [T2Space N] [PreconnectedSpace N] (y z : N) :
    Isotopic J y z := …

Milnor/Chapter4/Homogeneity.lean, line 142  ·  Milnor.Isotopic

def Isotopic (J : ModelWithCorners ℝ F H) (y z : N) : Prop := …

Milnor/Chapter4/BumpDiffeo.lean, line 23  ·  Milnor.Bump.shift

def shift (φ : F → ℝ) (c : F) (u : F) : F := u + φ u • c

What differs

  • Milnor allows $N$ to have a boundary and takes $y, z$ in its interior; the formal $N$ is boundaryless, so the restriction to interior points does not arise.
  • The isotopy is a smooth map on $N \times \mathbb R$ rather than $N \times [0,1]$; that every stage is a diffeomorphism is part of the formal conclusion, as in Milnor's definition.
  • Hausdorff is assumed on $N$, used once to see that the chart image of a closed ball is closed. Preconnected also allows $N$ empty, which the given $y, z$ exclude.
  • In the proof the flow of the field $u \mapsto \varphi(u)c$ is replaced by a single Euler step, the explicit map $u \mapsto u + \varphi(u)c$ of the model space, which changes no hypothesis.
V4.5

Diffeomorphisms preserve regular values

not in the book
unstated step of the Theorem's proofno compactness or connectedness

Milnor §4, p. 24

The book has no statement here. In the proof of the Theorem Milnor writes down, without argument, that $h \circ f$ has $z$ as a regular value and that $(h \circ f)^{-1}(z) = f^{-1}(y)$, where $h$ is the diffeomorphism supplied by the Homogeneity Lemma with $h(y) = z$.

Both facts are used in a single line of the proof, and the second is presented as a computation with $h^{-1}$.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$, $N$ be manifolds without boundary, $f : M \to N$ smooth, $h$ a diffeomorphism of $N$, and $y$ a regular value of $f$. Then $h(y)$ is a regular value of $h \circ f$, and $(h \circ f)^{-1}(h(y)) = f^{-1}(y)$. The derivative of a diffeomorphism is surjective: the chain rule on $h^{-1} \circ h = \mathrm{id}$ makes it injective, and it is an endomorphism of the model space.

V4.6

Theorem of §4: independence of the value

hypotheses explicit
Hausdorff and second countable on $M$Hausdorff on $N$even sum, not a congruence

Milnor §4, Theorem · p. 24

Under the standing hypotheses of the section — $M$ compact and boundaryless, $N$ connected, the two of equal dimension, $f : M \to N$ smooth — the number of preimages has the same parity at any two regular values $y$, $z$: $\#f^{-1}(y) \equiv \#f^{-1}(z) \pmod 2$.

The Theorem carries no letter and no number; its standing hypotheses on $M$ and $N$ are set out in the sentence just before it.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a compact, Hausdorff, second countable smooth manifold without boundary, $N$ a Hausdorff, preconnected smooth manifold without boundary of the same dimension, and $f : M \to N$ smooth. If $y$ and $z$ are regular values of $f$, then $\#f^{-1}(y) + \#f^{-1}(z)$ is even.

Milnor/Chapter4/Degree.lean, line 80  ·  Milnor.even_ncard_add_ncard_of_isRegularValue

What differs

  • $M$ is assumed Hausdorff and second countable and $N$ Hausdorff; for Milnor's submanifolds of $\mathbb R^k$ these hold automatically and are never mentioned.
  • Connectedness of $N$ is used in the weaker form of preconnectedness, which also allows $N$ empty, and the conclusion is stated as the evenness of a sum rather than a congruence.
  • Compactness of $N$ is not assumed, matching Milnor's parenthetical on p. 20 that one may as well assume it but need not.
V4.7

The degree modulo 2

restated
chosen regular value, by choice$0$ when no regular value existsdefined for any $f$, smooth or not

Milnor §4, p. 24

Having shown that the residue class is the same at every regular value, Milnor calls it $\deg_2(f)$; the name is announced already on p. 20. He does not say which regular value computes it, because by the Theorem the choice is immaterial and Sard's theorem supplies one.

The case of a map with no regular value never arises for him, since his $N$ is nonempty.

Proved in Lean

$\deg_2 f \in \mathbb Z/2$ is defined for an arbitrary map $f : M \to N$ as the parity of $\#f^{-1}(y_0)$ for a regular value $y_0$ picked by the axiom of choice, and as $0$ when $f$ has no regular value at all. Under the hypotheses of V4.6 the choice is immaterial: for every regular value $y$ of $f$, $\deg_2 f = \#f^{-1}(y) \bmod 2$.

Milnor/Chapter4/Degree.lean, line 109  ·  Milnor.degMod2

def degMod2 (I : ModelWithCorners ℝ E H) (J : ModelWithCorners ℝ F G) (f : M → N) : ZMod 2 := …

Milnor/Chapter4/Degree.lean, line 113  ·  Milnor.degMod2_eq

theorem degMod2_eq [CompactSpace M] [T2Space M] [SecondCountableTopology M] [T2Space N]
    [PreconnectedSpace N] (hdim : finrank ℝ E = finrank ℝ F) {f : M → N}
    (hf : ContMDiff I J ∞ f) {y : N} (hy : IsRegularValue I J f y) :
    degMod2 I J f = ((f ⁻¹' {y}).ncard : ZMod 2) := …

What differs

  • Milnor names a residue class already known to be well defined; the formal definition must produce a value first, so it chooses a regular value and proves independence afterwards.
  • The definition applies to any map whatever and returns $0$ when there is no regular value; for smooth $f$ on a second countable $M$ that happens only when $N$ is empty.
  • The count $\#f^{-1}(y)$ is the formal cardinality, which is $0$ on an infinite set; at a regular value the fibre is finite, so this never bites.
V4.8

Theorem of §4: homotopy invariance

restated
homotopy on $M \times \mathbb R$Hausdorff and second countable on $M$empty $N$ handled separately

Milnor §4, Theorem · p. 24

The second assertion of the same unlettered Theorem: the common residue class $\deg_2(f)$ is unchanged when $f$ is replaced by a smoothly homotopic map. Milnor proves it by picking, with Sard's theorem, a value regular for both maps at once and chaining the congruences.

He draws from it on p. 24 that the identity of a compact boundaryless manifold is not smoothly homotopic to a constant.

Proved in Lean

For $M$ and $N$ as in V4.6 and any smooth $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$, $\deg_2 F(\cdot, 0) = \deg_2 F(\cdot, 1)$. The proof takes a value regular for both ends: the regular values of the first form an open set, and those of the second are dense by Brown's theorem, so the two meet. If $N$ is empty, neither end has a regular value and both degrees are $0$.

    degMod2 I J (fun x => F' (x, 0)) = degMod2 I J (fun x => F' (x, 1)) := …

What differs

  • The homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$ rather than $M \times [0,1]$, and the two maps compared are named as its ends $F(\cdot, 0)$ and $F(\cdot, 1)$.
  • $M$ is assumed Hausdorff and second countable and $N$ Hausdorff and preconnected; Milnor's manifolds have these properties by sitting in $\mathbb R^k$.
  • An empty $N$ is a separate case, forced by the convention of V4.7 that a map with no regular value has degree $0$.
V4.9

Nonzero degree forces surjectivity

not in the book
not stated in the bookvacuous regularity off the image

Milnor §4, p. 20

The book states no such corollary. The nearest thing is the parenthetical aside made while the section is being set up, that nothing is lost by taking $N$ compact and boundaryless, because a degree into any other target is forced to vanish. That aside is this fact applied to a point missed by the compact image of $M$.

The examples he does draw on p. 24 concern the degree of a constant and of the identity, not surjectivity.

Proved in Lean

For $M$, $N$ and $f$ as in V4.6, if $\deg_2 f \ne 0$ then $f$ is surjective. A point outside the image is vacuously a regular value with empty fibre, so it computes $\deg_2 f = 0$.

Chapter 5

Milnor §5, pp. 26–31: oriented manifolds and the Brouwer degree

V5.0

Orientations and the sign of a derivative

restated
orientation as a ray of top formscompatibility read in the chartssign defined for every linear map

Milnor §5, Definitions, pp. 26–27

An orientation of a finite-dimensional real vector space is an equivalence class of ordered bases, two of them equivalent when the change-of-basis determinant is positive; for the zero space an orientation is the symbol $+1$ or $-1$. An oriented manifold carries an orientation of each $T_xM$, and for $m \ge 1$ these must fit together: around every point there is a diffeomorphism onto an open subset of $\mathbb R^m$ or $\mathbb H^m$ whose derivative carries each chosen orientation to the standard orientation of $\mathbb R^m$. For $f : M \to N$ between oriented $n$-manifolds and $x$ a regular point, $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ is $+1$ or $-1$ according as $df_x$ preserves or reverses orientation.

He admits boundary from the start, so a chart may land in the half space $\mathbb H^m$, and he fixes the standard orientation of $\mathbb R^n$ to have something to compare with.

Proved in Lean

An orientation of a finite-dimensional $E$ is a ray of nonzero alternating $n$-forms, the class of the determinant form of a positive basis. For a linear $T : E \to F$ and orientations $a$ of $E$, $b$ of $F$, the sign is the sign of the constant $c$ in $\eta \circ T = c\,\omega$ for representatives $\omega$ of $a$ and $\eta$ of $b$; it is $0$ exactly when $T$ is singular, it is multiplicative under composition and equals $1$ for the identity. An orientation of a manifold $M$ modelled on $E$ is a choice of orientation $o_x$ of $E = T_xM$ at each $x$ such that, for every $x$, the derivative at all nearby $y$ of the extended chart at $x$ carries $o_y$ to $o_x$. The sign of $f$ at $x$ is the sign of $df_x$ for $o_x$ and $o_{f(x)}$, and $M \times \mathbb R$ is oriented by declaring a positive basis of $E$ followed by $(0,1)$ positive.

Milnor/Chapter5/Orient.lean, line 48  ·  Milnor.Orient

structure Orient (I : ModelWithCorners ℝ E H) (M : Type*) [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace H M]
    (n : ℕ) where

Milnor/Chapter5/Orient.lean, line 55  ·  Milnor.signAt

Milnor/Chapter5/OrSign.lean, line 133  ·  Milnor.orSign

def orSign (a : Orientation ℝ E (Fin n)) (b : Orientation ℝ F (Fin n)) (T : E →L[ℝ] F) : ℤ := …

Milnor/Chapter5/Orient.lean, line 207  ·  Milnor.Orient.prodReal

def Orient.prodReal [IsManifold I ∞ M] [I.Boundaryless] (hE : finrank ℝ E = n)
    (oM : Orient I M n) : Orient (I.prod 𝓘(ℝ, ℝ)) (M × ℝ) (n + 1) where

What differs

  • Milnor's orientation of a space is a class of ordered bases; the formal one is a ray of nonzero alternating $n$-forms, the class of the determinant form of a positive basis.
  • Milnor asks for charts onto $\mathbb R^m$ or $\mathbb H^m$ carrying the chosen orientations to the standard one; the formal condition compares $o_y$ with $o_x$ through the chart at $x$ alone.
  • Milnor defines the sign only at a regular point, where it is $\pm 1$; the formal sign is defined for every linear map and is $0$ exactly when the map is singular.
  • The dimension $n$ is a parameter of the formal definition, so each theorem carries $\dim E = n$ as a hypothesis; there is no oriented atlas and no standard orientation of a model space.
V5.1

Local constancy of the sign of the derivative

not in the book
stated only in the formalizationvariant for f smooth on an open set

Milnor §5, p. 27

The book states nothing here. On p. 27 Milnor asserts that $\deg(f;y)$ is a locally constant function of $y$, and in that assertion the local constancy of $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ in $x$ near a regular point is used without comment.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ and $N$ be smooth manifolds without boundary modelled on $E$ and $F$ with $\dim E = \dim F = n$, both oriented, let $f : M \to N$ be smooth and $x_0$ a regular point. Then $\operatorname{sign} df_x = \operatorname{sign} df_{x_0}$ for all $x$ in a neighbourhood of $x_0$. A variant assumes $f$ smooth only on an open set containing $x_0$.

V5.2

The signed count deg(f; y) and its local constancy

hypotheses explicit
M and N Hausdorffcount defined at every ydensity of regular values not included

Milnor §5, p. 27

For $M$ compact and $N$ connected, oriented $n$-manifolds without boundary, and $y$ a regular value of a smooth $f : M \to N$, the degree at $y$ is $\deg(f;y) = \sum_{x \in f^{-1}(y)} \operatorname{sign} df_x$. As in §1, this integer is a locally constant function of $y$, and it is defined on a dense open subset of $N$.

He asserts the local constancy in a single sentence, with a reference back to §1 and no proof.

Proved in Lean

$\deg(f;y)$ is defined at every $y$ as the finite sum of $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ over $f^{-1}(y)$, with the convention that a sum having infinitely many nonzero terms is $0$. If $M$ is compact and Hausdorff, $N$ Hausdorff, $\dim E = \dim F = n$, both oriented and without boundary, $f$ smooth and $y$ a regular value, then $\deg(f;y') = \deg(f;y)$ for every $y'$ near $y$, regular or not. Outside the range of $f$ the count is $0$.

Milnor/Chapter5/DegreeAt.lean, line 25  ·  Milnor.degAt

  • The formal statement names $M$ and $N$ Hausdorff, automatic for Milnor's submanifolds of $\mathbb R^k$; compactness of $M$ he assumes himself, and connectedness of $N$ is not needed here.
  • The formal count is defined at every $y$, not only at a regular value, and the local constancy is asserted for all nearby $y'$ rather than for nearby regular values.
  • Milnor's second claim, that the degree is defined on a dense open subset of $N$, is Sard's theorem and is not part of this item.
V5.3

Lemma 1 of §5, in ambient form

restated
boundary presented as {g ≥ 0}signs of the ambient pair map (f, g)y regular for f on all of M′

Milnor §5, Lemma 1 · p. 28

Let $X$ be a compact oriented manifold with boundary, $M = \partial X$ oriented as the boundary of $X$, and $N$ an oriented $n$-manifold. If $f : M \to N$ extends to a smooth $F : X \to N$, then $\deg(f;y) = 0$ for every regular value $y$ of $f$.

He proves it first for a $y$ regular for $F$ as well, then reaches a general regular value of $f$ by the local constancy of the count; the arc $A \subseteq F^{-1}(y)$ is oriented by a rule on positive bases of $TX_x$, after which the two end signs "follow immediately".

Proved in Lean

Let $M'$ be a Hausdorff oriented $(n{+}1)$-manifold without boundary, $N$ a Hausdorff oriented $n$-manifold without boundary, $f : M' \to N$ smooth with $y$ a regular value, and $g : M' \to \mathbb R$ smooth, so that $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ and $\partial X = \{g = 0\}$. Assume $f^{-1}(y) \cap X$ compact and that at each $x \in f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}$ some $v$ has $df_x v = 0$ and $dg_x v \ne 0$. Then $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}$ is finite and $$\sum_{x \in f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}} \operatorname{sign} d(f,g)_x = 0,$$ the signs taken for the orientation of $M'$ and the product orientation of $N \times \mathbb R$.

What differs

  • Milnor's $X$ is an abstract compact oriented manifold with boundary; the formal $X$ is the region $\{g \ge 0\}$ inside a boundaryless $M'$, and only $f^{-1}(y) \cap X$ is assumed compact.
  • The boundary sign is that of the ambient pair map $(f,g)$; it differs from the sign for Milnor's boundary orientation by $(-1)^{n+1}$, which leaves the vanishing intact.
  • The formal $y$ must be a regular value of $f$ on all of $M'$, so this is Milnor's first case; his passage to a $y$ that is critical for $F$ is carried out inside V5.4 instead.
  • $0$ need not be a regular value of $g$: only the transversality at the points of $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}$ is required, which is Milnor's "$y$ is a regular value of $f|\partial X$".
V5.4

Lemma 2 of §5: homotopy at a common regular value

hypotheses explicit
homotopy defined on all of M × ℝM second countable, N HausdorffN need not be connected

Milnor §5, Lemma 2 · p. 29

Let $F : [0,1] \times M \to N$ be a smooth homotopy between $f = F(0,\cdot)$ and $g = F(1,\cdot)$, with $M$ compact and $M$, $N$ oriented $n$-manifolds without boundary. Then $\deg(g;y) = \deg(f;y)$ for any common regular value $y$.

He deduces it from Lemma 1 for the oriented product $[0,1] \times M$, whose boundary is $1 \times M$ with the right orientation and $0 \times M$ with the wrong one, so that the boundary degree is the difference of the two counts.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be compact, Hausdorff and second countable, $N$ Hausdorff, both oriented and without boundary with $\dim E = \dim F = n$ and $E$, $F$ in one universe. Let $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$ be smooth, $f = F(\cdot,0)$, $g = F(\cdot,1)$, and let $y$ be a regular value of both. Then $\deg(f;y) = \deg(g;y)$.

What differs

  • The formal homotopy is smooth on $M \times \mathbb R$, not on $[0,1] \times M$; under Milnor's convention his $F$ already extends smoothly to a neighbourhood of the cylinder.
  • Second countability of $M$ is named because the reduction from a common regular value of $f$ and $g$ to a regular value of the homotopy goes through Sard and Brown.
  • Hausdorffness of $M$ and $N$ is named; connectedness of $N$, which Milnor assumes when he introduces the degree, is nowhere used at a fixed regular value.
  • The cylinder is realized as $\{t(1-t) \ge 0\}$ in $M \times \mathbb R$, the sign of $(F, t(1-t))$ being $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ at $t = 0$ and $-\operatorname{sign} dg_x$ at $t = 1$.
V5.5

An isotopy of the identity preserves orientation

restated
asserted without proof in the bookisotopy data repeated in the conclusionN Hausdorff assumed but unused

Milnor §5, p. 29

In the proof of Theorems A and B he chooses a diffeomorphism $h : N \to N$ carrying the regular value $y$ to the regular value $z$ and isotopic to the identity, and says that $h$ will then preserve orientation.

The claim occupies half a sentence and is given no argument; it rests on his notion of isotopy as a homotopy through diffeomorphisms.

Proved in Lean

Let $N$ be a Hausdorff smooth manifold without boundary modelled on $F$ with $\dim F = n$ and oriented, and suppose $y$ and $z$ are isotopic, that is, there are a diffeomorphism $h$ of $N$ and a smooth $H : N \times \mathbb R \to N$ with $H(\cdot,0)$ the identity, $H(\cdot,1) = h$, $h(y) = z$ and every stage $H(\cdot,t)$ a diffeomorphism. Then such $h$ and $H$ can be chosen with the further property $\operatorname{sign} dh_x = 1$ at every $x$.

What differs

  • Milnor's sentence is an aside inside a proof; here it is a theorem, proved by the local constancy in $t$ of the sign of $(x,t) \mapsto (H(x,t),t)$ and its value $1$ at $t = 0$.
  • The formal statement hands back the whole isotopy together with the sign, because the proof of Theorem A reuses $H$ as the homotopy from $f$ to $h \circ f$.
  • That every stage is a diffeomorphism, implicit in Milnor's word "isotopic", is carried explicitly and is what makes the sign nonzero for all $t$.
  • The formal statement assumes $N$ Hausdorff, a hypothesis the proof never uses.
V5.6

Theorem A: independence of the regular value

hypotheses explicit
M Hausdorff and second countableN preconnected, so possibly empty

Milnor §5, Theorem A · p. 27

For $M$ compact and $N$ connected, oriented $n$-manifolds without boundary, and $f : M \to N$ smooth, the integer $\deg(f;y)$ does not depend on the choice of regular value $y$.

He proves it as in §4: move $y$ to $z$ by a diffeomorphism isotopic to the identity, note $\deg(f;y) = \deg(h \circ f; h(y))$ "by inspection", then apply Lemma 2.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be compact, Hausdorff and second countable, $N$ Hausdorff and preconnected, both oriented and without boundary with $\dim E = \dim F = n$ and $E$, $F$ in one universe, and let $f : M \to N$ be smooth. Then $\deg(f;y) = \deg(f;z)$ for any two regular values $y$ and $z$.

  • Connectedness of $N$ is weakened to preconnectedness, so an empty $N$ is allowed and the statement is then vacuous.

V5.7

The Brouwer degree and Theorem B

hypotheses explicit
degree total, 0 when N is emptyregular value picked by choicesurjectivity added, not in the book

Milnor §5, Theorem B · p. 27

The common value of $\deg(f;y)$ over the regular values is the degree of $f$, written $\deg f$. Theorem B: if $f$ is smoothly homotopic to $g$, then $\deg f = \deg g$.

He illustrates with $z \mapsto z^k$ on the circle, with degree $0$ for a constant map and $\pm 1$ for a diffeomorphism, and concludes that an orientation reversing diffeomorphism is not homotopic to the identity.

Proved in Lean

$\deg f$ is defined for every smooth $f$ as $\deg(f;y)$ at a regular value $y$ chosen by the axiom of choice, and as $0$ when no regular value exists. For $M$ compact, Hausdorff and second countable, $N$ Hausdorff and preconnected, both oriented and without boundary of dimension $n$, it equals $\deg(f;y)$ at every regular value. If $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$ is smooth then $F(\cdot,0)$ and $F(\cdot,1)$ have the same degree, with no common regular value assumed; a map of nonzero degree is surjective, and the identity has degree $1$.

Milnor/Chapter5/Degree.lean, line 160  ·  Milnor.deg_eq_of_homotopy

    (oN : Orient J N n) {F' : M × ℝ → N} (hF' : ContMDiff (I.prod 𝓘(ℝ, ℝ)) J ∞ F') :
    deg oM oN (fun x => F' (x, 0)) = deg oM oN (fun x => F' (x, 1)) := …

What differs

  • The formal $\deg f$ is a total function, defined with no hypotheses by choosing a regular value; the theorems then add compactness, Hausdorffness, second countability and preconnectedness.
  • Its value is $0$ when $f$ has no regular value, which under those hypotheses happens only for $N$ empty, the one case Milnor's connectedness excludes.
  • Homotopy invariance needs no common regular value in the formal statement: one is produced by Sard applied to the two ends of the homotopy.
  • That a map of nonzero degree is surjective has no counterpart in §5; Milnor there only remarks that a constant map has degree $0$.
V5.8

The boundary orientation of a level set

narrower
only level sets in a vector spaceoutward vector given by a formulacontinuity proved, not assumed

Milnor §5, p. 26

An orientation of $X$ induces one on $\partial X$: at $x \in \partial X$ take a positively oriented basis $(v_1,\dots,v_m)$ of $TX_x$ with $v_2,\dots,v_m$ tangent to the boundary and $v_1$ an outward vector; then $(v_2,\dots,v_m)$ is a positive basis of $T(\partial X)_x$. For $m = 1$ a boundary point gets $-1$ or $+1$ according as a positive vector there points inward or outward.

He leaves unmentioned that the rule is independent of the chosen basis and varies continuously, and gives $\mathbb S^{m-1}$ oriented as $\partial \mathbb D^m$ as his example.

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ be an oriented real space of dimension $n+1$, $g : E \to \mathbb R$ smooth with $0$ a regular value, and $S = g^{-1}(0)$, a submanifold modelled on an $n$-dimensional space $Z$. Put $\nu(q) = -\sum_i dg_q(e_i)\,e_i$ for a reference basis $e$ of $E$, a vector with $dg_q(\nu(q)) < 0$. A basis $z$ of $T_qS$ is declared positive when $(\nu(q), d\iota_q z)$ is a positive basis of $E$; this is proved to be an orientation of $S$ in the sense of V5.0, and it does not depend on the form chosen to represent the orientation of $E$.

Milnor/Chapter5/LevelOrient.lean, line 342  ·  Milnor.levelOrient

def levelOrient :
    letI hS := isSliceSubmanifold_preimage (Z := Z) (finrank_Z_add hE hZ) hg h0

Milnor/Chapter5/LevelOrient.lean, line 155  ·  Milnor.levelOrient_cont

theorem levelOrient_cont :
    letI hS := isSliceSubmanifold_preimage (Z := Z) (finrank_Z_add hE hZ) hg h0

Milnor/Chapter5/LevelOrient.lean, line 71  ·  Milnor.contractOr
