What differs

  • Milnor orients $\partial X$ for any oriented manifold with boundary; the formal construction covers only $S = g^{-1}(0)$ inside an oriented vector space, that is, the boundary of $\{g \ge 0\}$.
  • Milnor's $v_1$ is any outward vector; the formal one is the fixed $\nu(q) = -\sum_i dg_q(e_i)e_i$, and separate lemmas show that any $w$ with $dg_q(w) < 0$ gives the same orientation.
  • Milnor takes for granted that his rule defines an orientation; the formal item includes the proof that it varies continuously in the sense of V5.0.
  • The regions of $\mathbb R^m$ and the spheres that the book actually orients this way are all level sets in a vector space, so the restriction costs nothing in §§5–8.
V5.9

The degree of the antipodal map

stronger
sign computed at every pointsphere oriented from an arbitrary basisoutward vector points into the ball

Milnor §5, p. 30

The antipodal map of $\mathbb S^n$ has degree $(-1)^{n+1}$, since it is the composition of the $n+1$ coordinate reflections $r_i$, each of degree $-1$. Hence for $n$ even the antipodal map is not smoothly homotopic to the identity, a fact the degree modulo $2$ does not see.

The sphere carries the orientation fixed on the preceding page, as the boundary of the disk $\mathbb D^{n+1}$.

Proved in Lean

Give $\mathbb S^n \subseteq \mathbb R^{n+1}$ the submanifold structure of the level set $\langle x,x \rangle - 1 = 0$ and the level-set orientation of V5.8 for the orientation of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ coming from a reference basis. Then $\operatorname{sign} d\alpha_q = (-1)^{n+1}$ at every $q$ and for every $n \ge 0$, where $\alpha(x) = -x$; and for $n \ge 1$, where $\mathbb S^n$ is connected, $\deg \alpha = (-1)^{n+1}$.

Milnor/Chapter5/HairyBall.lean, line 265  ·  Milnor.Sphere.deg_antip

theorem deg_antip (hn : 0 < n) : deg (sphOr n) (sphOr n) (antip (n := n)) = (-1) ^ (n + 1) := …

Milnor/Chapter5/HairyBall.lean, line 163  ·  Milnor.Sphere.signAt_antip

theorem signAt_antip (q : Sph n) : signAt (sphOr n) (sphOr n) antip q = (-1) ^ (n + 1) := …

Milnor/Chapter5/HairyBall.lean, line 114  ·  Milnor.Sphere.sphOr

def sphOr (n : ℕ) : Orient 𝓘(ℝ, EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)) (Sph n) n := …

What differs

  • The formal statement first gives the sign of $d\alpha$ at every point and for every $n \ge 0$; the degree, for $n \ge 1$, then follows because $\alpha^{-1}(y) = \{-y\}$.
  • Milnor factors $\alpha$ into $n+1$ reflections; the formal proof reads the sign off the contracted form, using $\nu(-q) = -\nu(q)$ and $d\iota_{-q} \circ d\alpha_q = -d\iota_q$.
  • The orientation is that of V5.8 for a basis of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ chosen by the axiom of choice, and $\{\langle x,x \rangle \ge 1\}$ is the exterior of the ball, so its "outward" vector points inward.
  • It is therefore Milnor's orientation of $\mathbb S^n = \partial \mathbb D^{n+1}$ or its opposite, and the text does not decide which; the degree is unaffected, being taken between two copies of it.
V5.10

The hairy ball theorem and its converse

narrower
n > 0 assumed in the even halffield splits the coordinates in halves

Milnor §5, p. 30

Following Brouwer, $\mathbb S^n$ admits a smooth field of nonzero tangent vectors if and only if $n$ is odd; a tangent field on $\mathbb S^n$ is a smooth $v : \mathbb S^n \to \mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $v(x) \cdot x = 0$. For $n$ even a nowhere-zero $v$, normalized to unit length, would give the homotopy $F(x,\theta) = x \cos\theta + v(x) \sin\theta$ from the identity to the antipodal map, which the degree forbids; for $n = 2k-1$ an explicit field pairing the coordinates in twos has no zero.

He notes in passing that for $n$ odd the antipodal map is therefore homotopic to the identity, and points to Hopf's theorem, proved in §7.

Proved in Lean

For $n$ even and positive, every smooth $v : \mathbb S^n \to \mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $\langle q, v(q) \rangle = 0$ for all $q$ vanishes somewhere: otherwise $(q,t) \mapsto \cos(\pi t)\,q + \sin(\pi t)\,v(q)/|v(q)|$ is a smooth homotopy in $\mathbb S^n$ from the identity to the antipodal map, whose degrees $1$ and $-1$ would have to agree. Conversely, for $n$ odd, writing $n+1 = 2(m+1)$ and $x = (a,b)$ with $a$ and $b$ the two halves of the coordinates, the field $(a,b) \mapsto (b,-a)$ is smooth, tangent and nowhere zero.

Milnor/Chapter5/HairyBall.lean, line 282  ·  Milnor.Sphere.hairy_ball

theorem hairy_ball (hn : Even n) (hn0 : 0 < n) (v : Sph n → EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin (n + 1)))
    (hv : ContMDiff 𝓘(ℝ, EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)) 𝓘(ℝ, EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin (n + 1))) ∞ v)
    (htang : ∀ q, ⟪q.1, v q⟫ = 0) : ∃ q, v q = 0 := …

Milnor/Chapter5/HairyBall.lean, line 343  ·  Milnor.Sphere.exists_nonvanishing_field

theorem exists_nonvanishing_field (hn : Odd n) :
    ∃ v : Sph n → EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin (n + 1)),
      ContMDiff 𝓘(ℝ, EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)) 𝓘(ℝ, EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin (n + 1))) ∞ v ∧
      (∀ q, ⟪q.1, v q⟫ = 0) ∧ ∀ q, v q ≠ 0 := …

What differs

  • Milnor states the equivalence for every $n$; the formal even half assumes $n > 0$ as well, so $\mathbb S^0$, where every tangent field vanishes for trivial reasons, is not covered.
  • Milnor's field for odd $n$ pairs adjacent coordinates; the formal one splits the coordinates into two halves, $(a,b) \mapsto (b,-a)$, the two differing by a permutation of coordinates.
  • The rotation is parametrized by $t \in \mathbb R$ with angle $\pi t$, so that the homotopy is defined on $\mathbb S^n \times \mathbb R$ rather than on $\mathbb S^n \times [0,\pi]$.
  • Milnor's theorem, like the formal one, is about smooth fields; the continuous case, which follows by approximation, is not formalized.

Chapter 6

Milnor §6, pp. 32–40: vector fields, the index, the Euler number

V6.0

The index of a zero of a vector field

restated
field smooth on the whole spacedegree taken on the unit sphereindex 0 at a non-isolated zero

Milnor §6, p. 32

For an open $U \subseteq \mathbb R^m$ and a smooth $v : U \to \mathbb R^m$ with an isolated zero at $z$, the normalized field $\bar v = v/\|v\|$ carries a small sphere about $z$ into the unit sphere, and the degree of that map is the index $\iota$ of $v$ at $z$. Each sphere is oriented as the boundary of the corresponding disk. Which small radius is meant is not said; the independence is left to homotopy invariance of the degree.

He introduces the index through pictures (Figure 12) and through the plane fields $z^k$ and $\bar z^k$, and never states the independence of the radius, or the invariance under a homotopy without zeros on the sphere, as a lemma.

Proved in Lean

Let $m = n+1$ with $n \ge 1$. For a smooth $v : \mathbb R^{n+1} \to \mathbb R^{n+1}$ and a radius $r$ such that $v(z) = 0$ and $v$ has no other zero in the closed $r$-ball, the index at a radius $0 < \varepsilon \le r$ is the degree of $u \mapsto v(z + \varepsilon u)/|v(z + \varepsilon u)|$ from the unit sphere $S^n$ to itself, both copies carrying the orientation of V5.9. This degree is the same for all such $\varepsilon$, so the index of $v$ at $z$ is defined by choosing one admissible radius, and is set to $0$ when none exists. It is unchanged by a smooth homotopy of $v$ that leaves no zero on the $\varepsilon$-sphere, and it depends only on the germ of $v$ at $z$.

Milnor/Chapter6/Index.lean, line 81  ·  Milnor.Sphere.indexAt

    (z : EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin (n + 1))) (ε : ℝ) : ℤ := …

What differs

  • Milnor's $v$ lives on an open $U \subseteq \mathbb R^m$; the formal $v$ is smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$, which loses nothing since a local field agrees with a global one near a compact set.
  • The source of the degree is the unit sphere reparametrized by $u \mapsto z + \varepsilon u$, not the sphere of radius $\varepsilon$ about $z$; the affine identification carries the orientation across.
  • Milnor orients each sphere as the boundary of its disk; the formal spheres carry the opposite convention, but the same one at source and at target, so the integer is his.
  • Independence of the radius is proved and needs $n \ge 1$; the index is defined to be $0$ when $z$ is not an isolated zero, a case Milnor excludes by hypothesis.
V6.1

Lemma 1 of §5 for a level set in a manifold

restated
$X$ presented as $\{g \ge 0\}$$f$ smooth only on an open $U$transversality assumed, not derived

Milnor §5, Lemma 1 · p. 28

Let $M$ be the boundary of a compact oriented manifold $X$, oriented as the boundary of $X$. If $f : M \to N$ extends to a smooth $F : X \to N$, then $\deg(f; y) = 0$ for every regular value $y$.

This is the lemma on which every degree computation of §6 rests; there it is applied to a region with small balls removed and to $f = v/|v|$, which is defined only where $v \ne 0$.

Proved in Lean

The level-set orientation of V5.8 is carried from a vector space to any oriented $(n+1)$-manifold $M$ with charts in $E$: for a smooth $g : M \to \mathbb R$ with $0$ a regular value, $g^{-1}(0)$ is oriented by an outward vector $w$ with $dg(w) < 0$ first, followed by a positive basis of the tangent space, and this is continuous in charts. At a point of $g^{-1}(0)$ the sign of $f|g^{-1}(0)$ for this orientation is $(-1)^{n+1}$ times the sign of the pair map $(f, g)$. Milnor's lemma then reads: for $M$ and $N$ Hausdorff, $N$ an oriented boundaryless $n$-manifold, $f$ smooth on an open $U \supseteq g^{-1}(0)$, $y$ a regular value of $f|U$, the transversality condition of V2.6 holding at each point of $f^{-1}(y) \cap g^{-1}(0)$, and $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g \ge 0\} \cap U$ compact, the set $f^{-1}(y) \cap g^{-1}(0)$ is finite and $\deg(f|g^{-1}(0); y) = 0$.

What differs

  • Milnor's $X$ is an abstract compact oriented manifold with boundary; here $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ sits inside a boundaryless $M$ and $\partial X$ is the level set $g^{-1}(0)$.
  • Compactness is required of $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g \ge 0\} \cap U$ rather than of $X$, because the application has $f = v/|v|$, smooth only on the open set where $v \ne 0$.
  • The hypothesis at the points of $f^{-1}(y) \cap g^{-1}(0)$ is the transversality condition of V2.6; that regularity of $y$ for $f|g^{-1}(0)$ implies it is proved as a separate lemma.
  • The boundary signs are read through the ambient pair map $(f, g)$, which differs from the level-set sign by the factor $(-1)^{n+1}$.
V6.2

Sign of a map between two level sets

not in the book
tool for the sign computationsreplaces Milnor's isotopies

Milnor no counterpart in the book

The book has no such lemma. Where a degree has to be moved from one hypersurface to another, Milnor deforms the map instead: in §6 he compares the index before and after a diffeomorphism by an isotopy (Lemma 2), and inside the proof of Lemma 3 he homotopes $\bar v|\partial X$ to the Gauss map.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ and $M'$ be oriented manifolds with charts in $E$, let $G : M \to \mathbb R$ and $G' : M' \to \mathbb R$ be smooth with $0$ a regular value of each, and let $\Phi : M \to M'$ be smooth along $G^{-1}(0)$, differentiable at $q \in G^{-1}(0)$, and carry $G^{-1}(0)$ into $G'^{-1}(0)$. If $dG'_{\Phi(q)} \circ d\Phi_q = c\, dG_q$ for some $c \ne 0$, then the sign at $q$ of the restricted map $G^{-1}(0) \to G'^{-1}(0)$, both level sets carrying the orientation of V6.1, equals $\operatorname{sgn}(c)$ times the sign of $\Phi$ at $q$ for the ambient orientations.

Milnor/Chapter6/LevelMap.lean, line 231  ·  Milnor.signAt_levelOrientM_map'

theorem signAt_levelOrientM_map' {Φ : M → M'} (hΦG : ∀ x, G x = 0 → G' (Φ x) = 0) :
    letI hS := isSliceSubmanifold_preimage (Z := Z) (finrank_Z_add hE hZ) hG h0

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book: it is what lets every degree of §6 be transported along an explicit map — the inclusion of a component, the affine map $u \mapsto z + \varepsilon u$, a diffeomorphism.
  • It is used three times in the proof of the region lemma and twice in the proof of Lemma 1 of §6, where it takes the place of Milnor's Lemma 2.
  • Its proof is the contracted-form calculus of V5.8: the pullback of the outward vector is a positive multiple of the outward vector plus a tangent vector, and the tangent part drops out.
V6.3

Index sum over a region as a boundary degree

restated
region presented as $\{G \ge 0\}$one common radius for all ballsthe $z_i$ need not be zeros

Milnor §6, p. 36

In the proof of Lemma 3 Milnor removes an $\varepsilon$-ball around each zero of $v$, obtaining a manifold with boundary on which $\bar v = v/\|v\|$ is defined. The degrees of $\bar v$ over the boundary components then add to zero; since each small sphere gets the wrong orientation, the degree over $\partial X$ equals the index sum.

He states this only as a step inside a proof, and leaves implicit the choice of a common regular value of $\bar v$ on the several boundary components.

Proved in Lean

Let $X = \{G \ge 0\}$ be compact for a smooth $G : \mathbb R^{n+1} \to \mathbb R$ with $n \ge 1$ and $0$ a regular value, let $\varepsilon > 0$ and $z_1, \dots, z_k$ satisfy $G > 0$ on every closed ball $\bar B_\varepsilon(z_i)$ and $2\varepsilon < |z_i - z_j|$ for $i \ne j$, and let every zero of the smooth field $v$ in $X$ be one of the $z_i$. Then the sum over $i$ of the indices of $v$ at $z_i$, each taken at the radius $\varepsilon$, equals the degree of $v/|v| : \partial X \to S^n$, where $\partial X = G^{-1}(0)$ carries the orientation of V6.1 reversed.

theorem isOpen_regularValues [CompactSpace M] [T2Space N] (hE : finrank ℝ E = n)

What differs

  • Milnor's $X$ is any compact manifold with boundary in $\mathbb R^m$; the formal $X$ is $\{G \ge 0\}$ for a globally smooth $G$ with $0$ a regular value, and $\partial X$ is $G^{-1}(0)$.
  • All the balls share one radius $\varepsilon$, disjointness is the explicit inequality $2\varepsilon < |z_i - z_j|$, and the indices are all taken at that same radius.
  • The hypothesis is only that every zero of $v$ in $X$ is among the $z_i$, so the $z_i$ themselves need not be zeros; a point where $v \ne 0$ contributes $0$ to the sum.
  • The common regular value of $v/|v|$ on $\partial X$ and on the $k$ spheres exists because regular values of a map with compact domain are open and dense and $S^n$ is a Baire space.
V6.4

Lemma 3 of §6, Hopf's lemma

restated
region presented as $\{G \ge 0\}$outward means $dG(v) < 0$isolated zeros replaced by $k$ balls

Milnor §6, Lemma 3 · p. 35

Let $X \subseteq \mathbb R^m$ be a compact $m$-manifold with boundary and let the Gauss map $g : \partial X \to S^{m-1}$ send a boundary point to the outward unit normal there. If a smooth vector field $v$ on $X$ has isolated zeros and points out of $X$ along the boundary, the index sum $\sum \iota$ equals the degree of $g$; in particular it does not depend on the choice of $v$.

The proof is one paragraph: remove small balls, apply Lemma 1 of §5, and note that $\bar v|\partial X$ is homotopic to $g$.

Proved in Lean

Under the hypotheses of the region lemma — $X = \{G \ge 0\}$ compact, $0$ a regular value of the smooth $G$, pairwise disjoint closed $\varepsilon$-balls about $z_1, \dots, z_k$ inside $\{G > 0\}$ containing every zero of $v$ in $X$ — suppose in addition that $dG_x(v(x)) < 0$ at every $x$ of $\partial X = G^{-1}(0)$. Then the sum of the indices of $v$ at the $z_i$ equals the degree of the Gauss map $x \mapsto -\nabla G(x)/|\nabla G(x)|$ of $\partial X$, an expression in which $v$ does not occur.

  • Milnor's $X$ is an abstract compact manifold with boundary in $\mathbb R^m$; here it is $\{G \ge 0\}$, and the outward unit normal is the explicit $-\nabla G/|\nabla G|$.
  • "Points out of $X$" becomes the inequality $dG(v) < 0$ on $G^{-1}(0)$, and "isolated zeros" becomes finitely many disjoint closed balls of one radius covering all zeros of $v$ in $X$.

V6.5

Lemma 1 of §6, invariance of the index

restated
Lemma 2 proved but not used (V6.12)fields global, matched on one ballno orientation hypothesis on $f$

Milnor §6, Lemma 1 · p. 33

Suppose the vector field $v$ on $U$ corresponds under a diffeomorphism $f : U \to U'$ to the field $v' = df \circ v \circ f^{-1}$ on $U'$. Then the index of $v$ at an isolated zero $z$ equals the index of $v'$ at $f(z)$.

He proves it from Lemma 2 (p. 33), that an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $\mathbb R^m$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity, and settles the orientation-reversing case by conjugating with a reflection.

Proved in Lean

Let $U, U' \subseteq \mathbb R^{n+1}$ be open with $n \ge 1$, and let $f$ and $g$ be smooth on $U$ and on $U'$ and mutually inverse there. Let $v$ be smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $v(z) = 0$ and no other zero in the closed ball $\bar B_r(z) \subseteq U$, and let $w$ be smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ and equal to $df \circ v \circ f^{-1}$ at every point of $f(\bar B_r(z))$. Then the index of $w$ at $f(z)$ equals the index of $v$ at $z$; no orientation hypothesis is placed on $f$.

What differs

  • Milnor's route is his Lemma 2, that an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $\mathbb R^m$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity; the formal proof does not take it. Lemma 2 is itself proved, in V6.12, and nothing in the development depends on it.
  • In its place the index of $w$ at $f(z)$ is a boundary degree over $f(S_r(z))$, transported by V6.2 with $c = -r^2$, which produces the factor $-\operatorname{sgn}\det df$, constant on the ball.
  • A second factor $\operatorname{sgn}\det df_z$ is the sign of $u \mapsto Au/|Au|$ on $S^n$ for $A = df_z$, by the matrix determinant lemma; the two cancel, so $f$ need not preserve orientation.
  • The correspondence $w = df \circ v \circ f^{-1}$ is required only on $f(\bar B_r(z))$, and both fields are smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ rather than on $U$ and $U'$.
V6.6

The ε-neighbourhood theorem

restated
an exercise in the book, proved hereno normal bundle in the statement$r$ defined on all of $\mathbb R^k$

Milnor §8, Problems 11–12 · p. 52

Problem 11 asks the reader to show that the normal bundle space of a compact boundaryless $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ is a smooth manifold and that $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ carries the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of $M \times 0$ in it diffeomorphically onto the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood $N_\varepsilon$ of $M$ in $\mathbb R^k$. Problem 12 asks for the retraction $r : N_\varepsilon \to M$ with $r(x + v) = x$, and for the fact that $x + v$ is closer to $x$ than to any other point of $M$.

In §6 (p. 38) he uses the theorem without proof: for $x \in N_\varepsilon$ he takes $r(x)$ to be the closest point of $M$, notes that $x - r(x)$ must be perpendicular to the tangent space at $r(x)$, and asserts that $r$ is smooth and well defined for $\varepsilon$ small.

Proved in Lean

Let $N \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ be a compact nonempty submanifold, given by slice charts with Euclidean model $\mathbb R^n$ and Euclidean complement. Then there are $\varepsilon > 0$ and a map $r : \mathbb R^k \to N$ with seven properties: $|x - r(x)| = \operatorname{dist}(x, N)$ for every $x$ of $\mathbb R^k$, so the distance to $N$ is attained everywhere and $r$ selects a point attaining it; for $\operatorname{dist}(x, N) < \varepsilon$ any nearest point of $x$ in $N$ equals $r(x)$; $r$ is the identity on $N$; $r$ is $C^\infty$ at each such $x$; $x - r(x)$ is orthogonal to $T_{r(x)}N$ there; $dr_x$ takes values in $T_{r(x)}N$ there; and for $x \in N$ and $v$ orthogonal to $T_xN$ one has $r(x + sv) = x$ for all $s$ near $0$.

Milnor/Chapter6/Tubular3.lean, line 146  ·  Milnor.Tub.tubular

theorem tubular : ∃ ε > 0, ∃ r : EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin k) → N,
    letI := hN.chartedSpace

Milnor/Chapter6/Tubular4.lean, line 35  ·  Milnor.Tub.tubr_spec

theorem tubr_spec :
    letI := hN.chartedSpace

Milnor/Chapter6/Tubular4.lean, line 33  ·  Milnor.Tub.tubr

def tubr : EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin (K + 1)) → N := (tubular hN hNc hne).choose_spec.2.choose

Milnor/Chapter6/Tubular4.lean, line 30  ·  Milnor.Tub.tubε_pos

theorem tubε_pos : 0 < tubε hN hNc hne := (tubular hN hNc hne).choose_spec.1

What differs

  • Milnor leaves this to the reader in §8 and quotes it in §6; it is proved here from the inverse function theorem applied to the nearest-point equation $(d\varphi_z)^*(x - \varphi(z)) = 0$.
  • The formal statement says nothing about the normal bundle space, about $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ being a diffeomorphism, or about $N_\varepsilon$ being a manifold with boundary, which Problem 11 asks.
  • It gives two things the book does not state: $r$ is defined on all of $\mathbb R^k$ and always realizes $\operatorname{dist}(x, N)$, and $dr_x$ maps into $T_{r(x)}N$ on the neighbourhood.
  • Hidden hypotheses: $N$ is nonempty, and its slice charts have Euclidean model and complement so that the adjoint $(d\varphi_z)^*$ is available.
V6.7

The region N_ε/2 and the extended field

restated
region at radius $\varepsilon/2$$G$ made global by a cut-offtangency of $v$ an explicit hypothesis

Milnor §6, p. 39

With $\varphi(x) = \|x - r(x)\|^2$, whose gradient is $2(x - r(x))$, the boundary $\partial N_\varepsilon = \varphi^{-1}(\varepsilon^2)$ has outward unit normal $g(x) = (x - r(x))/\varepsilon$. A vector field $v$ on $M$ is extended over $N_\varepsilon$ by $w(x) = (x - r(x)) + v(r(x))$; then $w \cdot g = \varepsilon > 0$ along the boundary, so $w$ points outward, and $w$ vanishes only at the zeros of $v$, the two summands being orthogonal.

The gradient computation is called easy and the statement about the zeros clear; both rest on the orthogonality supplied by the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem.

Proved in Lean

With $\varepsilon$ and $r$ as above, $X = \{x : \operatorname{dist}(x, N) \le \varepsilon/2\}$ is compact and equals $\{G \ge 0\}$ for a smooth $G : \mathbb R^{K+1} \to \mathbb R$ that agrees with $(\varepsilon/2)^2 - |x - r(x)|^2$ on a neighbourhood of $X$, and $0$ is a regular value of $G$. For a smooth $v$ on $N$ all of whose values are tangent to $N$, the field $w(x) = v(r(x)) + (x - r(x))$ is smooth on the tubular neighbourhood, restricts to $v$ on $N$, and its zeros there are exactly the zeros of $v$. Along $\partial X$ one has $dG_x(w(x)) = -2|x - r(x)|^2 < 0$, so $w$ points outward.

What differs

  • The region is taken at radius $\varepsilon/2$, so that it lies well inside the set where $r$ is smooth, and is presented as $\{G \ge 0\}$ rather than as $\varphi \le \varepsilon^2$.
  • $G$ is only locally the formula $(\varepsilon/2)^2 - |x - r(x)|^2$; a cut-off makes it smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$, which is what the region lemma requires of it.
  • Outwardness is recorded as $dG(w) = -2|x - r(x)|^2 < 0$ rather than as $w \cdot g = \varepsilon$; the two say the same thing up to a positive factor.
  • That $v$ takes its values in the tangent spaces of $N$ is an explicit hypothesis of the statements about the zeros and about outwardness; for Milnor it is part of being a vector field on $M$.
V6.8

The index of a tangent field on a submanifold

restated
definition and theorem exchangedchart definition is V6.14depends on the retraction $r$

Milnor §6, p. 33

Granting Lemma 1, Milnor defines the index of a vector field $w$ on an arbitrary manifold $M$ at a zero $z$: take a parametrization $h : U \to M$ of a neighbourhood of $z$ and let $\iota$ be the index of the corresponding field $dh^{-1} \circ w \circ h$ at $h^{-1}(z)$. Lemma 1 is exactly what makes this independent of the parametrization.

On p. 39 he observes that for $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ this index also equals the index of the extended field $(x - r(x)) + v(r(x))$ at $z$, by the derivative computation of Lemma 5 (p. 37).

Proved in Lean

For a compact nonempty submanifold $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ and a smooth tangent field $v$ on $N$, the index of $v$ at $q \in N$ is defined to be the index at $q$ of a globally smooth field agreeing with $x \mapsto v(r(x)) + (x - r(x))$ near the region $X$. Since the index of a field depends only on its germ, the value is independent of the extension chosen, and it may be computed from any smooth field that agrees with $v(r(\cdot)) + (\cdot - r(\cdot))$ near $q$ and has $q$ as an isolated zero.

    (hv : letI := hN.chartedSpace;

What differs

  • Milnor's definition goes through a parametrization and needs Lemma 1 of §6 to be well posed; the formal definition is his p. 39 identity instead, so no invariance lemma is used.
  • Consequently the chart definition is a development of its own, V6.14, where it is formalized together with Milnor's Lemma 5 (V6.13) and shown to agree with this one at a non-degenerate zero; the agreement at an arbitrary isolated zero is not proved.
  • The definition refers to the nearest-point retraction $r$, which is canonical once $N$ is given, and to an arbitrary global smooth extension, which the germ property makes harmless.
V6.9

The Poincaré–Hopf theorem

restated
degenerate zeros allowed$\chi(N)$ only asserted to existEuler characteristic not formalized

Milnor §6, Theorem 1 · p. 38

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ be a compact boundaryless manifold and $N_\varepsilon$ its closed $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood, a smooth manifold with boundary for $\varepsilon$ small. For any vector field $v$ on $M$ with only nondegenerate zeros, the index sum $\sum \iota$ equals the degree of the Gauss map $g : \partial N_\varepsilon \to S^{k-1}$; in particular it does not depend on the choice of $v$.

The theorem announced on p. 35 is stronger: for a compact $M$ and a field with isolated zeros the sum is the Euler number $\chi(M) = \sum_i (-1)^i \operatorname{rank} H_i(M)$, and p. 40 sketches the three further steps that would establish it.

Proved in Lean

Let $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ with $K \ge 1$ be a compact nonempty submanifold. There is an integer $\chi(N)$ such that for every smooth tangent field $v$ on $N$ whose zero set is a finite, injectively indexed list $z_1, \dots, z_j$, the sum of the indices of $v$ at the $z_i$, in the sense of V6.8, equals $\chi(N)$. In the proof $\chi(N)$ is the degree of the Gauss map of $\partial N_{\varepsilon/2}$, but the statement only asserts that such an integer exists.

Milnor/Chapter6/PoincareHopf.lean, line 114  ·  Milnor.Tub.poincare_hopf

theorem poincare_hopf (hK : 0 < K) :
    ∃ χN : ℤ, ∀ (v : N → EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin (K + 1)))
      (hv : letI := hN.chartedSpace;

What differs

  • Milnor's Theorem 1 assumes all zeros nondegenerate; the formal statement assumes only that there are finitely many, which on a compact $N$ is the same as isolated.
  • That gain is bought by defining the index through the extended field (V6.8). Milnor's Lemma 5 (p. 37), which identifies that index with the one computed in a chart at a non-degenerate zero, is proved separately, in V6.13 and V6.14, and is not used here.
  • The Gauss-map degree appears only inside the proof, over $\partial N_{\varepsilon/2}$ rather than $\partial N_\varepsilon$, and the statement quantifies the invariant existentially.
  • Not formalized: the identification of $\chi(N)$ with the Euler number of p. 35 (G.1), and the theorem for a manifold not presented as a submanifold of Euclidean space. The value of $\chi(N)$ for the sphere is computed in V6.15.
V6.10

Lemma 4 of §6: the index at a non-degenerate zero

hypotheses explicit
$n \ge 1$, the index being a degreefield smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$isolatedness proved, not assumed

Milnor §6, Lemma 4 · p. 36

A zero $z$ of $v$ is non-degenerate when $dv_z$ is non-singular, and such a zero is isolated, by the inverse function theorem. Lemma 4: the index of $v$ at a non-degenerate zero is $+1$ or $-1$ according as the determinant of $dv_z$ is positive or negative, that is $\operatorname{sign}\det dv_z$. Milnor shrinks $U$ to a ball on which $v$ is a diffeomorphism, applies the construction of Lemma 2 to $v$ itself to replace $v$ by $dv_z$, connects $dv_z$ to the identity inside $GL^+(m)$ when the determinant is positive, and conjugates with a reflection when it is negative.

This is what makes an index computable; with Lemma 5 it is how every index of the section is evaluated.

Proved in Lean

Let $n \ge 1$ and let $v$ be a smooth vector field on $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $v(z) = 0$ and $dv_z$ bijective. Then the index of $v$ at $z$, in the sense of V6.0, is $\operatorname{sgn}\det dv_z$; Milnor's own form, $+1$ or $-1$ according to the sign of the determinant, stands beside it. That $z$ is an isolated zero is not assumed but proved: one uniform estimate does two jobs, giving a radius $r > 0$ such that on the punctured closed $r$-ball no combination $dv_z(x - z) + b\,(v(x) - dv_z(x - z))$ with $0 \le b \le 1$ vanishes — the case $b = 1$ says that $z$ is isolated, and the whole range says that the straight-line homotopy from $v$ to its linear part leaves the $r$-sphere free of zeros. The degree of the sphere map $u \mapsto dv_z(u)/|dv_z(u)|$ of the linear field is then $\operatorname{sgn}\det dv_z$: that map is a bijection of $S^n$ whose sign is that constant at every point, so every point is a regular value with exactly one preimage.

    (hvz : v z = 0) (hA : Bijective (fderiv ℝ v z)) :

Milnor/Chapter6/Nondegenerate.lean, line 69  ·  Milnor.Sphere.exists_radius_of_nondegenerate

theorem exists_radius_of_nondegenerate {v : 𝔼 → 𝔼}
    (hv : ContMDiff 𝓘(ℝ, 𝔼) 𝓘(ℝ, 𝔼) ∞ v) {z : 𝔼} (hvz : v z = 0)
    (hA : Bijective (fderiv ℝ v z)) :

      fderiv ℝ v z (x - z) + b • (v x - fderiv ℝ v z (x - z)) ≠ 0 := …

What differs

  • The dimension condition $n \ge 1$ is named, as everywhere in §6: the index is a degree on $S^n$, so the ambient space has dimension at least two. Milnor's index needs $S^{m-1}$ connected for the same reason, and he does not say so.
  • As in V6.0 the field is smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ rather than on an open $U$; the index depends only on the germ at $z$, and a field on an open set agrees near a compact subset with a global one, so nothing is lost.
  • Milnor deduces the lemma from his Lemma 2 (V6.12), through the isotopy $v(tx)/t$ and the connectedness of $GL^+$; the formal proof is a straight-line homotopy to the linear part, and the single estimate above makes the zero isolated and the homotopy zero-free at once.
  • The step from a constant pointwise sign to a degree is a new lemma: the sphere map of a bijective linear field is a bijection of $S^n$, so its degree is that one sign. Milnor reads the degree off the identity map and a reflection instead.
  • His worked examples at the start of §6 — the source, the sink, the saddle, the centre — are linear fields and so are instances of this formula; the complex examples $v(z) = z^k$ of index $k$ and $v(z) = \bar z^{\,k}$ of index $-k$ have no formal counterpart.
V6.11

Hadamard's lemma, and a smooth parametric integral

stronger
the integral theorem is not in Mathlibcoordinate-free, any normed spacestarget complete, not $\mathbb R^m$

Milnor §6, p. 33

Inside the proof of Lemma 2 Milnor needs the decomposition $f(x) = \sum_i x_i\,g_i(x)$ of a smooth $f$ with $f(0) = 0$, with the $g_i$ smooth and $g_i(0) = \partial f/\partial x_i(0)$. It comes from $f(x) = \int_0^1 \frac{d}{dt} f(tx)\,dt$, and the smoothness of $g_i(x) = \int_0^1 \partial_i f(tx)\,dt$ is settled by the remark that one may differentiate under the integral sign. With it his isotopy $F(x, t) = \sum_i x_i\,g_i(tx)$, which is $f(tx)/t$ for $t > 0$ and $df_0$ at $t = 0$, is smooth up to $t = 0$.

The whole difficulty of his isotopy is that smoothness at $t = 0$, and the decomposition is what supplies it.

Proved in Lean

Three statements, for real normed spaces $E$ and $F$ with $F$ complete. First: if $h : E \times \mathbb R \to F$ is of class $C^n$, or $C^\infty$, then so is $x \mapsto \int_0^1 h(x, t)\,dt$. Mathlib has no such theorem; the proof is an induction on $n$, differentiating under the integral sign and dominating on a tube around the compact segment $\{x\} \times [0, 1]$, so that neither finite dimension nor local compactness is needed. Secondly, Hadamard's lemma, coordinate-free: a smooth $f$ with $f(0) = 0$ factors as $f(x) = L(x)\,x$ for a smooth family $L$ of continuous linear maps, namely $L(x) = \int_0^1 df_{tx}\,dt$, and $L(0) = df_0$. Thirdly, consequently, $t^{-1} f(tx)$ extends to a smooth map on all of $E \times \mathbb R$ with value $df_0(x)$ at $t = 0$, both as an existence statement and for the explicit formula with a case split at $t = 0$ in which the isotopy of V6.12 is written.

What differs

  • Milnor works in $\mathbb R^m$ and writes the decomposition in coordinates; the formal statement is coordinate-free, $f(x) = L(x)\,x$ with $L$ a smooth family of continuous linear maps between arbitrary real normed spaces.
  • The one hypothesis he would not state is completeness of the target, needed for the Bochner integrals to exist; his spaces being $\mathbb R^m$, it is automatic.
  • His "differentiate under the integral sign" is a theorem Mathlib does not have: that an integral over a fixed interval is $C^n$ in a parameter when the integrand is. It is proved here by induction on $n$, the domination coming from compactness of the segment, and it needs neither finite dimension nor local compactness.
  • The consequence is stated in the form the isotopy needs, including the explicit formula with a case split at $t = 0$, so that no smoothness has to be rechecked when the isotopy of V6.12 is assembled.
V6.12

Lemma 2 of §6: a diffeomorphism of ℝᵐ is isotopic to the identity

stronger
any finite-dimensional space, any $m$determinant positive at one pointevery real time slice a diffeomorphism

Milnor §6, Lemma 2 · p. 33

Any orientation-preserving diffeomorphism $f$ of $\mathbb R^m$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity. Milnor composes with a translation so that $f(0) = 0$, uses the decomposition of p. 33 to see that $F(x, t) = f(tx)/t$, with $F(x, 0) = df_0$, is a smooth isotopy from $df_0$ to $f$, and then joins $df_0$ to the identity through linear isomorphisms, the matrices of positive determinant being path connected. He uses the lemma to prove Lemma 1, that the index is unchanged by a diffeomorphism.

The connectedness of $GL^+(m)$ is asserted rather than proved, here and again in §7 (V7.3).

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional real normed space and let $f : E \to E$ be a diffeomorphism in the unbundled sense of V6.5: smooth, with a smooth two-sided inverse. If the Jacobian determinant of $f$ is positive at one point — equivalently at every point, the sign being constant, which is proved — then $f$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity. An isotopy is a smooth $H : E \times \mathbb R \to E$ with $H(\cdot, 0) = f$, $H(\cdot, 1) = \mathrm{id}$ and every time slice, at every real time, a diffeomorphism of $E$, the notion already used in V4.4. Milnor's own case $E = \mathbb R^m$ is recorded separately, with no condition on $m$. The three steps are his: the translation to a diffeomorphism fixing the origin; the isotopy $f(tx)/t$ from that one to its derivative there, smooth by V6.11; and the $GL^+$ path of V7.3.

What differs

  • Milnor's $\mathbb R^m$ becomes any finite-dimensional real normed space, and positivity of the dimension is not needed; his own statement is recorded as the case $E = \mathbb R^m$.
  • "Orientation-preserving" is positivity of $\det df$ at a single point, the weakest of the equivalent forms, and that the sign is the same at every point is part of the development rather than an observation.
  • Isotopy is the project's notion (V4.4): every slice, at every real time, is a diffeomorphism, where Milnor's $F$ lives on $\mathbb R^m \times [0, 1]$. Three things he passes over: transitivity of isotopy, which needs the two isotopies made constant near the join before gluing; that the slices at negative times are diffeomorphisms too; and invertibility of $df_0$, which comes from the chain rule on the two-sided inverse.
  • The Lean file sits in Chapter 7, because its last step is the $GL^+$ path proved there (V7.3) and Chapter 6 may not import Chapter 7.
  • Nothing in the development depends on this lemma: §6 replaced Milnor's appeal to it in Lemma 1 by a direct computation of signs (V6.5), so it is formalized only because it is a theorem of the book.
V6.13

Lemma 5 of §6, and the derivative of the retraction

hypotheses explicit
$dr_q$ is the orthogonal projectiontwo readings of the derivative$N$ compact, for the retraction

Milnor §6, Lemma 5 · p. 37

Regard a field $w$ on $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ as a map $M \to \mathbb R^k$, so that $dw_z : TM_z \to \mathbb R^k$ is defined. Lemma 5: at a zero $z$ of $w$ the map $dw_z$ carries $TM_z$ into $TM_z$, and if the resulting endomorphism has determinant $D \ne 0$ then $z$ is an isolated zero of index $\operatorname{sign} D$. He proves it with a parametrization $h$, $h(u_0) = z$: writing $w(h(u)) = \sum_j v_j(u)\,t_j(u)$ in the basis $t_i = \partial h/\partial u_i$ of the tangent spaces and differentiating in $u_i$, the terms with $v_j$ drop out at $u_0$, so the matrix of $dw_z$ in that basis is the Jacobian of $v$ at $u_0$. On p. 39 he applies the lemma to the extended field $w = v \circ r + (\mathrm{id} - r)$ on the tubular neighbourhood, whose derivative is block diagonal with blocks $dv_z|_{TM_z}$ and the identity.

It is the bridge between the index on the manifold and the index of the extended field, and the place where the derivative of the retraction is used without comment.

Proved in Lean

Let $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty submanifold of dimension $n$ and $r$ the nearest-point retraction of V6.6. First, at $q \in N$ the derivative $dr_q$ is the orthogonal projection of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ onto $T_qN$: it is the identity on $T_qN$, its image is $T_qN$, it is idempotent, and its kernel is exactly $(T_qN)^\perp$. Secondly, Lemma 5 itself, in two readings: if $v$ is a smooth tangent field on $N$ with $v(q) = 0$, then the derivative of $v$ at $q$ carries $T_qN$ into itself, both in the ambient reading — the derivative at $q$ of $x \mapsto v(r(x))$ — and in the intrinsic one, the derivative of $v : N \to \mathbb R^{K+1}$ on the image of the derivative of the inclusion; the two readings are shown to agree. Thirdly, at such a zero the derivative of the extended field $w = v \circ r + (\mathrm{id} - r)$ of V6.7 is $dv_q \circ dr_q + (\mathrm{id} - dr_q)$, which in the splitting $\mathbb R^{K+1} = T_qN \oplus (T_qN)^\perp$ is $dv_q$ on the tangent part and the identity on the normal part; hence $\det dw_q$ is the determinant of the restriction of $dv_q$ to $T_qN$, and $dw_q$ is invertible exactly when that restriction is.

What differs

  • Compactness and nonemptiness of $N$ are assumed, because the tubular neighbourhood and the retraction $r$ of V6.6 exist here only for a compact submanifold; Milnor's Lemma 5 itself needs no compactness.
  • What "the derivative of a field on a submanifold" means is not left to the reader: the ambient reading, the derivative of $v \circ r$, and the intrinsic one, the derivative of $v$ as a map of manifolds, are both defined and shown to agree on the tangent space.
  • The derivative of the retraction, which Milnor uses without comment on p. 39, is proved to be the orthogonal projection onto the tangent space; the block-diagonal form of $dw_q$ and the determinant identity follow from that.
  • His proof differentiates the field written in a coefficient basis, which presumes the coefficients smooth; the formal proof differentiates the identity $(\mathrm{id} - dr)(v \circ r) = 0$ instead and avoids the point.
  • The hypothesis $v(q) = 0$ is Milnor's and is essential: without it the derivative of $v$ at $q$ has no invariant meaning as an endomorphism of $T_qN$. The index clause of Lemma 5 is V6.14.
V6.14

The chart definition of the index, and its agreement

narrower
agreement only at non-degenerate zeros$K \ge 1$ ambient, $n \ge 2$ in a chart$N$ compact, for the retraction

Milnor §6, p. 33

Milnor defines the index of a zero of a field on a manifold through a parametrization (p. 33): a local parametrization $\psi : \Omega \to M$, with $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb R^n$ open and $\psi(u_0) = z$, pulls the field back to a field on $\Omega$, and the index at $z$ is the index of the pull-back at $u_0$; Lemma 1 (V6.5) is what makes the value independent of the parametrization. On p. 39 he identifies that index with the index of the extended field $(x - r(x)) + v(r(x))$ at $z$, by the derivative computation of Lemma 5 (p. 37).

The identification is what lets him prove the Poincaré–Hopf theorem on the tubular neighbourhood and read the answer on the manifold.

Proved in Lean

Let $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty submanifold of dimension $n$ and $v$ a smooth tangent field on $N$. The pull-back of $v$ through a slice-chart parametrization is smooth, and its index at the parameter point is Milnor's chart index. It depends neither on the parametrization nor on the extension: for any smooth local parametrization $\psi$ of $N$ at $q$ with a smooth ambient left inverse and with $d\psi_u$ spanning the tangent space, and any globally smooth field on $\mathbb R^n$ agreeing near the parameter point with the pull-back and having an isolated zero there, the index is that same number — this is Milnor's Lemma 1 (V6.5) applied to the transition map. At a non-degenerate zero the two definitions agree: the index of the extended field is $\operatorname{sgn}\det(dv_q|T_qN)$ by V6.13 and V6.10, and so is the chart index, because the derivative of the chart conjugates the derivative of the pulled-back field into that restriction. Two dimension conditions are needed, and they differ, the two indices being degrees on spheres of different dimensions: $K \ge 1$ for the ambient index and $n \ge 2$ for the chart index, the index of a field on $\mathbb R^n$ being a degree on $S^{n-1}$.

What differs

  • The agreement of the two definitions is proved only at a non-degenerate zero, which is what Lemma 5 gives and what the book uses. At an arbitrary isolated zero the two are degrees of maps of spheres of different dimensions, $S^K$ and $S^{n-1}$, so no appeal to invariance under diffeomorphisms compares them: one would first need a suspension formula, that the index of a field of the shape $(u, y) \mapsto (\tilde v(u), y)$ is the index of $\tilde v$. The project does not have it, and Milnor does not prove it either.
  • Two dimension conditions are carried where Milnor states none, and they are not the same one: $K \ge 1$ for the ambient index, $n \ge 2$ for the chart index, each because a degree is taken on a sphere that has to be connected.
  • Compactness and nonemptiness of $N$ come from the tubular neighbourhood, as in V6.13, and the chart index is defined from the slice-chart parametrizations of Chapter 2 rather than from a parametrization chosen by hand.
  • Independence is proved in the strong form: any smooth local parametrization with a smooth ambient left inverse and spanning derivative, and any global smooth extension of the pull-back with an isolated zero, give the same number. Milnor fixes one parametrization and appeals to Lemma 1.
  • Definition and theorem are exchanged, as V6.8 records: the extended-field index is the definition here and the chart index the theorem, so the Poincaré–Hopf theorem of V6.9 does not rest on this row.
V6.15

The Euler number of the sphere

restated
index sum, not the Euler characteristicevery field with finitely many zerosthe odd case computed twice

Milnor §6, Example · p. 39

On $S^n$ the field $v(x) = p - (p \cdot x)\,x$, with $p$ the north pole, is tangent and vanishes exactly at $\pm p$; near the south pole it is the identity in the chart given by projection to $p^\perp$, of index $+1$, and near the north pole minus the identity, of index $\operatorname{sign}\det(-I_n) = (-1)^n$. So the Euler number of the sphere is $\chi(S^n) = 1 + (-1)^n$, which is $2$ for $n$ even and $0$ for $n$ odd. He observes that this reproves the hairy ball theorem, a field without zeros having index sum $0 \ne 2$, and that for odd $n$ the value also follows from replacing $v$ by $-v$.

$\chi$ is his name (p. 35) for the common value of the index sum; that it is the Euler characteristic of topology is the step he refers to Hopf and to the literature for.

Proved in Lean

Let $n \ge 1$. For every smooth tangent field $v$ on $S^n$ — tangency being $\langle q, v(q)\rangle = 0$ — whose zeros are finitely many distinct points $z_1, \dots, z_j$, the sum of the indices of $v$ at the $z_i$, in the sense of V6.8, is $1 + (-1)^n$; and the integer whose existence V6.9 asserts is pinned down, not merely bounded: any integer with the Poincaré–Hopf property for $S^n$ equals $1 + (-1)^n$. The even case, the odd case and the evenness of the number are stated separately. The field is Milnor's, the gradient on $S^n$ of the height function $x \mapsto \langle e, x\rangle$ for a unit vector $e$: its zeros are exactly $e$ and $-e$, the restriction of its derivative to the tangent space is minus the identity at $e$ and the identity at $-e$ (V6.13), and V6.10 with V6.14 turns those into the indices $(-1)^n$ and $1$. For odd $n$ there is a second and independent computation: the sphere then carries a nowhere-zero smooth tangent field (V5.10), whose index sum is the empty sum $0$, and a third theorem derives $1 + (-1)^n = 0$ from the two, as it must.

What differs

  • Milnor writes the value as $\chi(S^n)$; the formal statement is about the integer of V6.9, the common value of the index sum. That this integer is the Euler characteristic is not formalized and cannot even be stated — Mathlib has no Euler characteristic of a space, no Betti numbers and no cellular homology — so the sentence $\chi(S^n) = 1 + (-1)^n$ cannot be written down (G.1).
  • The hypothesis is that the zeros are finitely many, not that they are non-degenerate: Milnor computes with his own field, whose two zeros are non-degenerate, and V6.9 carries the value to every field.
  • The integer is pinned down, not merely computed: any integer with the property V6.9 asserts of $\chi(N)$ for $S^n$ is $1 + (-1)^n$, so the value belongs to the sphere and not to the proof.
  • The odd case is proved a second time with no derivative at all, from the nowhere-zero field of §5, and the two computations are shown to agree; Milnor gets the odd case from $v \mapsto -v$.
  • Three things had to be added for §6's machinery to reach the sphere: the sphere presented again as a level set with a one-dimensional complement model, its tangent space at $q$ identified as $q^\perp$ for that structure, and the derivative through the retraction of a field coming from the ambient space. For the same reason the odd-dimensional field of §5 had to be rebuilt as an ambient linear map, the project having no lemma comparing two slice-submanifold structures on the same set.

Chapter 7

Milnor §7, pp. 41–50: framed cobordism, the Pontryagin construction, the Hopf theorem

V7.0

Framed submanifolds and framed cobordism

restated
cobordism without boundaryproduct ends built into the definitionindependence in place of a basis

Milnor §7, pp. 41–42

Let $N$ and $N'$ be compact $n$-dimensional submanifolds of $M$ with $\partial N = \partial N' = \partial M = \emptyset$; the difference $m - n$ is the codimension. They are cobordant within $M$ if $N \times [0,\varepsilon) \cup N' \times (1-\varepsilon,1]$ extends to a compact manifold $X \subseteq M \times [0,1]$ with $\partial X = N \times 0 \cup N' \times 1$, meeting $M \times 0 \cup M \times 1$ only in $\partial X$. A framing of $N$ is a smooth $\mathfrak v$ assigning to each $x \in N$ a basis of the space $TN_x^\perp \subseteq TM_x$ of normal vectors, and $(N,\mathfrak v)$ is a framed submanifold. Two framed submanifolds are framed cobordant if such an $X$ carries a framing $\mathfrak u$ with $u^i(x,t) = (v^i(x),0)$ near $t = 0$ and $(w^i(x),0)$ near $t = 1$.

The product structure near the two ends is written into the definition, as the set that $X$ is required to extend.

Proved in Lean

A framed submanifold of dimension $n$ and codimension $p$ of a boundaryless slice submanifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ of dimension $m$ is a compact slice submanifold $N \subseteq M$ of dimension $n$ with $n + p = m$, together with smooth $v_1,\dots,v_p : N \to \mathbb R^k$ such that $v_i(x) \in T_xM$, $\langle v_i(x), w\rangle = 0$ for $w \in T_xN$, and the $v_i(x)$ are linearly independent. A framed cobordism is a boundaryless $(n+1)$-dimensional slice submanifold $S \subseteq M \times \mathbb R$ together with $0 < \varepsilon < \tfrac12$ such that $(x,t) \in S$ exactly when $x \in N_0$ for $t < \varepsilon$ and exactly when $x \in N_1$ for $t > 1 - \varepsilon$, with $S \cap (M \times [0,1])$ compact, and a framing $u_i : S \to \mathbb R^k \times \mathbb R$ smooth, independent, with first component in $T_xM$, orthogonal to $T_qS$ for the inner product $\langle a,b\rangle + st$, and equal to $(v^0_i,0)$ and $(v^1_i,0)$ on the two ends. Framed cobordant means that such an $S$ exists.

Milnor/Chapter7/Framed.lean, line 55  ·  Milnor.Framed.FramedSub

structure FramedSub (n p : ℕ) where

Milnor/Chapter7/Framed.lean, line 94  ·  Milnor.Framed.FramedCob

structure FramedCob (F₀ F₁ : FramedSub hM n p) where

Milnor/Chapter7/Framed.lean, line 115  ·  Milnor.Framed.FramedCobordant

def FramedCobordant (F₀ F₁ : FramedSub hM n p) : Prop := Nonempty (FramedCob hM F₀ F₁)

What differs

  • Milnor's cobordism is a compact $X \subseteq M \times [0,1]$ with boundary $N_0 \times 0 \cup N_1 \times 1$; the formal $S \subseteq M \times \mathbb R$ has no boundary and exists for all $t$.
  • Milnor's collar becomes an equality: $S$ is exactly $N_0 \times (-\infty,\varepsilon)$ and exactly $N_1 \times (1-\varepsilon,\infty)$ at the two ends; his $X$ is $S \cap (M \times [0,1])$.
  • The framing asks only that the $p$ vectors be linearly independent; that they form a basis of $TN_x^\perp$, which is Milnor's requirement, follows from $n + p = m$.
  • The ambient $M$ is a boundaryless slice submanifold of $\mathbb R^k$; its compactness, a standing hypothesis of the book, is not part of the formal definition.
V7.1

The Pontryagin manifold of a map at a regular value

restated
framing written as a pseudo-inversebasis need not be positive$M$ compact and nonempty

Milnor §7, p. 42

For $f : M \to S^p$ smooth and $y$ a regular value, choose a positively oriented basis $\mathfrak v = (v_1,\dots,v_p)$ of $T(S^p)_y$. At $x \in f^{-1}(y)$ the map $df_x$ sends $Tf^{-1}(y)_x$ to zero and carries its orthogonal complement in $TM_x$ isomorphically onto $T(S^p)_y$, so there is a unique vector $w^i(x)$ in that complement with $df_x(w^i(x)) = v_i$. The framed manifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^*\mathfrak v)$ is by definition the Pontryagin manifold associated with $f$.

The framing is pinned down by a uniqueness property, and nothing is said about its smoothness in $x$.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a compact nonempty slice submanifold of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ of dimension $m = n + p$, $f : M \to S^p$ smooth, $y$ a regular value and $b_1,\dots,b_p$ any linearly independent vectors of $\mathbb R^p$. The Pontryagin manifold is the framed submanifold with underlying set $f^{-1}(y)$ and framing $v_i(x) = A_x^*(A_xA_x^*)^{-1}b_i$, where $A_x = d\tilde F_x$ for the extension $\tilde F = c_y \circ f \circ r$ of $f$ to the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of $M$ through the nearest-point retraction $r$ and the chart $c_y$ at $y$. It satisfies $A_xv_i(x) = b_i$, and $b$ is not required to be positively oriented.

def pontryagin (hnp : n + p = m) (b : Fin p → EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin p))
    (hb : LinearIndependent ℝ b) : FramedSub hM n p where

What differs

  • Milnor's $w^i(x)$ is the unique normal vector with $df_x(w^i(x)) = v_i$; the formal framing is the explicit minimal-norm solution $A_x^*(A_xA_x^*)^{-1}b_i$, so its smoothness is a formula.
  • $A_x$ is the derivative of an ambient extension through the nearest-point retraction of the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem, which is why $M$ must be compact and nonempty.
  • A basis of $T_yS^p$ is a basis of the model space $\mathbb R^p$, because derivatives into $S^p$ are read in the chart at $y$.
  • Positivity is not asked here: orientations enter with Lemma 1 and positivity with Theorem A, while the agreement with Milnor's vector is proved separately (V7.8).
V7.2

The cobordism cut out by a homotopy

restated
isolated from two of Milnor's proofshomotopy defined for all real $t$ends assumed regular as well

Milnor §7, p. 44 (inside Lemmas 2 and 3)

The book states no such result. Inside the proofs of Lemmas 2 and 3 Milnor observes that for a homotopy $F : M \times [0,1] \to S^p$ with regular value $z$ the set $F^{-1}(z) \subseteq M \times [0,1]$ is a framed manifold and provides a framed cobordism between the framed manifolds of the two ends. In Lemma 3 he arranges beforehand that $F(x,t) = f(x)$ for $t \le \varepsilon$ and $F(x,t) = g(x)$ for $1 - \varepsilon < t \le 1$.

The same step is used twice: in Lemma 2 with the rotated homotopy $F(x,t) = r_tf(x)$, and in Lemma 3 with a homotopy already constant near the ends.

Proved in Lean

Let $0 < \varepsilon < \tfrac12$, let $F : M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ be smooth with $F(x,t) = f(x)$ for $t \le \varepsilon$ and $F(x,t) = g(x)$ for $t \ge 1 - \varepsilon$, and let $z$ be a regular value of $F$, of $f$ and of $g$. Then for any linearly independent $b$ the Pontryagin manifolds $(f^{-1}(z), f^*b)$ and $(g^{-1}(z), g^*b)$ are framed cobordant, the cobordism being $S = F^{-1}(z)$ framed through the extension of $F$ to $\mathbb R^{K+1} \times \mathbb R$. A separate lemma reparametrizes any smooth homotopy whose total map and whose two ends all have $z$ as a regular value into one that is constant for $t \le \varepsilon$ and for $t \ge 1 - \varepsilon$ and still has $z$ regular.

  • The book never states this step separately; it carries it out once inside the proof of Lemma 2 and again inside the proof of Lemma 3.
  • The homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$, constant for $t \le \varepsilon$ and for $t \ge 1 - \varepsilon$, where Milnor's $F$ is defined on $M \times [0,1]$.
  • Regularity of $z$ for $f$ and for $g$ is assumed although it follows from regularity for $F$, because the two Pontryagin manifolds must exist before the statement can be made.
  • Milnor reparametrizes and then chooses the regular value; the formal lemma keeps a value chosen beforehand, so it needs $\rho' > 0$ in the middle and regular end maps.
V7.3

Lemma 1 of §7: the positive basis does not matter

stronger
two maps allowed, not two basessame orientation, not positivityconnectedness of $GL^+$ proved

Milnor §7, Lemma 1 · p. 43

If $\mathfrak v$ and $\mathfrak v'$ are two different positively oriented bases at $y$, then the Pontryagin manifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^*\mathfrak v)$ is framed cobordant to $(f^{-1}(y), f^*\mathfrak v')$. The proof chooses a smooth path from $\mathfrak v$ to $\mathfrak v'$ in the space of positively oriented bases of $T(S^p)_y$, identified with $GL^+(p,\mathbb R)$ and therefore connected, and uses the path to frame the cobordism $f^{-1}(y) \times [0,1]$.

The connectedness of $GL^+(p,\mathbb R)$ is asserted without proof, here and again at the end of his proof of Lemma 2 of §6.

Proved in Lean

General form: let $f$ and $g$ be smooth with regular values $y$ and $z$, let $b$ and $c$ be linearly independent $p$-tuples in $\mathbb R^p$ and $A$ a $p \times p$ matrix with $\det A > 0$, and suppose $g^{-1}(z) = f^{-1}(y)$ and $g^*c = f^*(A\cdot b)$ at every point of that set. Then $(g^{-1}(z), g^*c)$ and $(f^{-1}(y), f^*b)$ are framed cobordant, the cobordism being $f^{-1}(y) \times \mathbb R$ framed by a smooth path of bases. Taking $g = f$, $z = y$ gives Milnor's Lemma 1 for any two bases of $\mathbb R^p$ that determine the same orientation. That $GL^+(p,\mathbb R)$ is connected by smooth paths is proved rather than quoted.

theorem framedCobordant_of_det_pos' (hnp : n + p = m)

    (hf : letI := hM.chartedSpace; haveI := hM.isManifold;

      ∀ t, 0 ≤ t → t ≤ 1 → 0 < det (G t) := …

What differs

  • Milnor assumes both bases positively oriented at $y$; the formal statement assumes only that they determine the same orientation of $\mathbb R^p$, which is all the proof uses.
  • The general form lets the two maps differ, provided the level sets coincide and $g^*c = f^*(A\cdot b)$ with $\det A > 0$; the proof of Theorem A needs exactly this, for $f$ and $r \circ f$.
  • The path in $GL^+(p,\mathbb R)$ is built from transvections, planar rotations by $\pi$ pairing the negative diagonal entries, and a scaling of the diagonal.
  • The cobordism is $f^{-1}(y) \times \mathbb R$, realized as a level set of $(x,t) \mapsto f(x)$, and the path of bases is made constant for $t \le \tfrac14$ and $t \ge \tfrac34$.
V7.4

Framed cobordism is an equivalence relation

restated
asserted in the book, proved herereflexivity goes through Theorem C$M$ need not be compact

Milnor §7, p. 42

After defining cobordism Milnor remarks that it is clearly an equivalence relation, and after defining framed cobordism that this again is an equivalence relation. Neither claim is proved; a figure shows two cobordisms pasted together end to end within $M$.

Transitivity is the pasting drawn in the figure, and the collars of the definition are what make the pasting smooth.

Proved in Lean

For framed submanifolds of dimension $n$ and codimension $p$ of a slice submanifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, with no compactness assumed, framed cobordism is symmetric and transitive: symmetry takes the preimage under $(x,t) \mapsto (x,1-t)$ and negates the $t$-component of the framing; transitivity shifts the second cobordism by $1$, glues along the common product strip $N_1 \times (1-\varepsilon_0, 1+\varepsilon_1)$ and halves time, doubling the $t$-component. Reflexivity is not proved directly: Chapter 7 proves it only for framed $0$-manifolds and Chapter 8 in every codimension, both times by writing the framed submanifold as a Pontryagin manifold (Theorem C) and applying the cobordism of a constant homotopy, which needs $M$ compact and nonempty.

Milnor/Chapter7/Equiv.lean, line 225  ·  Milnor.Framed.FramedCobordant.symm

    FramedCobordant hM F₁ F₀ := …

Milnor/Chapter7/Equiv.lean, line 283  ·  Milnor.Framed.FramedCobordant.trans

    (h₂ : FramedCobordant hM F₁ F₂) : FramedCobordant hM F₀ F₂ := …

Milnor/Chapter7/Reach.lean, line 47  ·  Milnor.Framed.FramedCobordant.refl'

theorem FramedCobordant.refl' (F : FramedSub hM 0 m) : FramedCobordant hM F F := …

theorem framedCobordant_refl (F : FramedSub hM n p) : FramedCobordant hM F F := …

What differs

  • Milnor asserts both equivalence relations without proof; the formal text proves symmetry and transitivity and reaches reflexivity only by a detour.
  • Reflexivity is obtained by realizing a framed submanifold as a Pontryagin manifold (Theorem C) and applying the constant homotopy; in Chapter 7 this is done only for framed $0$-manifolds.
  • Symmetry and transitivity hold for any slice submanifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, compact or not; reflexivity needs $M$ compact and nonempty, through the tubular neighbourhood.
  • Three facts about submanifolds of $M \times \mathbb R$ carry the proof: preimages under $(x,t) \mapsto (x, at+c)$, locality of tangent spaces, and gluing along an open strip.
V7.5

Theorem A of §7: the regular value does not matter

hypotheses explicit
$p \ge 1$ is a hypothesis$M$ compact and nonemptypositive basis read in the chart

Milnor §7, Theorem A · p. 42

If $y'$ is another regular value of $f$ and $\mathfrak v'$ is a positively oriented basis for $T(S^p)_{y'}$, then the framed manifold $(f^{-1}(y'), f^*\mathfrak v')$ is framed cobordant to $(f^{-1}(y), f^*\mathfrak v)$. The proof takes rotations $r_t$ of $S^p$ with $r_0$ the identity and $r_1(y) = y'$, so that $f$ is homotopic to $r_1 \circ f$; Lemma 3 then gives the cobordism, since $(r_1 \circ f)^{-1}(y') = f^{-1} \circ r_1^{-1}(y') = f^{-1}(y)$.

The half-page proof on p. 45 does not say where Lemma 1 enters, although the two bases are different; the local case, $y'$ near $y$, is Lemma 2 on p. 44.

Proved in Lean

Assume $p \ge 1$ and let $M$ be a compact nonempty slice submanifold of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ of dimension $m = n + p$. Let $f : M \to S^p$ be smooth, let $y$ and $z$ be regular values, and let $e_0$ and $e$ be bases of $\mathbb R^p$ whose orientations are the orientation of $S^p$ read in the charts at $y$ and at $z$. Then $(f^{-1}(y), f^*e_0)$ and $(f^{-1}(z), f^*e)$ are framed cobordant.

What differs

  • The formal statement carries $p \ge 1$, which Milnor does not write; the proof rotates one regular value to the other, and for $p = 0$ there is nothing to rotate.
  • $M$ is assumed compact, nonempty and of dimension $n + p$; nonemptiness is needed for the tubular neighbourhood on which every framing is built.
  • Positivity is stated as: the orientation of the basis equals the orientation of $S^p$ at $y$ read in the chart at $y$, since derivatives into the sphere are taken in charts.
  • Milnor moves $y$ to $z$ in one rotation; the formal proof inserts a $z'$ near $z$ regular for $f$, for $r \circ f$ and for the homotopy, and chains four cobordisms, regular values being open.
V7.6

Theorem B of §7, first half, and Lemma 3

hypotheses explicit
$p \ge 1$ is a hypothesishomotopy given on $M \times \mathbb R$ends assumed smooth as well

Milnor §7, Theorem B, first half · p. 42

Two mappings from $M$ to $S^p$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if the associated Pontryagin manifolds are framed cobordant; the first half is the implication from homotopy to framed cobordism. It rests on Lemma 3: if $f$ and $g$ are smoothly homotopic and $y$ is a regular value for both, then $f^{-1}(y)$ is framed cobordant to $g^{-1}(y)$. There Milnor takes a homotopy $F$ equal to $f$ for $0 \le t \le \varepsilon$ and to $g$ for $1 - \varepsilon < t \le 1$, and a regular value $z$ of $F$ close enough to $y$ that Lemma 2 applies at both ends.

Lemma 3 (p. 44) is the special case of a common regular value; different regular values are reconciled by Theorem A.

Proved in Lean

Assume $p \ge 1$ and the same hypotheses on $M$ as in Theorem A. Let $F : M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ be smooth and write $f = F(\cdot,0)$, $g = F(\cdot,1)$, whose smoothness is also assumed although it follows. Let $y$ be a regular value of $f$ and $z$ one of $g$, with positive bases $e_0$ at $y$ and $e$ at $z$. Then $(f^{-1}(y), f^*e_0)$ and $(g^{-1}(z), g^*e)$ are framed cobordant; Milnor's Lemma 3 is the case $y = z$, $e_0 = e$.

Milnor/Chapter7/ThmA.lean, line 30  ·  Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_homotopic

theorem framedCobordant_of_homotopic (hnp : n + p = m) (hp : 1 ≤ p) {F : M × ℝ → Sph p}
    (hF : letI := hM.chartedSpace; haveI := hM.isManifold;

What differs

  • The formal statement carries $p \ge 1$, inherited from Theorem A, and assumes $M$ compact, nonempty and of dimension $n + p$.
  • A smooth homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$ rather than on $M \times [0,1]$, and it is given as data rather than quantified away.
  • Smoothness of the two ends is a hypothesis although it follows from smoothness of the homotopy.
  • Milnor reparametrizes and then picks the common regular value; the formal proof picks $z'$ regular for $F$, $f$ and $g$ first, so it needs the openness of regular values to make three dense sets meet.
V7.7

The Product Neighborhood Theorem

restated
tube over a ball, not over $\mathbb R^p$retraction in place of geodesicsabstract form allows noncompact $N$

Milnor §7, Product Neighborhood Theorem · p. 45

Let $N \subseteq M$ be a compact framed submanifold of codimension $p$ with framing $\mathfrak v$ and $\partial N = \partial M = \emptyset$. Then some neighborhood of $N$ in $M$ is diffeomorphic to the product $N \times \mathbb R^p$, by a diffeomorphism under which each $x \in N$ corresponds to $(x,0)$ and each normal frame $\mathfrak v(x)$ to the standard basis of $\mathbb R^p$. For $M = \mathbb R^{n+p}$ he takes $g(x;t_1,\dots,t_p) = x + t_1v^1(x) + \cdots + t_pv^p(x)$ and proves it one-one on $N \times U_\varepsilon$ by a compactness argument with sequences; in general he replaces straight lines by geodesics and leaves the verification to the reader.

He remarks that product neighborhoods do not exist for arbitrary submanifolds, with a figure of an unframable one.

Proved in Lean

There are a smooth clamp $\lambda : \mathbb R^p \to \mathbb R^p$ equal to the identity for $|t| \le a_0$ and radii $0 < \varepsilon \le a_0$ such that $G(x,t) = r\bigl(x + \sum_i \lambda(t)_iv_i(x)\bigr)$, with $r$ the nearest-point retraction onto $M$, is a smooth map $N \times \mathbb R^p \to M$ with $G(x,0) = x$ and $dG_{(x,0)}(a,s) = a + \sum_i s_iv_i(x)$, injective with bijective derivative on $N \times B_\varepsilon$; hence $V = G(N \times B_\varepsilon)$ is open, and for $N$ nonempty the inverse is smooth on $V$. On $N \times B_\varepsilon$ the clamp is the identity, so there $G(x,s) = r(x + \sum_i s_iv_i(x))$. The injectivity radius is proved abstractly for $N$ first countable, $X$ Hausdorff, boundaryless and finite-dimensional, and a compact $K \subseteq N$ outside which injectivity is already known, so $N$ itself need not be compact.

theorem exists_tubeData (F : FramedSub hM n p) : Nonempty (TubeData hMc hne F) := …

What differs

  • Milnor's diffeomorphism is defined on all of $N \times \mathbb R^p$; the formal $G$ is smooth everywhere but a diffeomorphism only from the ball $N \times B_\varepsilon$ onto the open set $V$.
  • Geodesics are replaced by the nearest-point retraction of the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem, so the passage Milnor leaves to the reader is never needed.
  • The clamp $\lambda$, the identity near $0$, keeps $x + \sum_i \lambda(t)_iv_i(x)$ inside the domain of $r$, so that $G$ is defined and smooth on all of $N \times \mathbb R^p$.
  • The abstract injectivity radius allows $N$ noncompact with injectivity known outside a compact $K$, which the cobordism of V7.10 requires; smoothness of the inverse asks $N$ nonempty.
V7.8

Identifying the Pontryagin framing

not in the book
stated for the ambient derivativekernel found by a dimension countlinks the formula to Milnor's vector

Milnor §7, p. 42

The book has no such statement. On p. 42 Milnor defines the framing by the property that $df_x$ carries $Tf^{-1}(y)_x^\perp \subseteq TM_x$ isomorphically onto $T(S^p)_y$, so that $w^i(x)$ is the unique normal vector with $df_x(w^i(x)) = v_i$. The formal item fills the gap between that characterisation and the explicit pseudo-inverse formula of V7.1, which the book never writes.

Proved in Lean

At $x \in f^{-1}(y)$ the kernel of the ambient derivative $A_x = d\tilde F_x$ is $T_xN \oplus (T_xM)^\perp$, by a dimension count. Hence a vector $v$ tangent to $M$, orthogonal to $f^{-1}(y)$ and with $A_xv = b_i$ is the framing vector $f^*b_i(x)$. As a corollary, if $k : M \to S^p$ is smooth with $k^{-1}(\mathrm{north}) = N$ and $k(r(x + sv_i(x))) = \pi_c(se_i)$ for all $x \in N$, all $i$ and $|s| < \varepsilon'$, where $\pi_c$ is the collapse map for $b$ and $c > 0$, then the north pole is a regular value of $k$ and the Pontryagin manifold of $k$ there with basis $c\,b$ is exactly $(N,v)$.

    (hk : letI := hM.chartedSpace; haveI := hM.isManifold;

What differs

  • Milnor needs only that $df_x$ is an isomorphism on the normal space; the formal statement computes the whole kernel of the ambient derivative $A_x$, an object the book does not have.
  • The count: $A_x$ is onto $\mathbb R^p$, so its kernel has dimension $K + 1 - p$, while $T_xN$ and $(T_xM)^\perp$ meet only in $0$ and have dimensions $n$ and $K + 1 - m$.
  • The framing is read off the behaviour of the map along the lines $s \mapsto r(x + sv_i(x))$, which is all that the construction of Theorem C controls.
  • The corollary identifies the Pontryagin manifold of the map built for Theorem C, and is reused unchanged at both ends of a framed cobordism.
V7.9

Theorem C of §7: every framed submanifold occurs

restated
basis realised up to a factor $c > 0$collapse map need not be injectiveregular value is the north pole

Milnor §7, Theorem C · p. 43

Any compact framed submanifold $(N,\mathfrak w)$ of codimension $p$ in $M$ occurs as the Pontryagin manifold of some smooth mapping $f : M \to S^p$. The proof takes a product representation $g : N \times \mathbb R^p \to V \subseteq M$ and the projection $\pi : V \to \mathbb R^p$ with $\pi(g(x,y)) = y$, for which $0$ is a regular value and $\pi^{-1}(0)$ is precisely $N$ with its given framing. It then chooses a smooth $\varphi : \mathbb R^p \to S^p$ carrying every $x$ with $\|x\| \ge 1$ to a base point $s_0$ and the open unit ball diffeomorphically onto $S^p - s_0$, and sets $f = \varphi \circ \pi$ on $V$ and $f = s_0$ off $V$, with regular value $\varphi(0)$.

That this $f$ is smooth, and that $f^{-1}(\varphi(0)) = \pi^{-1}(0)$ is the framed manifold $N$, is asserted in a line.

Proved in Lean

For every framed submanifold $(N,v)$ of $M$ of dimension $n$ and codimension $p$ and every linearly independent $b$ in $\mathbb R^p$ there are a smooth $f : M \to S^p$ with the north pole a regular value and a constant $c > 0$ such that $(f^{-1}(\mathrm{north}), f^*(c\,b)) = (N,v)$. The map is $f(G(x,s)) = \pi(cAs)$ on the product neighbourhood and the south pole elsewhere, where $\pi$ is one fixed smooth map $\mathbb R^p \to S^p$, equal to the north pole only at $0$ and to the south pole for $|t| \ge \tfrac12$, with $d\pi_0$ bijective, and $A = (d\pi_0)^{-1}B$ with $Be_i = b_i$. The case $N = \emptyset$ is handled separately by the constant map.

Milnor/Chapter7/ThmB.lean, line 69  ·  Milnor.Framed.exists_map_of_framedSub

theorem exists_map_of_framedSub (F : FramedSub hM n p) (b : Fin p → EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin p))
    (hb : LinearIndependent ℝ b) :
    letI := hM.chartedSpace; haveI := hM.isManifold;

What differs

  • Milnor's $\varphi$ maps the open unit ball diffeomorphically onto the punctured sphere; the formal $\pi$ is one fixed explicit map which is never required to be injective.
  • The formal conclusion names the basis: the framing is realized for $c\,b$, not for $b$, because the fixed $\pi$ must be rescaled into the ball $B_{\varepsilon/2}$ of the tube.
  • Milnor parametrizes his product neighbourhood by all of $\mathbb R^p$, which removes the factor $c$; since $c\,b$ and $b$ give the same orientation, Lemma 1 absorbs it.
  • Smoothness off the tube is proved by a closed-core argument, $f$ being the south pole outside a compact subset of $V$, where Milnor writes one line.
V7.10

Lemma 4, and the map of a framed cobordism

stronger
framings proportional, not equalfirst-order estimate, not Taylorcobordism map stated separately

Milnor §7, Lemma 4 · p. 47

Let $f, g : M \to S^p$ be smooth maps with a common regular value $y$. If the framed manifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^*v)$ is equal to $(g^{-1}(y), g^*v)$, then $f$ is smoothly homotopic to $g$. Milnor first disposes of the case where $f$ and $g$ already agree on a neighbourhood of $N = f^{-1}(y)$, by a straight-line homotopy in stereographic coordinates from the antipode of $y$; then he deforms $f$ until it agrees with $g$ near $N$, using a second-order Taylor bound to keep $F(x,u)$ and $G(x,u)$ in one open half-space so that no new point is carried to $y$.

The homotopy that realizes a framed cobordism is never stated as a lemma: in the proof of Theorem B (p. 49) Milnor says only that an argument analogous to his proof of Theorem C constructs it.

Proved in Lean

Let $n + p = m$, let $f, g : M \to S^p$ be smooth with a common regular value $y$, let $b$ be a linearly independent $p$-tuple in $\mathbb R^p$ and $c > 0$. If the Pontryagin manifold of $f$ at $y$ for the basis $b$ equals that of $g$ at $y$ for the basis $c\,b$, then $f$ and $g$ are smoothly homotopic. The engine is a deformation lemma: for $N$ nonempty with product neighbourhood $G$ of radius $\varepsilon$, and $k$ smooth with $k^{-1}(y) = N$ and extended derivative carrying every framing vector $v_i(x)$ to $c\,b_i$, there are $0 < \delta \le \varepsilon$ and a smooth $H : M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ with $H(\cdot, 0) = k$, with $H(G(x,s), 1) = \varphi_y^{-1}(\varphi_y(y) + \sum_i s_i b_i)$ for $|s| < \delta$, and with $H(z, 1) \ne y$ for every $z \notin N$. Separately, for a framed cobordism between $F_0$ and $F_1$ and any linearly independent $b$, there are a smooth $H : M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ and a $c > 0$ such that the north pole is a regular value of $H(\cdot, 0)$ and of $H(\cdot, 1)$ and their Pontryagin manifolds there, for the common basis $c\,b$, are $F_0$ and $F_1$.

theorem smoothHomotopic_of_pontryagin_eq (hnp : n + p = m) {f g : M → Sph p}
    (hf : letI := hM.chartedSpace; haveI := hM.isManifold;

What differs

  • Milnor's Lemma 4 asks that the two framed manifolds be equal; the formal statement asks only that $f^*b$ and $g^*(c\,b)$ agree for some $c > 0$, so it contains his case $c = 1$.
  • Milnor deforms $f$ onto $g$ with a second-order Taylor bound; the formal proof deforms each map separately onto the standard map $G(x,s) \mapsto \varphi_y^{-1}(\varphi_y(y) + \sum_i s_i b_i)$.
  • The deformation lemma controls the preimage of $y$ only at time $1$; Milnor's remark that no new point reaches $y$ during the deformation is not in the formal statement and is not used.
  • The map of a cobordism is a separate statement: it is built on $S \cap (M \times (-3,4))$, and time is clamped so that the result is smooth on all of $M \times \mathbb R$.
V7.11

Theorem B: framed cobordant gives homotopic

narrower
$p \ge 1$ assumedhomotopy smooth on $M \times \mathbb R$regular values may differ

Milnor §7, Theorem B · p. 42

Two mappings $M \to S^p$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if their associated Pontryagin manifolds are framed cobordant. The half proved on p. 49 is the converse: given a framed cobordism $(X, w)$ between $f^{-1}(y)$ and $g^{-1}(y)$, an argument like the proof of Theorem C produces $F : M \times [0,1] \to S^p$ whose Pontryagin manifold is exactly $(X, w)$; its two ends then have the same Pontryagin manifolds as $f$ and $g$, and Lemma 4 finishes.

Milnor works with a single regular value $y$ common to $f$ and $g$, which Theorem A lets him arrange, and with a positively oriented basis $v$ of $T(S^p)_y$ fixed once and for all.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty submanifold of dimension $m$, let $n + p = m$ and $p \ge 1$. Let $f, g : M \to S^p$ be smooth, let $y$ be a regular value of $f$ and $y'$ one of $g$, and let $e$ and $e'$ be bases of $\mathbb R^p$ whose orientations are those of $S^p$ at $y$ and at $y'$. If $(f^{-1}(y), f^*e)$ and $(g^{-1}(y'), g^*e')$ are framed cobordant, then $f$ and $g$ are smoothly homotopic. Smooth homotopy here means a smooth map $M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ restricting to $f$ at $t = 0$ and to $g$ at $t = 1$; it is proved reflexive, symmetric and transitive, transitivity by making both homotopies constant near their ends before gluing.

Milnor/Chapter7/ThmAconv.lean, line 109  ·  Milnor.Framed.smoothHomotopic_of_framedCobordant

theorem smoothHomotopic_of_framedCobordant (hnp : n + p = m) (hp : 1 ≤ p) {f g : M → Sph p}
    (hf : letI := hM.chartedSpace; haveI := hM.isManifold;

Milnor/Chapter7/Homotopy.lean, line 25  ·  Milnor.SmoothHomotopic

Milnor/Chapter7/Homotopy.lean, line 57  ·  Milnor.SmoothHomotopic.trans

What differs

  • The formal statement assumes $p \ge 1$, used only to rotate a regular value to the north pole, where a unit vector orthogonal to $y$ is needed; the case $p = 0$ is not covered.
  • A smooth homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$ with the two maps at $t = 0$ and $t = 1$, not a map on $M \times [0,1]$; the relation is proved to be an equivalence relation.
  • The two maps are allowed different regular values $y$ and $y'$, each with its own positive basis; Milnor first arranges a common $y$ by Theorem A.
  • Positivity of the two bases is a named hypothesis, as in Milnor's definition of the Pontryagin manifold, and $M$ is a compact submanifold of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ given by slice charts.
V7.12

Framed 0-manifolds: signs and the signed count

hypotheses explicit
finiteness of the point set provedsign read in the chart at $x$$m \ge 1$ assumed

Milnor §7, p. 50

Let $M$ be connected and oriented of dimension $m = p$. A framed submanifold of codimension $p$ is then just a finite set of points with a preferred basis at each, and $\operatorname{sgn}(x)$ is $+1$ or $-1$ according as that basis gives the right or the wrong orientation. Milnor says it is clear that $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$ equals the degree of the associated map $M \to S^m$.

He states this in one sentence, with no proof, and does not say that finiteness of the point set needs an argument.

Proved in Lean

A compact $0$-dimensional slice submanifold of any smooth manifold is finite, so a framed $0$-manifold of $M$ is a finite set $N$ carrying at each $x$ a frame $v(x) = (v_1(x), \dots, v_m(x))$ of $T_xM$. Relative to an orientation $o$ of $M$, the sign of $v(x)$ is the sign of the linear map carrying the standard basis of $\mathbb R^m$ to the chart coordinates of the frame at $x$, read from the standard orientation of $\mathbb R^m$ to $o_x$; for a frame it is $\pm 1$, and the signed count of $(N, v)$ is the sum of these signs. For $m \ge 1$, $f : M \to S^m$ smooth, $y$ a regular value of $f$, and $e$ a basis of $\mathbb R^m$ whose orientation is that of $S^m$ at $y$, the signed count of the Pontryagin manifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^*e)$ is $\deg f$.

theorem count_pontryagin (hm : 0 < m)
    (oM : letI := hM.chartedSpace; Orient 𝓘(ℝ, EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin m)) M m)
