What differs

  • Milnor calls the identity clear; the formal proof shows that $df_x$ carries the Pontryagin frame at $x$ to the positive basis $e$, so the two signs multiply to $+1$, and sums over $f^{-1}(y)$.
  • That a compact framed $0$-manifold is finite is proved, from a slice chart at each point whose domain meets it only there; Milnor takes it for granted.
  • The sign of a frame at $x$ is defined through the chart coordinates of its vectors, compared with $o_x$; Milnor speaks of the basis giving the right or the wrong orientation.
  • $m \ge 1$ is assumed, so that $S^m$ is connected and the degree may be computed at any regular value; Milnor's next clause, on the framed cobordism class, is V7.16.
V7.13

Moving a framed point inside a chart ball

not in the book
no statement in the bookclosed $R$-ball inside the chart imagechart signs compared, no orientation

Milnor §7, p. 50

The book has no statement of this kind. Moving a framed point is the first of the steps hidden in Milnor's single clause that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$; the picture he gives, in the non-orientable remark just below, is of a point slid "around $M$ in a closed loop" carrying its basis with it.

Connectedness of $M$ is the only property he invokes for such motions.

Proved in Lean

Let $m \ge 1$, let $M$ be compact and nonempty, and let $(N, v)$ be a framed $0$-manifold of $M$. No orientation of $M$ occurs. Let $x_c \in M$ with chart $\varphi$ centred there, let $R > 0$ be such that the closed ball of radius $R$ about $\varphi(x_c)$ lies in the chart image, and write $U_r$ for the chart ball of radius $r$ about $x_c$. (i) The move: let $x_0 \in N$ lie in $U_{R/4}$ and be the only point of $N$ in $\overline{U_R}$, let $x_1 \in U_{R/4}$, and let $v_1$ be a tangent independent frame at $x_1$ whose chart sign relative to the hub $x_c$ (V7.20) is that of the frame of $(N, v)$ at $x_0$ — an equality of two determinant signs read in that one chart, in which no orientation appears. Then $(N, v)$ is framed cobordant to the framed $0$-manifold on $(N \setminus \{x_0\}) \cup \{x_1\}$ carrying $v_1$ at $x_1$ and $v$ elsewhere. (ii) The ball: around any point $y$ outside $N \setminus \{x_0\}$, inside any open set containing it, there is such an $R$ — the good-ball lemma, itself orientation-free — and then any point of $U_{R/4}$ may be moved to any other with any frame of the same chart sign relative to $y$. (iii) The oriented readings, which stand beside these as corollaries: on a ball shrunk so that the chart preserves an orientation $o$ on it, equality of chart signs is equality of the frame signs of V7.12, which gives the move in oriented form; and reach, that every point of the connected component of $x_0$ in $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x_0\})$ is attained with any frame of the same frame sign, which is kept oriented, being the one statement of the local theory whose sign is compared across a whole component.

What differs

  • The book states no move: one clause at the end of §7 covers the whole induction, and the picture it suggests is a point sliding along a path in $M$ carrying its basis.
  • The formal statement needs the chart image at $x_c$ to contain the closed ball of radius $R$ about $\varphi(x_c)$, so that $\overline{U_R}$ stays inside the chart and the glued map is smooth.
  • It no longer needs $\varphi$ to preserve orientation on $U_R$, nor an orientation of $M$ at all, so neither is a difference from the book any more: the two frames are compared by their chart signs at the hub $x_c$, the determinant signs of the frames read in that one chart. All the old positivity hypothesis was ever used for is that $d\varphi_z$ is invertible, and a chart derivative is invertible at every point of its domain.
  • With it went two hypotheses from each of six theorems in the glued-homotopy files, the orientation of $M$ and that positivity, so that those files now mention no orientation at all; and the lemma asserting that the derivative of a glued slice is bijective, whose hypothesis that the chart preserves orientation was spurious, was deleted in favour of the general fact.
  • Both $x_0$ and $x_1$ must lie in $U_{R/4}$, and no point of $N$ other than $x_0$ in $\overline{U_R}$; paths are replaced by an open-and-closed argument on the reachable points.
V7.14

Cancelling two framed points of opposite sign

not in the book
no statement in the bookstandard frames of chart signs $\pm 1$no orientation, no positivity

Milnor §7, p. 50

The book has no statement of this kind either. Cancelling a pair of points of opposite sign is the second step hidden in Milnor's clause that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$; the geometry behind it is the U-shaped arc of a cobordism whose two ends are preimages of opposite sign, the picture already used for the degree in §5.

Proved in Lean

Let $m \ge 1$, let $M$ be compact and nonempty, and let $\varphi$, $x_c$, $R$ and $U_R$ be as in V7.13: the closed $R$-ball about $\varphi(x_c)$ inside the chart image. No orientation of $M$, and no positivity hypothesis on the chart. Let $w \in \mathbb R^m$ be a unit vector, $c_0$ a point with $|c_0 - \varphi(x_c)| < R/8$, and $a > 0$ with $8a \le R$; let $x_1, x_2 \in N$ lie in the source of $\varphi$ with $\varphi(x_1) = c_0 + a w$ and $\varphi(x_2) = c_0 - a w$, and let no other point of $N$ lie in $\overline{U_R}$. (i) The cancellation: the framed $0$-manifold on $N$ that agrees with $v$ off $\{x_1, x_2\}$ and carries at $x_1$ and $x_2$ the standard frames of the cancelling map, of chart signs $1$ and $-1$ relative to the hub $x_c$ (V7.20), is framed cobordant to the framed $0$-manifold on $N \setminus \{x_1, x_2\}$ with the same frames elsewhere. (ii) The sign at a zero: at the zero $\varphi^{-1}(c_0 + l w)$ of the slice at time $t$, where $l^2 = a^2 \rho(t)$, that frame has chart sign $\operatorname{sgn} l$ — flatly the sign of the signed distance along $w$, where the oriented version carried a factor read off the orientation. (iii) The oriented reading, a corollary beside them, and what the signed cancellation machinery consumes: on a ball on which the chart at $x_c$ preserves an orientation $o$, the two frame signs of V7.12 are $\varepsilon$ and $-\varepsilon$, with $\varepsilon$ the sign of the identity of $\mathbb R^m$ from $o_{x_c}$ to the standard orientation — the orientation factor at the centre of the ball. (iv) A companion, still oriented, cancels an arbitrary pair: if $z \in N$ has the frame sign opposite to that of $x_0 \in N$ and lies in the closure of the component of $x_0$ in $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x_0\})$, then $(N, v)$ is framed cobordant to $N \setminus \{x_0, z\}$ with the frames $v$.

What differs

  • The book states nothing here; cancelling a pair of opposite signs is one step of the induction left to the reader at the end of §7.
  • The cobordism is between $(N, v)$ with the standard frames installed at $x_1, x_2$ and $N \setminus \{x_1, x_2\}$, not between $(N, v)$ itself and the two points removed, so two frame changes come first.
  • Those frames no longer have signs $\pm\varepsilon$ for a factor $\varepsilon$ read off an orientation: their chart signs are $1$ and $-1$ on the nose, and the statement carrying the proof needs neither an orientation of $M$ nor the hypothesis that $\varphi$ preserve one on $U_R$, so neither is a difference from the book any more. The factor $\varepsilon$ survives only in the oriented corollary, where it is the orientation factor at the centre of the ball, and the positivity is the one hypothesis that corollary still carries.
  • The positivity was doing no work: regularity of the north pole for the cancelling homotopy and for its slices needs only that a chart derivative be invertible, which holds at every point of the chart's domain. So the lemma whose hypothesis was that the chart preserves orientation was spurious and is gone, and six theorems of the two glued-homotopy files lost two hypotheses each (V7.13).
  • The points must sit at $\varphi^{-1}(c_0 \pm a w)$ with $|c_0 - \varphi(x_c)| < R/8$ and $8a \le R$; the zeros at time $t$ are $\{\lambda w : \lambda^2 = a^2 \rho(t)\}$, with $\rho$ from $1$ to $-1$.
V7.15

The neighbour lemma and chains of neighbours

not in the book
no statement in the bookno sign, no orientation, no compactnesschains needed when $m = 1$

Milnor §7, p. 50

There is no such lemma in the book. On p. 50 Milnor lets $M$ be connected and, having identified $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$ with the degree, remarks in a single clause that the framed cobordism class of the $0$-manifold is determined by that integer; connectedness is all he invokes for moving points about.

For $m \ge 2$ the complement of a finite set is still connected and any point can be moved onto any other, so nothing more is needed; the difficulty is invisible until $m = 1$.

Proved in Lean

Let $X$ be a connected, locally connected $T_1$ space, let $N \subseteq X$ be finite, and write $C(x)$ for the connected component of $x$ in $X \setminus (N \setminus \{x\})$. Assume every $x \in N$ has an open set $J$ with $x \in J$, $J \cap N \subseteq \{x\}$ and $J \setminus \{x\} \subseteq C(x)$. Then for every $B \subseteq N$ with $B$ and $N \setminus B$ nonempty there are $x \in B$ and $z \in N \setminus B$ with $z \in \overline{C(x)}$, that is, $x$ has a neighbour in the other part. The hypothesis on $J$ holds for small chart balls of $M$, each point of such a ball being joined to the centre by a segment read in the chart, so the lemma applies to every finite subset of a connected $M$, compact or not. Nothing in it mentions a frame, a sign or an orientation: it is the one of the three moves of the endgame that the restatement in chart-sign form left untouched, and one statement serves both endgames — the oriented proof sorts the points of $N$ by the sign of their frames before applying it (V7.16), the sign-free proof hands it the partition $\{x\} \sqcup (N \setminus \{x\})$, there being no signs to sort by (V7.24). The same argument gives chains: for $x_0 \in B$ there is a sequence $x_0 = u_0, \dots, u_k$ of consecutive neighbours with $u_0, \dots, u_{k-1} \in B$ and $u_k \in N \setminus B$.

What differs

  • This is the formal content of Milnor's appeal to the connectedness of $M$; the book states no lemma of the kind.
  • It is proved for an abstract connected, locally connected $T_1$ space with a segment property at each point of $N$; for $M$ that property comes from a chart ball meeting $N$ only in its centre, and asks nothing of the chart but that the closed ball of the radius lie in its image.
  • Where the two moves of V7.13 and V7.14 had to be restated with the chart sign, this lemma needed no restating, and the row lists no orientation among its differences from the book: it carries no frame, no sign, no orientation, no compactness and no dimension hypothesis. What the sign-free endgame deletes is the sorting of the points by sign that precedes the application (V7.24), not anything in the lemma.
  • Chains are needed because for $m = 1$ points cannot pass each other: a point may reach its target only through points already in place, which are then moved one step each.
  • For $m \ge 2$ every point of $N$ is a neighbour of every other and every chain has length one, the component $C(x)$ being then the whole of $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x\})$ (V7.22); the formal proof is uniform in the dimension and never uses this.
V7.16

Equal signed counts give framed cobordant 0-manifolds

hypotheses explicit
$m \ge 1$ assumedproof left to the reader in the bookconnectedness used via neighbours

Milnor §7, p. 50

With $M$ connected, oriented and of dimension $m = p$, Milnor writes that it is "not difficult to see" that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is uniquely determined by the integer $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$, and passes at once to the Hopf theorem.

This is the one assertion of §7 for which the book gives no argument at all.

Proved in Lean

Let $m \ge 1$ and let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be compact, nonempty, connected and oriented by $o$. Two framed $0$-manifolds of $M$ with the same signed count are framed cobordant. The proof is an induction on the total number of points of the two: while one side carries both signs, the neighbour lemma produces a neighbouring pair of opposite signs and V7.14 removes it, leaving the count unchanged; once each side has a single sign, equal counts force the same sign and the same number of points, the frames at common points are matched by frame changes, and the remaining points of one side are moved onto those of the other along shortest chains of neighbours.

What differs

  • Milnor asserts this in a single clause and gives no argument; the formal proof is an induction on the total number of points of the two framed $0$-manifolds.
  • The formal statement names $m \ge 1$ and $M$ compact, nonempty, connected and oriented; all but $m \ge 1$ are already in force in Milnor's paragraph.
  • Connectedness enters only through the neighbour lemma and the chain lemma of V7.15, never as a way of joining two points by a path in $M$.
  • Cancelling a neighbouring pair of opposite signs leaves the count unchanged and drops the point count by two; the points left over are gathered along shortest chains.
V7.17

The Hopf theorem, and maps of every degree

restated
converse is Theorem B of §5homotopy means smooth homotopydegrees realized without connectedness

Milnor §7, Theorem of Hopf · p. 50

If $M$ is connected, oriented and boundaryless, then two maps $M \to S^m$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if they have the same degree. Milnor obtains it by putting together the identity $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x) = \deg$, the assertion that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by that integer, and Theorem B. He adds that for non-orientable $M$ an easy argument classifies the maps by the degree modulo $2$.

He states only the classification; that every integer occurs as a degree is not part of it.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty submanifold of dimension $m \ge 1$, oriented by $o$, with $S^m$ carrying the orientation of §5. If $M$ is connected, two smooth maps $M \to S^m$ with the same degree are smoothly homotopic, smooth homotopy meaning a smooth map $M \times \mathbb R \to S^m$ restricting to the two maps at $t = 0$ and $t = 1$; the converse is Theorem B of §5. Separately, and without assuming $M$ connected, every integer $d$ is the degree of a smooth map $M \to S^m$: starting from the empty framed $0$-manifold, $|d|$ points with frames of the sign of $d$ are added one at a time, which needs $M$ to be infinite, and Theorem C realizes the result as a Pontryagin manifold.

V7.18

Orientability of a manifold, and transport of an orientation

not in the book
no orientability in the booka hop records which chart it usesexactly two orientations if connected

Milnor §7, p. 50

The book defines an oriented manifold (§5, p. 26, V5.0) but never asks whether an orientation exists, and orientability itself is nowhere defined: the word appears in the remark at the end of §7, that for a connected non-orientable $M$ the maps to $S^m$ are classified by the degree modulo $2$. What Milnor relies on instead is the picture of a frame carried around a closed loop in $M$ and coming back reversed, and the fact that his manifolds are subsets of Euclidean space, where every tangent space is a subspace of one fixed $\mathbb R^k$.

Nothing in the book compares the orientations at two points along a chain of charts; that comparison is the content of this row.

Proved in Lean

Neither Mathlib nor the project had orientability in any form. Let $M$ be modelled on a finite-dimensional real $E$ with $\dim E = m$. The derivative at $y$ of the chart centred at $x$ is a linear isomorphism, so exactly one orientation of the model space at $y$ is carried by it to a given orientation at $x$: this is the transport through that chart, an involution which anticommutes with reversal; and an orientation of $M$ in the sense of V5.0 is exactly a family of orientations which near every point is the transport of its value at that point. A hop from $x$ to $y$ is a single chart whose source contains both; a chain is a finite sequence of hops; chains compose and reverse, transport along a composite is the composite of the transports, and transport along the reverse undoes the chain; in a connected manifold any two points are joined by a chain, the reachable set being open and closed. A chain-loop reverses if its transport carries some orientation at the base point to the opposite one, and which of the two is tested is immaterial. Half of the dichotomy follows: if no chain-loop at a point of a connected $M$ reverses, then $M$ is orientable; contrapositively, a connected non-orientable manifold carries at every point a chain-loop reversing every orientation there. Finally, a connected orientable manifold has exactly two orientations, a given one and its pointwise reverse.

    [ChartedSpace H M] (m : ℕ) : Prop := …

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book: Milnor's manifolds are subsets of Euclidean space, orientability is never defined for them, and the transport of an orientation along a chain of charts has to be built before the remark of p. 50 can be stated at all.
  • A hop has to record which chart it travels through. The naive relation "some chart contains both points" does not induce a symmetric transport, and the obvious symmetric repair is wrong, precisely because two charts can disagree on a disconnected overlap.
  • Only half of the dichotomy is unconditional at this level. The converse is false for an orientation in the sense of V5.0, whose compatibility is a condition only near each point; it holds for the stronger notion of an orientation coherent with each chart on the whole of that chart's source, and the counterexample is recorded in the source: on $\mathbb R$ with the atlas made of the identity chart and the chart that is $x$ on $(-1, 0)$ and $3 - x$ on $(1, 2)$, the orientation that is standard except reversed on $(1, 2)$ satisfies V5.0's condition, and the loop out through the second chart and back through the first reverses, although $\mathbb R$ is orientable. V7.19 removes the defect.
  • Nothing is assumed of $M$ beyond what each statement says: no boundarylessness, no Hausdorffness, no second countability, no positive dimension, and $M$ may be empty; connectedness appears as preconnectedness and only where it is stated.
V7.19

Chart balls, and the exact dichotomy

not in the book
round balls, where chart sources failevery orientation is ball-coherentthe converse holds, with no extra notion

Milnor §7, p. 50

For the remark at the end of §7 Milnor needs one alternative of a dichotomy he does not state: a connected manifold either carries a coherent choice of orientations, or admits a closed loop that brings an orientation back reversed, in which case a framed point slid around the loop returns with the sign of its frame changed. He takes the second alternative as read for a non-orientable $M$, and the loops he has in mind are the paths along which points are slid in the proof of the Hopf theorem.

The reversing loop is the one geometric input of the mod 2 remark; the rest of §7 is orientation-free.

Proved in Lean

A chart ball is the preimage under the chart centred at $c$ of a round ball about the chart image of $c$; when that ball lies in the target of the chart, the chart ball is connected, which is the one property a chart source lacks. A ball hop is a hop through such a ball containing both its endpoints, and a ball chain a chain of those; the whole transport algebra of V7.18 applies verbatim, only the predicate having to be shown closed under the constructions. The key lemma is that every orientation of $M$ is ball-coherent: transport along a ball hop carries the orientation at one end to the orientation at the other. On a chart ball about $c$ the sign of the orientation at $z$ against the one at $c$, read through the chart derivative, takes only the values $\pm 1$, is locally constant by the continuity field of V5.0, and equals $1$ at $c$; a locally constant function on a connected set is constant. Hence the exact dichotomy: for connected $M$ and any base point, $M$ is orientable if and only if no loop of ball hops at that point reverses; and a connected non-orientable $M$ carries at every point a loop of ball hops reversing every orientation there, which is the form the mod 2 endgame uses.

What differs

  • No statement of this kind in the book: it is what the remark of p. 50 presupposes, and the mod 2 endgame consumes it in the form "at every point outside a finite set there is a reversing loop of balls" (V7.26).
  • Round balls make the dichotomy exact, where chains of charts give only one half (V7.18): for a chart source the key lemma is false, and that is exactly the counterexample recorded there.
  • The key lemma is the one step with no chart-level counterpart, and it is where the continuity field of an orientation (V5.0) earns its keep; it needs a connected chart domain, which a round ball is and a chart source need not be.
  • Ball chains are chart chains together with a predicate, not a second inductive type, so composition, reversal, transport and the notion of a reversing loop are literally the chart-level ones and no algebra is duplicated.
  • Boundarylessness of the model enters twice, in both cases because a chart target must be open: a ball inside the target has to exist at all, which every statement producing a ball hop needs, and the sign of a chart derivative has to be locally constant, which the key lemma needs. Everything about a chart ball that is given rather than produced needs it not, and Hausdorffness, second countability and positivity of the dimension are needed nowhere.
V7.20

The chart sign of a frame, and the bridge to the oriented sign

not in the book
a sign with no orientation in itMilnor's sign, times a point factorframes of both signs at every point

Milnor §7, p. 50

The remark at the end of §7 is justified in a single clause: a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged. This row carries out those five words. The sign Milnor means is the one he defines a few lines earlier (V7.12): $\operatorname{sgn}(x)$ is $+1$ or $-1$ according as the frame at $x$ gives the right or the wrong orientation of $M$. On the manifolds of the remark there is no right orientation, so that sign is not defined, and the book offers no substitute: the clause says the sign changes without saying what it is measured against.

A sign of a frame that mentions no orientation, and the identity relating it to Milnor's sign where an orientation exists.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a slice submanifold of dimension $m$ as in V7.0, let $x_c$ and $x$ be points of it and $v$ a tuple of $m$ ambient vectors. The chart sign of $v$ at $x$ relative to the centre $x_c$ is the sign of the determinant of $v$ read in the chart centred at $x_c$ — the derivative at $x$ of that chart composed with the matrix of chart coordinates of the $v_i$; the self sign is the case $x_c = x$; the Jacobian sign is the sign of the determinant of the derivative at $x$ of the chart centred at $x_c$. None of the three mentions an orientation. For a tangent independent frame each is $1$ or $-1$ and squares to $1$ — the chart sign and the Jacobian sign once $x$ lies in the source of the chart, the self sign with no membership hypothesis at all — and the cocycle formula $\text{chart sign} = \text{Jacobian sign} \cdot \text{self sign}$ holds for all arguments, the Jacobian sign being $1$ at the centre. The bridge: for every orientation $o_M$ of $M$ in the sense of V5.0, the sign of V7.12 factors as $\operatorname{frameSign}(o_M, v, x) = \varepsilon(x) \cdot \text{self sign}(v, x)$, where $\varepsilon(x) = \pm 1$ depends only on the point and not on the frame. The sign of a frame against an orientation of the manifold is thus the determinant sign of the frame read in one chart, times a factor depending only on the point, and the factor cancels from every comparison of two such signs. On a chart ball, under the positivity hypothesis the oriented lemmas already carried, the same holds with the hub's factor, so equality of frame signs at two points of the ball is exactly equality of their chart signs relative to the hub. Two further statements, with no orientation and no case split on a global reference sign: for $0 < m$ there are tangent independent frames of self sign $1$ and of self sign $-1$ at every point of $M$; and given such a frame at $x$ and a second point $y$ in the source of the same chart, there is a frame at $y$ which is the same frame read in that chart, hence of the same chart sign, nothing being needed but the two memberships.

def chartSign (x_c x : M) (v : Fin m → EK) : ℤ := …

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book: the sign of a frame is defined there only against an orientation of $M$ (V7.12), which is exactly what a non-orientable $M$ lacks, so the sign the clause speaks of has to be built before the clause can be stated.
  • The bridge is what makes the rest of the chapter reusable rather than rewritten. Every local proof of §7 compares a product of two signs, and the point-dependent factor cancels from such a product; so V7.13 to V7.15 were restated in chart-sign form, six theorems losing two hypotheses each, and the oriented statements sit beside the sign-free ones as short corollaries, no proof being duplicated. A lemma whose hypothesis "the chart preserves orientation" turned out to be spurious was deleted in favour of the general one, and the sign of the frame at a zero of the cancelling map is now flatly the sign of the signed distance, where before it carried a factor depending on the orientation.
  • The self sign is a better primitive than the plan expected: it needs no chart-membership hypothesis at all, which is what makes it usable in the clopen arguments that subdivide a hop (V7.23).
  • Frames of both signs exist at every point with no case split on a global reference sign, where the oriented version had to compare against the orientation; the proof of that statement collapses from sixty-five lines to one.
V7.21

The sign of a chain, the transition cocycle, and chains that avoid a set

not in the book
reversing means chain sign $-1$jointly locally constant, singly notavoidance via the closed chart ball

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification turns on an orientation-reversing loop: a framed point carried around one comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged. This row carries out the loop. What reversing is, Milnor leaves to the picture of a frame taken around a closed loop in $M$ and coming back the other way; and the loop must be one the point can be slid along without ever running into the other points of the framed set, which the clause does not mention, those points not being in the picture.

Reversal becomes a sign attached to the loop, and the loops can be confined to a region chosen in advance.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a manifold modelled on a finite-dimensional real $E$ with $\dim E = m$. (i) The dictionary. The Jacobian sign at $z$ of the chart centred at $c$ is the sign of the determinant of the derivative there; it is $\pm 1$ for $z$ in the source of that chart, is $1$ at $c$, and transport of an orientation across the chart (V7.18) is multiplication by it. The sign of a hop is the product of the two Jacobian signs of its chart at the two endpoints, and the sign of a chain the product of the signs of its hops. The sign of a composite is the product of the signs, a chain and its reverse have the same sign, transport along a chain is multiplication by its sign, and a chain-loop reverses exactly when its sign is $-1$ — so the dichotomy of V7.19 becomes arithmetic: a connected $M$ is orientable if and only if every loop of balls at a base point has sign $1$, and a connected non-orientable $M$ carries a loop of balls of sign $-1$ at every point. All of this holds for arbitrary chart chains, not only ball ones, and none of it needs a boundaryless model. (ii) The transition cocycle lemma. For two centres $p$, $q$ and a point $y$ in both their sources, the product of the two Jacobian signs at $z$ is constant for $z$ near $y$. Neither factor is locally constant, each reading a fixed chart through the moving chart at $z$; in the product the moving chart cancels and what is left is the determinant sign of the fixed transition map $\varphi_p \circ \varphi_q^{-1}$, which is smooth on an open set, hence has continuous invertible derivative and locally constant determinant sign. (iii) Avoidance. A ball hop runs inside a set $C$ when the closed ball of its radius lies in the target of its chart and the resulting closed chart ball is contained in $C$; a chain is a ball chain inside $C$ when each of its hops is, again a predicate on the chains of V7.18 and not a second inductive type. For $C$ open and preconnected, any two points of $C$ are joined by a ball chain inside $C$, by the clopen argument of V7.19 run inside $C$; and if $P$ is disjoint from $C$ such a chain avoids $P$ with room to spare, the closed chart ball of each of its hops missing $P$.

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book: reversal is a picture there, and the dictionary is what makes it a quantity — after it, "this loop reverses" is the arithmetic statement that a product of $\pm 1$'s is $-1$, and the dichotomy of V7.19 can be used by calculation rather than by transporting orientations by hand.
  • The dictionary costs nothing extra in generality: a hop already carries the two membership facts that make its two Jacobian signs $\pm 1$, so everything is proved for arbitrary chart chains and nothing has to be repeated for ball chains, which are chart chains satisfying a predicate.
  • The cocycle lemma is the one genuinely analytic step in the whole orientation theory, and it has no counterpart at any point of the book. It is used twice: to extend a family of orientations across a puncture (V7.22), and to see that the sign of a chain does not notice a hop being subdivided (V7.23).
  • Two hypotheses came out weaker than the plan asked: the avoided set need not be closed, only disjoint from the region, and the region need only be preconnected, not connected. No local connectedness of $M$ and no hypothesis on the dimension is used, and $M$ may be empty.
  • One hypothesis had to be added, and the later rows must supply it: avoidance is phrased through the closed chart ball rather than the topological closure of the ball, because on a non-Hausdorff manifold the closure of a chart ball need not even lie in the source of its chart — on the line with two origins the closure of an interval about one origin contains the other. The closure form is given separately under Hausdorffness, which is free in this chapter, where the manifolds are subsets of a Euclidean space.
  • Boundarylessness of the model is assumed exactly where a chart target has to be open: to produce a ball inside that target at all, and to make the transition map smooth on an open set. The sign dictionary is free of it.
V7.22

The punctured manifold, and the punctured dichotomy

not in the book
$2 \le m$, and the hypothesis is sharporientations extend over puncturesno separation hypothesis: $T_1$ is free

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification — a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives — carries a side condition it never states, and that is this row. The point being slid is one of finitely many, and the loop must miss the others or the sliding would collide with them; Milnor's loop simply goes around $M$. Nothing in the book says that a non-orientable manifold still has a reversing loop once finitely many points have been deleted, and nothing says that the complement of a finite set is connected.

The dichotomy of V7.19 rerun on a punctured manifold, which is what makes the reversing loop available where the framed set leaves room for it.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a manifold modelled on a finite-dimensional real $E$ with $\dim E = m$ and a boundaryless model. For $2 \le m$: a chart ball minus any one point is path connected, hence connected; and the complement of a finite set $P$ in a connected $M$ is connected, nonempty already for $0 < m$, since a nonempty open subset of a manifold of positive dimension is infinite. Call a family of orientations of the tangent spaces ball-coherent on a set $C$ when transport along every ball hop running inside $C$ (V7.21) carries its value at one end of the hop to its value at the other; every orientation of $M$ is ball-coherent on every set (V7.19), and the extension theorem is the converse: for $2 \le m$ and $P$ finite, a family ball-coherent on the complement of $P$ agrees off $P$ with a transport section on the whole of $M$, hence with an orientation of $M$ in the sense of V5.0. Its value at a puncture $p$ is the value of the family at a point $y$ near $p$ transported back to $p$, well defined because that assignment is locally constant on a punctured ball about $p$ — which is the transition cocycle lemma of V7.21 — and because the punctured ball is connected. Hence the punctured dichotomy: a connected non-orientable $M$ of dimension at least two has, at every point $x_0$ outside a given finite set $P$, a loop of chart balls at $x_0$ each of whose closed chart balls misses $P$ and whose chain sign is $-1$. That is the hypothesis V7.23 to V7.25 take as given, in exactly this shape, and V7.26 discharges it once.

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book: avoiding the other points is not a matter of drawing the loop more carefully, but of rerunning the dichotomy of V7.19 on the complement of a finite set, which needs the point-set theorem that the complement is still connected and the orientation theorem that it is still non-orientable.
  • The hypothesis $2 \le m$ is sharp, not a convenience, and this row is the sole place in the development that needs it: for $m = 1$ a punctured chart ball is an interval minus an interior point, and $\mathbb R \setminus \{0\}$ is a disconnected complement of a finite set in a connected $1$-manifold; for $m = 0$ a punctured chart ball is empty. V7.26 no longer carries the hypothesis, however: since the classification of one-manifolds (V.A2) it is derived there from non-orientability, every connected one-manifold being orientable (V.A4). The statements about a chart ball that is given, with its ball inside the chart's target, need no boundarylessness; producing one does.
  • The extension theorem is where the cocycle lemma of V7.21 earns its keep. The value read at a nearby point involves two Jacobian signs which are separately discontinuous in that point and jointly locally constant, and it is the joint constancy, together with the connectedness of the punctured ball, that makes the value independent of the point it is read at.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong, twice and in the same direction. Well-definedness of the extension holds at every point, not only at the punctures, so the extension is one total function with no case split and the transport-section condition is checked at every point at once; and no separation hypothesis is needed anywhere, charts giving $T_1$ for free, which is all that the closedness of a finite set asks.
  • Two small facts had to be proved that Mathlib does not supply in usable form: that no point of a manifold of positive dimension is isolated, and that a locally constant function on a connected set is constant — Mathlib's version wants a discrete target, and the two orientations of a vector space carry no topology.
V7.23

The slide and the flip: Milnor's clause

not in the book
Milnor's clause, provedone hop is many movesthe loop is needed only where it starts

Milnor §7, p. 50

This row is Milnor's clause, and the only part of it he asserts outright: on p. 50, in the remark just after the Hopf theorem, a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged. He gives no construction. The sliding is the picture of §7, and its justification in the oriented case is the machinery of V7.13, whose every statement mentions an orientation of $M$.

The five other rows of this group are what it takes to state this one and to make its hypothesis available.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty slice submanifold of dimension $m$ with $0 < m$, and let $(N, v)$ be a framed $0$-manifold of $M$ (V7.0). (i) The slide: given a chain of chart balls from a point $x_0$ of $N$ to $y$, the closed chart ball of each of its hops missing every other point of $N$, the framed manifold $(N, v)$ is framed cobordant to one whose point set is $(N \setminus \{x_0\}) \cup \{y\}$, whose frames away from $x_0$ are those of $v$, and whose self sign at $y$ (V7.20) is the chain sign of the chain (V7.21) times the self sign at $x_0$. (ii) The flip: if the chain is a loop at $x_0$ of chain sign $-1$, the point set and every other frame are literally unchanged and the self sign at $x_0$ is reversed. (iii) Consequently: every point of the component of $x_0$ in $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x_0\})$ is reached with some frame, with no loop at all; and with a reversing loop at $x_0$ available, the frame there may be replaced by any tangent independent frame, and the point may be moved anywhere in that component carrying any tangent independent frame — which is V7.13 with the orientation and its sign hypothesis simply deleted. No orientation of $M$ occurs anywhere, and no hypothesis on the dimension beyond $0 < m$: connectedness and $2 \le m$ belong to V7.22, which produces the loop. The loop is carried as a hypothesis in exactly the form V7.22 produces it, so the two halves join with no glue, and Hausdorffness is never invoked.

What differs

  • This is the one row of the six with something in the book to compare, and the comparison is one clause against a file: what Milnor asserts is the flip, and the row is the slide of which the flip is the loop case, together with the bookkeeping that says what the sign of the frame does along the way.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong: a ball hop is too coarse to be performed by one move. A move (V7.13) needs its two points inside the ball of a quarter of a radius $R$ whose closed ball fits in the chart's target, while the endpoints of a ball hop need only lie in the ball itself and can be almost a full diameter apart, the target containing no ball four times as large. So each hop has to be subdivided, and the sign shown not to notice the subdivision: that needs an open-set form of the cocycle lemma of V7.21 and a change-of-hub lemma, and the invariant carried across a hop turns out to be the chart sign at the hop's own hub.
  • An improvement on the plan in the other direction: the sign-free reach lemma needs the reversing loop only at the point that moves, not at its destination. That is what lets the parity induction of V7.25 finish by carrying one point onto one point.
  • What the slide asserts about the frame is a self sign, not a sign against an orientation; there being no orientation to be had, the bookkeeping is the dictionary of V7.20 and V7.21 throughout, and the hypothesis relating the two frames that V7.13 carries has no analogue.
  • Hypotheses: $0 < m$, and compactness and nonemptiness of $M$, which the Theorem-B machinery behind the moves needs. No orientation, no connectedness, no $2 \le m$, no Hausdorffness — the closed chart ball of V7.21 is used in place of the closure of a ball, and each move gets the closure condition it needs from its own good ball.
V7.24

Cancelling any two neighbouring points, with no signs

not in the book
no condition on the two framesshorter than the signed versionthe case split disappears

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification ends and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged, and this row is the place where it does not. The oriented endgame cancels a point of sign $+1$ against a point of sign $-1$ (V7.14), sorting the points by sign to find such a pair (V7.15), and that is what makes the signed count the complete invariant; a framed point slid around a reversing loop comes back with its sign changed, so with no signs to be had the step has to become the cancellation of any two neighbouring points. Milnor does not remark on the difference.

In the book this is the sentence that the framed cobordism class is determined by the count; the mod $2$ version needs a different cancellation, not merely a different count.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty slice submanifold of dimension $m$ with $0 < m$, let $(N, v)$ be a framed $0$-manifold of $M$ as in V7.23, and let reversing ball loops be available at every point off every finite set — the conclusion of V7.22, carried here as a hypothesis. (i) Frames are free at two points: two framed $0$-manifolds with the same point set and the same frames away from two distinct points $u \ne z$ are framed cobordant, with no condition relating their frames at $u$ and at $z$. (ii) The sign-free cancel step: if $x_0 \ne z$ are points of $N$ and $z$ lies in the closure of the connected component of $x_0$ in $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x_0\})$, then $(N, v)$ is framed cobordant to the framed $0$-manifold with $x_0$ and $z$ deleted and the other frames unchanged — V7.14 with its opposite-sign hypothesis deleted outright. (iii) A neighbouring pair: if $N$ has two distinct points and $M$ is connected, then two of its points stand in that relation, which is V7.15 with the signs deleted; this last needs neither the loops nor compactness, only connectedness. No orientation occurs anywhere, and $2 \le m$ does not appear.

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book: "the rest applies unchanged" is the claim that this cancellation exists, and it is the one place where dropping the orientation is not a restatement but a different theorem — any two neighbours, rather than a plus and a minus.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong, and this is the point of Milnor's remark: it prescribed choosing, on arrival, a frame whose chart sign is the negative of the sign at the point to be cancelled against, and replacing the old case split by a new one. Neither is needed. The cancelling map supplies its own two frames, of chart signs $+1$ and $-1$, and "frames are free at two points" connects any configuration to that one, so the arriving frame may be arbitrary and the case split disappears rather than being replaced. The sign-free cancel step is therefore shorter than the signed one it replaces.
  • What is deleted is a hypothesis, not a case. V7.14 asks the two signs to be opposite, and the oriented endgame (V7.16) sorts the points of the framed set by sign before applying the neighbour lemma (V7.15), which carries no sign itself; here neither statement mentions a sign, and the neighbour lemma is applied to the partition $\{x\} \sqcup (N \setminus \{x\})$, which exists as soon as there are two points.
  • The reversing loop is carried in the uniform shape "at every point off every finite set", the finite set always being instantiated to the other points of the framed $0$-manifold, so that nothing here depends on how the loop is obtained and V7.26 can discharge the hypothesis once from V7.22.
V7.25

The count modulo 2, and the classification by parity

not in the book
parity is a complete invariantno gathering step in the inductionthe bridge ignores the framing

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification concludes that, a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop coming back with the sign of its frame changed, only the number of points modulo $2$ survives. This row carries out that conclusion. For an oriented $M$ Milnor writes on p. 50 that it is "not difficult to see" that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by the integer $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$ (V7.16), and that it is clear that this integer is the degree (V7.12); the mod $2$ statements are those two sentences with the integer replaced by the parity of the number of points, and neither is written down.

The invariant, that it is complete, and the bridge to the degree modulo $2$ of §4 (V4.7).

Proved in Lean

The count modulo $2$ of a framed $0$-manifold of $M$ is the cardinality of its point set read in $\mathbb Z/2$; no orientation occurs in the definition, in contrast with the signed count of V7.12. Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty slice submanifold of dimension $m$ with $0 < m$. (i) The classification: if $M$ is connected and reversing ball loops are available off every finite set (V7.22), two framed $0$-manifolds of $M$ with the same count modulo $2$ are framed cobordant. This is V7.16 with the signed count replaced by the parity, and it is Milnor's assertion for a non-orientable $M$. (ii) The bridge: for a smooth $f : M \to S^m$ with regular value $y$, and any linearly independent tuple of $\mathbb R^m$ as frame, the count modulo $2$ of the Pontryagin manifold of $f$ at $y$ (V7.1) is the degree of $f$ modulo $2$ (V4.7). Both sides are the parity of the number of points of $f^{-1}(y)$, so nothing about the framing enters; the bridge needs no loop and no connectedness of $M$, only its compactness and the preconnectedness of the sphere. The hypothesis $2 \le m$ appears in neither statement, being needed only to produce the loops.

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book: Milnor states the signed versions and leaves these to "the rest applies unchanged". The count is a different definition, mentioning no orientation, and the classification is a different induction.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong: the signed induction of V7.16 can cancel only while both signs occur, and needs a separate gathering argument to bring the remaining points of one sign together when they do not. Here there is no gathering step at all. The induction runs on the cruder condition "two or more points", cancels any two neighbours by V7.24, and stops when each side has at most one point, equal counts then forcing equal cardinalities; one point is carried onto one point by the reach lemma of V7.23, which is exactly where that row's improvement — the loop needed only at the point that moves — is used.
  • The bridge asks less than its oriented counterpart. The point set of the Pontryagin manifold is $f^{-1}(y)$ and the mod $2$ degree is the parity of its cardinality at any regular value, so the frame may be an arbitrary independent tuple, where the signed identity of V7.12 needs a positively oriented basis of the tangent space of the sphere at $y$.
  • The reversing loop is a hypothesis here as in V7.24, discharged only in V7.26; and the two statements rest on different hypotheses, connectedness of $M$ being used by the classification and not by the bridge.
V7.26

The mod 2 theorem: maps of a non-orientable manifold

restated
no dimension hypothesis$M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, compacthomotopy means smooth homotopy

Milnor §7, Theorem, p. 50

If $M$ is connected but non-orientable, two maps $M \to S^m$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if they have the same degree modulo $2$. Milnor states it in a remark just after the Hopf theorem, and gives the reason in one clause: a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives, and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged.

The degree modulo $2$ is the one of §4 (V4.7); with no orientation of $M$ there is no signed degree to be had.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a closed — compact and nonempty — connected non-orientable submanifold of dimension $m$, in the sense of V7.0 and with the manifold structure of Chapter 2, non-orientable in the sense of V7.18. Then two smooth maps $f, g : M \to S^m$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if they have the same degree modulo $2$, smooth homotopy being a smooth map $M \times \mathbb R \to S^m$ restricting to the two maps at $t = 0$ and $t = 1$. No dimension hypothesis is carried, and Milnor states none: the $2 \le m$ that the punctured stage needs (V7.22) is now derived from the non-orientability of a connected $M$ through the classification of one-manifolds (V.A2, V.A4). The easy half is Theorem A of §4 (V4.8) transcribed into that relation: it needs no orientation of any kind, no connectedness, and nothing of the dimension beyond $0 < m$, which is only what the mod 2 degree asks of its target. The substantial half is Milnor's remark, and runs through Sard (V3.3) for a regular value of each map; a positively oriented basis of the sphere at each — orientations of the target sphere, which the Pontryagin construction and the converse of Theorem B require, and which are the only orientations in the whole argument, the source carrying none and being assumed to have none; the two Pontryagin manifolds (V7.1); the parity bridge and the parity classification (V7.25), whose reversing loops are discharged here in one line from the punctured dichotomy (V7.22); and the converse half of Theorem B (V7.11), which is itself orientation-free.

theorem hopf_homotopic_mod2
    (hconn : letI := hM.chartedSpace; ConnectedSpace M)

Milnor/Chapter7/HopfMod2.lean, line 153  ·  Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_card_parity

theorem framedCobordant_of_card_parity
    (hconn : letI := hM.chartedSpace; ConnectedSpace M)

What differs

  • The formal statement carries no dimension hypothesis, and neither does Milnor's. It used to carry $2 \le m$, which the punctured stage needs (V7.22) and which is empty mathematics, a connected $1$-manifold being the circle or an interval and all of those orientable. The hypothesis has now been removed, not excused: the classification of one-manifolds (V.A2) and the orientability of every connected one-manifold (V.A4) make non-orientability of a connected $M$ imply $2 \le m$, so it is derived in one line at the single point where it is used. It is gone from the three statements that carried it — the equivalence, its substantial half and the parity classification. The easy half keeps an explicit $0 < m$, as it must: it assumes neither connectedness nor non-orientability, so it has nothing to derive a dimension bound from, and $0 < m$ is only what the mod 2 degree asks of its target.
  • $M$ is a compact nonempty submanifold of a Euclidean space, as in all of §§6–7, and homotopy means smooth homotopy, a smooth map $M \times \mathbb R \to S^m$; Milnor's manifolds are subsets of Euclidean space and his homotopies are smooth, so neither restricts the mathematics.
  • The clause Milnor offers as the reason is the whole of the sign-free machinery, and it is six items long: a sign of a frame that mentions no orientation (V7.20), the sign of a chain and the chains that avoid a finite set (V7.21), the punctured dichotomy that produces the reversing loop (V7.22), the slide and the flip, which are the clause itself (V7.23), the cancellation of any two neighbours (V7.24), and the parity count with its classification (V7.25). What the theorem itself adds is the discharge of the reversing-loop hypothesis and the two halves of the equivalence.
  • The easy half assumes neither connectedness nor non-orientability, and no orientation anywhere, while the classification by parity assumes both; the two directions of the equivalence rest on very different hypotheses.

Chapter 8

Milnor §8, pp. 51–53: the seventeen problems

V8.0

Tools built for the problems

not in the book
local zero sets of submersionsframe-first orientationsdegrees with compact preimages

Milnor no counterpart in the book

The book has no counterpart here. Writing the problems "for the reader", Milnor uses without comment that a set cut out near each of its points by a submersion is a manifold with the expected tangent space, that a product of oriented manifolds is oriented by a positive basis of the first factor followed by one of the second, that $S^n$ is oriented as the boundary of the disk, and that the local degree of §5 behaves well as soon as the relevant preimages are compact.

§2 proves only the global case, that $f^{-1}(y)$ is a manifold for a regular value $y$ of a map defined on all of $M$.

Proved in Lean

Four groups of results. (i) If $M$ is boundaryless and modelled on a finite-dimensional $E$, and $F$, $Z$ are finite-dimensional with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$, a set $S \subseteq M$ such that every $x \in S$ has an open $U \ni x$ and a map $g : M \to F$, smooth on $U$, with $S \cap U = g^{-1}(0) \cap U$ and $dg_y$ onto for $y \in S \cap U$, is a submanifold given by slice charts with model $Z$ and complement $F$, and $TS_x = \ker dg_x$. (ii) For oriented $M$, $N$ of dimensions $m$, $n$ (no boundarylessness needed), $M \times N$ carries the orientation with a positive basis of $TM$ first, a product map has the product of the two signs, and the swap has sign $(-1)^{mn}$ everywhere. (iii) A submanifold of Euclidean space with a continuous complementary frame is oriented by "frame first, then tangent vectors"; this gives Milnor's orientation of $S^n$, the outward normal first for the standard orientation of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$, which for $n \ge 1$ agrees up to one global sign with the orientation of §5, and the orientation of $f^{-1}(y) \subseteq S^{d+p}$ used for the Hopf invariant. (iv) For $M$, $N$ Hausdorff, boundaryless, oriented, of the same finite dimension $n$ and with model spaces in one universe: if a neighbourhood $V$ of a regular value $y$ has compact preimage then nearby values are regular with the same local degree, and if $M$ is in addition second countable and the part of $F^{-1}(V)$ over $[0,1]$ is compact, a smooth homotopy does not change the local degree at $y$. (v) Genericity: the image of a second countable manifold of smaller dimension has dense complement, the complement of the diagonal in $S^p \times S^p$ is connected for $p \ge 1$, and a function locally constant near every point of a preconnected set is constant on a dense subset of it.

What differs

  • §2 makes a global level set a manifold; the local criterion asks only for a defining map near each point, which is what Problems 8 to 11 actually supply.
  • The sphere orientation of §5 was built from a basis chosen by the axiom of choice; for $n \ge 1$ it agrees with Milnor's only up to a global sign, so linking numbers needed a new one.
  • Putting the complementary frame first, rather than the tangent vectors as in §5, changes the orientation of a preimage by $(-1)^{dp}$, which is $+1$ in the case $p = d + 1$ used later.
  • The degree results replace compactness of the source by compactness of $f^{-1}(V)$, and add Hausdorff, second countability and a common universe for the two model spaces.
V8.1

Problem 1: the degree of a composition

hypotheses explicit
$N$ compact, so $\deg g$ exists$P$ Hausdorff, not compactpreconnected, so possibly empty

Milnor §8, Problem 1 · p. 51

The degree of a composition $g \circ f$ is the product $(\deg g)(\deg f)$.

One line, with no hypotheses named: the degrees are those of §5, so the manifolds are the compact oriented ones of that section.

Proved in Lean

Let $f : M \to N$ and $g : N \to P$ be smooth maps of oriented boundaryless manifolds whose model spaces all have dimension $n$ and lie in a common universe. Assume $M$ and $N$ compact, Hausdorff and second countable, $P$ Hausdorff, and $N$ and $P$ preconnected. Then $\deg(g \circ f) = \deg g \cdot \deg f$. The proof picks a $z$ regular for both $g$ and $g \circ f$, notes that every $y \in g^{-1}(z)$ is then a regular value of $f$, and splits the signed count over $(g \circ f)^{-1}(z)$ along the fibres of $f$.

    deg oM oP (fun x => g (f x)) = deg oN oP g * deg oM oN f := …

What differs

  • Milnor names no hypotheses; the formal statement needs $N$ compact for $\deg g$ to exist, and uses that compactness again to make the regular values of $g$ open.
  • $P$ is only assumed Hausdorff and preconnected: it is neither compact nor second countable, and it may be empty, in which case both sides are $0$ by the definition of the degree.
  • Preconnected is weaker than Milnor's connected, so the empty target is covered rather than excluded.
  • The three model spaces must lie in one universe, a condition of the formal degree of §5 and not of the mathematics.
V8.2

Problem 2: the degree of a polynomial map

restated
Riemann sphere, not the Gauss sphereneeds $\deg P \ge 1$

Milnor §8, Problem 2 · p. 51

Every complex polynomial of degree $n$ gives rise to a smooth self-map of the Gauss sphere $S^2$ of degree $n$.

The sphere is $S^2 \subseteq \mathbb R^3$, identified with $\mathbb C \cup \{\infty\}$ by stereographic projection as in §1.

Proved in Lean

The Riemann sphere $\mathbb C \cup \{\infty\}$ is given the orientation that is the standard orientation of $\mathbb C = \mathbb R^2$ in both charts $z$ and $1/z$; this is consistent because the chart change has complex derivative $w \ne 0$, whose real determinant is $|w|^2 > 0$. For a polynomial $P$ of degree at least $1$, the extension of $P$ by $P(\infty) = \infty$ is a smooth self-map of the Riemann sphere, and its degree for that orientation on both sides is the degree of $P$. At a regular value $y \in \mathbb C$ the preimages are the distinct simple roots of $P - y$, at each of which the derivative is multiplication by $P'(z) \ne 0$, of sign $+1$.

Milnor/Chapter8/PolyDeg.lean, line 154  ·  Milnor.Problems.deg_polyMap

theorem deg_polyMap (P : Polynomial ℂ) (hP : 0 < P.degree) :
    deg riemannOr riemannOr (polyMap P) = P.natDegree := …

Milnor/Chapter8/PolyDeg.lean, line 123  ·  Milnor.Problems.riemannOr

    orSign Complex.orientation Complex.orientation (cmul w) = 1 := …

What differs

  • Milnor's $S^2 \subseteq \mathbb R^3$ is replaced throughout by the Riemann sphere with the charts $z$ and $1/z$, as already in §1; no identification of the two is formalized.
  • The hypothesis $\deg P \ge 1$ is named and used: for a constant $P$ the extension by $P(\infty) = \infty$ is not even continuous at $\infty$.
  • The orientation of the Riemann sphere has to be constructed before the statement can be made, since the sphere is not presented as a level set.
V8.3

Problem 3: maps at distance less than 2

stronger
$X$ any topological spacemodel space may be infinite-dimensionalhomotopy on $X \times \mathbb R$

Milnor §8, Problem 3 · p. 51

If two maps $f$ and $g$ from $X$ to $S^p$ satisfy $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < 2$ for all $x$, then $f$ is homotopic to $g$, the homotopy being smooth if $f$ and $g$ are.

He does not say what $X$ is; in the smooth half it must be a manifold.

Proved in Lean

Two statements. For an arbitrary topological space $X$ and continuous $f, g : X \to S^p$ with $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < 2$ everywhere, $f$ and $g$ are homotopic by the normalized chord $((1-t)f + tg)/\|(1-t)f + tg\|$, which never meets the origin. If $X$ is a smooth manifold, modelled on a real normed space that need not be finite-dimensional, with no Hausdorff, countability or boundarylessness hypothesis, and $f$, $g$ are smooth with the same bound, they are smoothly homotopic.

What differs

  • The continuous half assumes nothing about $X$ beyond a topology, where Milnor's $X$ is a manifold.
  • The smooth half allows any model space, finite-dimensional or not, with or without boundary, and asks neither that $X$ be Hausdorff nor that it be second countable.
  • Smooth homotopy here means a smooth map on $X \times \mathbb R$, so the chord is reparametrized by a transition function that is $0$ near $t = 0$ and $1$ near $t = 1$.
V8.4

Problem 4: approximation and smoothing

hypotheses explicit
$X$ compact in $\mathbb R^{K+1}$homotopy on $X \times \mathbb R$

Milnor §8, Problem 4 · p. 51

If $X$ is compact, every continuous map $X \to S^p$ can be uniformly approximated by a smooth map; and two smooth maps $X \to S^p$ that are continuously homotopic are smoothly homotopic.

Compactness of $X$ is the only hypothesis he names.

Proved in Lean

Let $X \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be compact, presented by slice charts with model $\mathbb R^m$ and complement $\mathbb R^w$. For every continuous $G : X \to S^p$ and every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is a smooth $f : X \to S^p$ with $\|f(x) - G(x)\| < \varepsilon$ for all $x$; and two smooth maps $X \to S^p$ that are continuously homotopic are smoothly homotopic, the smooth homotopy being a smooth map on $X \times \mathbb R$. Underneath is a Stone–Weierstrass theorem: a map $E \to F$ of finite-dimensional real normed spaces, continuous on a compact $S \subseteq E$, is uniformly approximated on $S$ by maps smooth on all of $E$.

    letI := hM.chartedSpace; haveI := hM.isManifold;

Problem 5: maps into a higher sphere

narrower
continuous case needs $M$ compactsecond countable sourcemodel in the lowest universe

Milnor §8, Problem 5 · p. 51

If $m < p$, every map $M^m \to S^p$ is homotopic to a constant.

One line: he says neither that $M$ is compact nor whether the maps are smooth.

Proved in Lean

Two statements. If $X$ is a second countable boundaryless manifold of dimension $m < p$ whose model space lies in the lowest universe, every smooth map $X \to S^p$ is smoothly homotopic to a constant: a regular value, which Brown's theorem supplies, cannot be attained when $m < p$, and stereographic projection from the missed point contracts the map. If $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ is compact, presented by slice charts with model $\mathbb R^m$, and $m < p$, every continuous map $M \to S^p$ is homotopic to a constant, by Problems 4 and 3.

Problem 6: fixed points of self-maps of $S^n$

narrower
smooth maps only$n \ge 1$

Milnor §8, Problem 6 · p. 51

Any map $S^n \to S^n$ whose degree is different from $(-1)^{n+1}$ must have a fixed point.

He labels the problem "Brouwer" and does not say the map is smooth.

Proved in Lean

For $n \ge 1$, a smooth map $f : S^n \to S^n$ whose degree, taken with one and the same orientation of $S^n$ on source and target, is different from $(-1)^{n+1}$ has a fixed point. If it had none, $f(x)$ would never be antipodal to $-x$, so $\|f(x) - (-x)\| < 2$; Problem 3 then makes $f$ smoothly homotopic to the antipodal map, whose degree is $(-1)^{n+1}$.

    (hdeg : deg (sphOr n) (sphOr n) f ≠ (-1) ^ (n + 1)) : ∃ x, f x = x := …

What differs

  • The formal degree is defined only for smooth maps, so the statement is about smooth $f$, whereas Milnor's "any map" covers continuous ones.
  • The hypothesis $n \ge 1$ is needed so that $S^n$ is connected, as the degree of §5 requires; $S^0$ is excluded.
  • The degree is taken for the orientation of §5 on both sides; reversing that orientation on both sides changes no degree, so the value $(-1)^{n+1}$ is unaffected.
V8.7

Problem 7: odd degree and antipodal pairs

narrower
smooth maps only$n \ge 1$

Milnor §8, Problem 7 · p. 51

Any map $S^n \to S^n$ of odd degree must carry some pair of antipodal points into a pair of antipodal points.

Stated for maps in general, with no hint of a proof.

Proved in Lean

For $n \ge 1$, a smooth map $f : S^n \to S^n$ of odd degree satisfies $f(-x) = -f(x)$ for some $x$. The lemma underneath: a smooth $h : S^n \to S^n$ with $h(-x) = h(x)$ for all $x$ has even degree, because the preimage of a regular value is carried to itself by the fixed-point-free involution $x \mapsto -x$ and each of its points contributes $\pm 1$, hence $1$ modulo $2$. If $f$ had no antipodal pair, $w(x) = f(x) + f(-x)$ would never vanish and $w/\|w\|$ would be an even map never antipodal to $f$.

Milnor/Chapter8/SphereMaps.lean, line 103  ·  Milnor.Problems.exists_antipodal_of_odd_deg

    (hdeg : Odd (deg (sphOr n) (sphOr n) f)) : ∃ x, f (antip x) = antip (f x) := …

    (heven : ∀ x, h (antip x) = h x) : Even (deg (sphOr n) (sphOr n) h) := …

What differs

  • As in Problem 6 the statement is confined to smooth maps, the only ones for which the formal degree is defined.
  • $n \ge 1$ is required again, so that $S^n$ is connected and the degree of §5 is defined.
  • The parity lemma counts the preimage modulo $2$; the signs at $x$ and at $-x$ are never compared with each other.
V8.8

Problem 8: products and their tangent spaces

stronger
ambient spaces any finite-dimensionalmanifold structure also proved

Milnor §8, Problem 8 · p. 51

Given smooth manifolds $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ and $N \subseteq \mathbb R^l$, the tangent space $T(M \times N)_{(x,y)}$ is equal to $TM_x \times TN_y$.

He asks only for the tangent space; that $M \times N$ is a manifold is taken for granted.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq E_1$ and $N \subseteq E_2$ be given by slice charts in finite-dimensional real normed spaces, with models $Z_1$, $Z_2$ and complements $W_1$, $W_2$, all finite-dimensional. Then $M \times N \subseteq E_1 \times E_2$ is a submanifold with model $Z_1 \times Z_2$ and complement $W_1 \times W_2$, of dimension $\dim M + \dim N$, and at every point $T(M \times N)_{(x,y)} = TM_x \times TN_y$, tangent spaces being the ranges of the derivatives of the inclusions. The dimension identity $\dim Z_i + \dim W_i = \dim E_i$ is recorded separately and holds as soon as the submanifold is nonempty.

What differs

  • Milnor asks only for the tangent space; the formal statement also proves that $M \times N$ is a manifold, which he takes as known.
  • The ambient spaces are arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed spaces, not only $\mathbb R^k$ and $\mathbb R^l$.

Problem 9: the graph of a smooth map

stronger
ambient spaces any finite-dimensionalcomplement $W_1 \times E_2$

Milnor §8, Problem 9 · p. 51

The graph $\Gamma$ of a smooth map $f : M \to N$, the set of $(x, y) \in M \times N$ with $f(x) = y$, is a smooth manifold, and the tangent space $T\Gamma_{(x,y)} \subseteq TM_x \times TN_y$ is the graph of the linear map $df_x$.

Proved in Lean

With $M \subseteq E_1$ and $N \subseteq E_2$ as in Problem 8 and $f : M \to N$ smooth, the graph $\Gamma = \{(x, f(x))\} \subseteq E_1 \times E_2$ is a submanifold with model $Z_1$, hence of the dimension of $M$, and complement $W_1 \times E_2$; its tangent space at $(x, f(x))$ is the range of $u \mapsto (d\iota_x u,\ d\iota_{f(x)} df_x u)$, that is, the graph of $df_x$. Near a point, $\Gamma$ is the zero set of $(a, b) \mapsto ((\varphi a)_2,\ b - \tilde f(a))$, where $\varphi$ is a slice chart of $M$ and $\tilde f$ the smooth extension of $f$ it provides. Only $E_1$, $E_2$, $Z_1$ and $W_1$ are required to be finite-dimensional.

V8.10

Problem 10: the tangent bundle and $df$

stronger
ambient space any finite-dimensional$df$ reads $v$ in chart coordinates

Milnor §8, Problem 10 · p. 52

Given $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$, the tangent bundle space $TM = \{(x, v) \in M \times \mathbb R^k : v \in TM_x\}$ is again a smooth manifold; any smooth $f : M \to N$ gives rise to a smooth $df : TM \to TN$, with $d(\mathrm{identity}) = \mathrm{identity}$ and $d(g \circ f) = (dg) \circ (df)$.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq E$ be given by slice charts in a finite-dimensional real normed space, with model $Z$ and complement $W$, both finite-dimensional. Then $TM \subseteq E \times E$ is a submanifold with model $Z \times Z$ and complement $W \times W$, of dimension $2 \dim M$. For $N \subseteq E'$ of the same kind and $f : M \to N$ smooth, $df(x, v) = (f(x),\ d\iota_{f(x)} df_x u)$ is smooth, where $u$ is the chart coordinate of $v$, the unique vector with $d\iota_x u = v$. The rule $d(\mathrm{identity}) = \mathrm{identity}$ is stated with no smoothness hypothesis and without finite dimensionality of $E$, $Z$, $W$; the chain rule $d(g \circ f) = dg \circ df$ is stated for smooth $f$ and $g$.

  • The ambient space is an arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed space rather than $\mathbb R^k$, and $M$ comes with a named model $Z$ and complement $W$.

V8.11

Problem 11: the normal bundle and its tube

hypotheses explicit
$M$ nonemptydimension identity $m + w = k$second part only for $k = K + 1$

Milnor §8, Problem 11 · p. 52

The normal bundle space $E = \{(x, v) \in M \times \mathbb R^k : v \perp TM_x\}$ is a smooth manifold; and if $M$ is compact and boundaryless, $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ carries the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of $M \times 0$ in $E$ diffeomorphically onto the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood $N_\varepsilon$ of $M$ in $\mathbb R^k$.

He asks the reader to compare the product neighbourhood theorem of §7, and does not say how $\varepsilon$ is found.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ be given by slice charts with model $\mathbb R^m$ and complement $\mathbb R^w$, where $m + w = k$. Then $E \subseteq \mathbb R^k \times \mathbb R^k$ is a submanifold with model $\mathbb R^k$ and complement $\mathbb R^w \times \mathbb R^m$, so of dimension $k$; near a point it is the zero set of the normal coordinates of $x$ together with the tangential components of $v$. If in addition $k = K + 1$ and $M$ is compact and nonempty, then for $\varepsilon$ the smaller of a uniform injectivity radius and the tubular radius of §6, the map $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ is a diffeomorphism of $\{(x, v) \in E : \|v\| < \varepsilon\}$ onto $\{y : \operatorname{dist}(y, M) < \varepsilon\}$, with inverse $y \mapsto (r(y),\ y - r(y))$ for the nearest-point retraction $r$.

What differs

  • $M$ is assumed nonempty, because the tubular radius of §6 and the minimum over a finite cover of $M$ both need it; for empty $M$ the claim is trivial but is not treated.
  • Boundarylessness, which Milnor states, is automatic here: a submanifold given by slice charts has no boundary by construction.
  • The identity $m + w = k$ must be named, since model and complement are given separately, and the second part is stated for $k = K + 1$, as everywhere in §6 and §7.
  • The order of the argument is reversed: uniform injectivity of $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ is proved first from compactness, and $r(x + v) = x$ is deduced from it.
V8.12

Problem 12: the retraction, and maps into $M$

restated
$r$ is the nearest-point retraction$M$ nonemptystrict inequality for every $z \ne x$

Milnor §8, Problem 12 · p. 52

Define $r : N_\varepsilon \to M$ by $r(x + v) = x$; then $r(x + v)$ is closer to $x + v$ than any other point of $M$, and using this retraction one proves the analogue of Problem 4 with the sphere $S^p$ replaced by a manifold $M$.

He defines $r$ by the formula and takes for granted that the formula determines a map.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be compact and nonempty, with model $\mathbb R^m$, complement $\mathbb R^w$, $m + w = K + 1$, and $\varepsilon$ as in Problem 11. Here $r$ is the nearest-point retraction of §6, and for $(x, v)$ in the normal bundle with $\|v\| < \varepsilon$ one has $r(x + v) = x$ together with $\|(x + v) - x\| < \|(x + v) - z\|$ for every $z \in M$ other than $x$. For a compact $X \subseteq \mathbb R^{L+1}$ given by slice charts with model $\mathbb R^l$, every continuous map $X \to M$ is a uniform limit of smooth maps, and two smooth maps $X \to M$ that are continuously homotopic are smoothly homotopic. The intermediate step, that two smooth maps closer than the tubular radius are joined by $r((1-s)f + sg)$, is stated without compactness of $X$.

V8.13

Problem 13: the linking number

stronger
smooth maps, not embedded M and Ntotal dimension k > 0bounding in the ambient {g ≥ 0} form

Milnor §8, Problem 13 · p. 52

For disjoint manifolds $M, N \subseteq \mathbb R^{k+1}$ the linking map $\lambda : M \times N \to S^k$ is $\lambda(x, y) = (x - y)/\|x - y\|$. When $M$ and $N$ are compact, oriented and boundaryless with $m + n = k$, the degree of $\lambda$ is the linking number $l(M, N)$. Milnor asks for the symmetry $l(N, M) = (-1)^{(m+1)(n+1)} l(M, N)$, and for $l(M, N) = 0$ whenever $M$ bounds an oriented manifold $X$ disjoint from $N$.

He leaves the orientations of $M \times N$ and of $S^k$ unsaid, does not exclude $k = 0$, and gives no meaning to "bounds" beyond the word.

Proved in Lean

The formal statement replaces Milnor's embedded $M, N$ by smooth maps $\alpha : A \to \mathbb R^{k+1}$ and $\beta : B \to \mathbb R^{k+1}$ with disjoint images, where $A$ and $B$ are compact Hausdorff second countable oriented boundaryless manifolds of dimensions $a$ and $b$ with $a + b = k$; the linking map is $\lambda(x, y) = (\alpha(x) - \beta(y))/\|\alpha(x) - \beta(y)\|$ and $l(\alpha, \beta)$ is its degree, for the product orientation of $A \times B$ and the orientation of $S^k$ as the boundary of the disk. If $k > 0$, then $l(\beta, \alpha) = (-1)^{(a+1)(b+1)} l(\alpha, \beta)$. If $k > 0$ and $A = \{g = 0\}$ is the boundary, with the boundary orientation, of a compact $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ inside a boundaryless oriented $(a+1)$-manifold $W$ with $0$ a regular value of $g$, and if $\alpha$ extends to a smooth $\bar\alpha : W \to \mathbb R^{k+1}$ with $\bar\alpha(X)$ disjoint from $\beta(B)$, then $l(\alpha, \beta) = 0$.

  • Milnor's disjoint $M, N \subseteq \mathbb R^{k+1}$ are the case in which $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are inclusions; the formal statement allows any two smooth maps with disjoint images.

Problem 13: linking numbers in the sphere

stronger
the book only asks for a definitionindependence of the rotation provedneeds a, b ≥ 1

Milnor §8, Problem 13 · p. 52

The last sentence of Problem 13 asks the reader to define the linking number for disjoint manifolds in the sphere $S^{m+n+1}$.

It asks for a definition and supplies neither a construction nor the hypotheses under which one is well posed.

Proved in Lean

Let $A$ and $B$ be compact oriented boundaryless manifolds of dimensions $a$ and $b$ with $a + b = k$, and $\alpha : A \to S^{k+1}$, $\beta : B \to S^{k+1}$ smooth with disjoint images. A rotation $R$ of $\mathbb R^{k+2}$, that is a linear isometry of positive determinant, is admissible when it carries both images off the north pole; the linking number in the sphere is then the Euclidean linking number of the stereographic projections of $R \circ \alpha$ and $R \circ \beta$ from the north pole onto $\mathbb R^{k+1}$, and is set to $0$ when no admissible rotation exists. Admissible rotations exist as soon as $A$ and $B$ are second countable, since the two images are meagre in $S^{k+1}$; the formal existence statement also carries $a, b \ge 1$, which its proof does not use. If $a, b \ge 1$ and $A$, $B$ are compact Hausdorff and second countable, every admissible rotation gives the same value.

V8.14

Problem 14: the Hopf invariant

narrower
p ≥ 2smooth homotopiesmixed form needs disjoint preimages

Milnor §8, Problem 14 (The Hopf Invariant) · p. 52

If $y \ne z$ are regular values of a map $f : S^{2p-1} \to S^p$, the preimages $f^{-1}(y)$ and $f^{-1}(z)$ carry the orientations of §5, so their linking number is defined. Milnor asks: a) that $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ be locally constant as a function of $y$; b) that if $y$ and $z$ are regular values of $g$ as well and $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < \|y - z\|$ for all $x$, the three numbers $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$, $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ and $l(g^{-1}(y), g^{-1}(z))$ agree; c) that the value depend only on the homotopy class of $f$ and not on the choice of $y$ and $z$. That integer is the Hopf invariant $H(f)$.

He restricts $p$ nowhere, works with continuous homotopy classes, and states a) for one map $f$.

Proved in Lean

With $p \ge 2$ and $f, g : S^{2p-1} \to S^p$ smooth, $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ is the sphere linking number of Problem 13 for the §5 preimage orientations, taken along positive bases of the tangent spaces at $y$ and $z$. a) For $y \ne z$ regular values of $f$, the map $y' \mapsto l(f^{-1}(y'), f^{-1}(z))$ is constant on a neighbourhood of $y$, and likewise in the second value; the two-map form, with $g$ in the first slot, assumes in addition that $g^{-1}(y)$ and $f^{-1}(z)$ are disjoint. b) is exactly the book's statement. c) $H(f)$ is the value at a chosen pair of distinct regular values, and $0$ on maps that are not smooth; every pair of distinct regular values gives that same value, and smoothly homotopic maps have the same invariant.

def hopfInvariant (f : Sph (d + (d + 1)) → Sph (d + 1)) : ℤ := …

What differs

  • The formal statement assumes $p \ge 2$: for $p = 1$ the preimages are finite subsets of $S^1$ and the sphere linking number of Problem 13 is not available.
  • Homotopy invariance is stated for smooth homotopies, the convention of the whole formalization, and $H$ is set to $0$ on maps that are not smooth.
  • Part a) is formalized in Milnor's case $g = f$, where $y \ne z$ already makes the preimages disjoint; the version for two maps adds the hypothesis that $g^{-1}(y)$ and $f^{-1}(z)$ be disjoint.
  • Without it one should not expect local constancy, since moving $y$ can push $g^{-1}(y)$ through $f^{-1}(z)$; that is an informal observation, and no counterexample is formalized.
V8.15

Problem 15: odd p, compositions, the Hopf map

value differs
H(π) = −1, not +1p ≥ 2, smooth mapsthree conventions flip the sign

Milnor §8, Problem 15 · p. 53

If the dimension $p$ is odd then $H(f) = 0$. For a composition $S^{2p-1} \xrightarrow{f} S^p \xrightarrow{g} S^p$, $H(g \circ f)$ is $H(f)$ times the square of the degree of $g$. The Hopf fibration $\pi : S^3 \to S^2$ is given by $\pi(x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4) = h^{-1}\!\left((x_1 + i x_2)/(x_3 + i x_4)\right)$, where $h$ is the stereographic projection to the complex plane, and Milnor asks for $H(\pi) = 1$.

He specifies $\pi$ only through that formula, and fixes no orientation conventions for $h$, for $S^2$, or for the preimages.

Proved in Lean

With $p \ge 2$ and all maps smooth: $H(f) = 0$ for odd $p$, and $H(g \circ f) = (\deg g)^2\, H(f)$. The Hopf fibration is formalized as the quadratic map $\pi(z_1, z_2) = (2 z_1 \bar z_2,\ |z_1|^2 - |z_2|^2)$ on $S^3 \subseteq \mathbb C^2$ with $z_1 = x_1 + i x_2$ and $z_2 = x_3 + i x_4$; it equals Milnor's $h^{-1}(z_1/z_2)$ wherever $z_2 \ne 0$, for $h$ the stereographic projection from $(0,0,1)$, every point of $S^2$ is a regular value, and $H(\pi) = -1$. The two fibres used are the great circles over $(1,0,0)$ and $(-1,0,0)$, and the value $-1$ is the product of three signs: both fibre directions are negatively oriented as preimages, and the derivative of the linking map sends them to a negatively oriented basis. The comparison with other sign conventions below is an informal consequence of the formal sign lemmas, not itself a formal theorem.
