What differs

  • Milnor asks for $H(\pi) = 1$; with his conventions taken literally the formal value is $-1$, a matter of convention rather than an error in the mathematics.
  • Those conventions are: $S^k$ oriented as the boundary of the disk, outward normal first; preimages oriented as in §5; $A \times B$ a product; $\lambda(x, y) = (x - y)/\|x - y\|$.
  • Any one of three equally natural changes restores $+1$: taking $\lambda(x, y) = (y - x)/\|y - x\|$, ordering the product as $B \times A$, or reversing the orientation of the target $S^2$ of $\lambda$.
  • Reversing the orientation of $S^3$, or of the target $S^2$ of $\pi$, changes nothing, since both preimage orientations reverse at once; the other two parts need only $p \ge 2$ and smoothness.
V8.16

Problem 16: transversality

stronger
injective, δ-close deformationcodimension p + p′ and tangent spaceneeds p + p′ ≤ m; M compact nonempty

Milnor §8, Problem 16 · p. 53

Two submanifolds $N$ and $N'$ of $M$ intersect transversally if at each $x \in N \cap N'$ the subspaces $TN_x$ and $TN'_x$ together generate $TM_x$; when $n + n' < m$ this means that $N \cap N' = \emptyset$. Milnor asks that a framed submanifold $N$ be deformable slightly so as to meet a given $N'$ transversally, and that the resulting intersection be a smooth manifold.

"Deformed slightly" is left imprecise, and he asks only that the intersection be a manifold, not what its codimension or tangent space is.

Proved in Lean

As in §7, $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ is a submanifold modelled on $\mathbb R^m$ and $N, N'$ are submanifolds of $M$ of dimensions $n, n'$ and codimensions $p, p'$, with all tangent spaces read inside $\mathbb R^{K+1}$; transversality is $TN_x + TN'_x = TM_x$ at every common point. If $n + n' < m$, transverse submanifolds are disjoint. If $p + p' \le m$, a transverse intersection is a submanifold of $M$ of codimension $p + p'$ whose tangent space at $x$ is $TN_x \cap TN'_x$. For a framed $N$ with a product neighbourhood $G : N \times B_\varepsilon \to M$, which needs $M$ compact and nonempty, and for any $\delta > 0$, there is $u$ with $\|u\| < \delta$ such that $N_u = G(N \times \{u\})$ is a submanifold, $x \mapsto G(x, u)$ is injective and moves no point of $N$ by as much as $\delta$, and $N_u$ meets $N'$ transversally.

What differs

  • "Deformed slightly" is made precise: the translate $N_u = G(N \times \{u\})$ inside a product neighbourhood is transverse to $N'$, injectively parametrized, and within any prescribed $\delta$ of $N$.
  • The intersection is not only a manifold: it is a submanifold of $M$ of codimension $p + p'$ with tangent space $TN_x \cap TN'_x$, stated under the hypothesis $p + p' \le m$.
  • The deformation goes through the Product Neighborhood Theorem of §7 and so assumes $M$ compact and nonempty; the statements about intersections assume neither.
  • Transversality is formulated with the tangent spaces read inside the ambient $\mathbb R^{K+1}$, as everywhere in §7, and $M$ itself is a submanifold of Euclidean space.
V8.17

Problem 17: the intersection product

narrower
p, q ≥ 1 and p + q ≤ mexplicit framing of the intersectionuniqueness added, for p ≥ 1

Milnor §8, Problem 17 · p. 53

Let $\Pi^p(M)$ be the set of all framed cobordism classes of codimension $p$ in $M$. Milnor asks that the transverse intersection operation be used to define a correspondence $\Pi^p(M) \times \Pi^q(M) \to \Pi^{p+q}(M)$.

He says nothing about how the intersection is to be framed, and does not mention that transverse representatives have to be found first.

Proved in Lean

$\Pi^p(M)$ is the set of framed cobordism classes of framed submanifolds of the compact nonempty $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, indexed by the dimension $n$ as well as the codimension $p$ and empty unless $n + p = m$. For transverse framed $F$ of codimension $p$ with framing $v$ and $G$ of codimension $q$ with framing $v'$, with $p + q \le m$, the intersection is framed by $w_k = A^*(AA^*)^{-1} e_k$, where $\alpha_F$ is the linear map sending each $v_i$ to $e_i$ and vanishing on the vectors orthogonal to all the $v_i$, $\alpha_G$ is its counterpart for $v'$, and $A = (\alpha_F, \alpha_G)$ is surjective along the intersection by transversality. For $p \ge 1$ every pair of classes has transverse representatives. For $p, q \ge 1$ with $p + q \le m$ there is a map $\Pi^p(M) \times \Pi^q(M) \to \Pi^{p+q}(M)$ carrying the classes of transverse $F, G$ to the class of the framed intersection, and for $p \ge 1$ it is the only such map.

What differs

  • The formal product is built for $p, q \ge 1$ and $p + q \le m$; $p, q \ge 1$ comes from the theorems of §7 that the proof uses, whose arguments rotate regular values, and is not claimed necessary.
  • Transverse representatives exist, and the product is determined by its values on them, under $p \ge 1$ alone; the book states neither fact.
  • The framing is written out: $w_k$ is the vector in the span of $v$ and $v'$ for which $(\alpha_F w_k, \alpha_G w_k)$ is the $k$-th standard basis vector of $\mathbb R^p \times \mathbb R^q$.
  • $\Pi^p(M)$ carries the dimension $n$ as a second index and is empty unless $n + p = m$, and $M$ is required to be compact and nonempty, as it already is in §7.
V8.17′

Problem 17: Πᵖ(M) as an abelian group

hypotheses explicit
indexed by n, with n + p = mzero and inverse namedredundant hypothesis p ≥ 1

Milnor §8, Problem 17 · p. 53

If $p \ge \tfrac12 m + 1$, Milnor asks that the disjoint union operation be used to make $\Pi^p(M)$ into an abelian group, comparing with the corresponding discussion on p. 49.

One line of exercise, with the zero and the inverses left entirely to the reader.

Proved in Lean

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be compact and nonempty, let $n + p = m$, and let $m + 2 \le 2p$, which is Milnor's condition read over the integers. Then any two classes in $\Pi^p(M)$ have disjoint representatives, and if $F \sim F_1$ and $G \sim G_1$ with $F, G$ disjoint and $F_1, G_1$ disjoint, then $F \sqcup G \sim F_1 \sqcup G_1$, so disjoint union descends to classes. It makes $\Pi^p(M)$ an abelian group whose zero is the class of the empty submanifold and in which $-[F]$ is the class of $F$ with its first framing vector reversed. The formal group structure also takes $p \ge 1$ as a hypothesis, which is redundant, since $m + 2 \le 2p$ implies it.

What differs

  • $\Pi^p(M)$ is a set of classes indexed by the dimension $n$ as well as by the codimension $p$, and $n + p = m$ is needed for the empty submanifold to count as a framed submanifold at all.
  • The zero is the class of the empty submanifold and $-[F]$ the class of $F$ with its first framing vector reversed; the book leaves both to the reader.

  • Milnor's $p \ge \tfrac12 m + 1$ is written $m + 2 \le 2p$, and $M$ is compact, nonempty and embedded in $\mathbb R^{K+1}$.

Appendix

Milnor pp. 54–56: classifying one-manifolds

V.A

Compact 1-manifold: even number of boundary points

stronger
topological, not smoothclassification not used, see V.A2every chart sends $B$ to $0$

Milnor Appendix, Theorem · p. 54

Milnor proves that every smooth connected 1-manifold is diffeomorphic to the circle $S^1$ or to an interval of real numbers, so that up to diffeomorphism there are exactly four connected 1-manifolds. The boundary count is nowhere stated in the Appendix: it is used in the text, on p. 14, in the form that a compact 1-manifold is a union of finitely many circles and arcs and so has an even number of boundary points, with a footnote pointing to the Appendix for the proof.

The proof rests on parametrization by arc length and on Lemma 1 (p. 54), that two such parametrizations overlap in at most two components, two components forcing $S^1$.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a compact Hausdorff space with a charted-space structure over a model $H$ homeomorphic to the half-line $[0,\infty)$, and let $B \subseteq M$ be a set such that every chart of the atlas sends exactly the points of $B$ in its domain to coordinate $0$. Then $B$ is finite and has an even number of points. Nothing beyond the topology is assumed: charts are homeomorphisms, there is no smooth structure, no arc length, and neither connectedness nor second countability is required.

Milnor/Appendix/OneManifold.lean, line 1678  ·  Milnor.OneManifold.even_ncard_boundary

theorem even_ncard_boundary [CompactSpace M] (hB : BoundaryCompatible φ B) :
    B.Finite ∧ Even B.ncard := …

Milnor/Appendix/OneManifold.lean, line 198  ·  Milnor.OneManifold.BoundaryCompatible

def BoundaryCompatible (B : Set M) : Prop := …

Milnor/Appendix/OneManifold.lean, line 34  ·  Milnor.OneManifold.Arc

Milnor/Appendix/OneManifold.lean, line 1601  ·  Milnor.OneManifold.exists_closed_arc

theorem exists_closed_arc [CompactSpace M] (hB : BoundaryCompatible φ B) {p : M} (hp : p ∈ B) :
    ∃ A : Arc M B, IsFrom A p ∧ sSup A.target ∈ A.target := …

What differs

  • Milnor's 1-manifolds are smooth and his proof uses arc length; the formal theorem assumes only a topological 1-manifold with boundary, which is all the book's applications need.
  • The classification is not used here, and this proof does not state it: what it gives is that the component of a boundary point is the image of an arc whose coordinate interval is closed, and the circle case never arises, the parametrization being always started at a boundary point. The classification itself is now proved, in the smooth setting (V.A2), and the even count derived from it a second time (V.A3); this topological theorem is left untouched and, through V.A′, is still what Chapters 2 and 4 cite.
  • The boundary is given as a set $B$, with the hypothesis that every chart of the atlas, not only the chart at a given point, sends exactly the points of $B$ to coordinate $0$.
  • Compactness and Hausdorffness are used explicitly: the first for finiteness of $B$ and for a finite cover by chart arcs, the second to identify limits in the overlap lemma.
V.A′

Even boundary count for Chapter 2's manifolds

stronger
topological proof underneathambient model space arbitrary

Milnor §2, p. 14

In the proof of Lemma 5 Milnor needs that $f^{-1}(y)$, a compact 1-manifold whose boundary is its intersection with $\partial X$, has an even number of boundary points, and gets this from the classification: such a manifold is a union of finitely many circles and arcs. A footnote refers to the Appendix for the proof. The manifolds he applies it to are the level sets produced by his Lemma 4 inside a manifold with boundary.

He never isolates the parity statement as a lemma; it recurs in the homotopy lemma of §4, in Lemma 1 of §5, and in §6.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a Hausdorff smooth manifold over any model with corners, its model space an arbitrary real normed space, and let $X \subseteq M$ be a compact half-slice submanifold with boundary $B \subseteq X$ modelled on the half-space over a space $Z$ with a single point — that is, a compact 1-manifold with boundary in the sense of Chapter 2. Then $B$ is finite and has an even number of points. It is deduced from the topological theorem by checking that the charted-space structure that $X$ inherits sends the points of $B$ to coordinate $0$ in every chart.

What differs

  • Only $X$ is assumed compact and $M$ merely Hausdorff, and the model space of $M$ is an arbitrary real normed space, not required finite-dimensional or boundaryless.
  • Being a 1-manifold is expressed by the half-space model $\mathbb R \times Z$ having $Z$ a single point; the smooth structure of $X$ is then discarded and the topological theorem applied.
  • As in the topological form, this proof does not go through the classification Milnor proves: it gives only the parity of the boundary count, which is the sole consequence the book uses. The classification is proved separately (V.A2) and yields the same count a second time, with each component identified and the pairing named (V.A3); this theorem is untouched and is the one Chapters 2 and 4 cite.
V.A1

Appendix Lemma 1: the overlap of two arcs, and the affine transition

stronger
no dimension hypothesis, no compactnesstopological half: no smoothness at allthe circle case counted, not excluded

Milnor Appendix, Lemma 1 · p. 54

Milnor's one lemma of the Appendix (p. 54). Let $f : I \to M$ and $g : J \to M$ be parametrizations by arc length of a smooth 1-manifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ — diffeomorphisms onto open subsets whose velocity has Euclidean length $1$. Then $f(I) \cap g(J)$ has at most two connected components; if it has one, $f$ extends to a parametrization by arc length of $f(I) \cup g(J)$; and if it has two, $M$ is diffeomorphic to $S^1$. His proof studies the graph $\Gamma = \{(s, t) : f(s) = g(t)\}$: differentiating $g(\tau(s)) = f(s)$ for $\tau = g^{-1} \circ f$ gives $|\tau'| = 1$, so $\tau'$ is continuous with values $\pm 1$ and constant on each component, $\Gamma$ is a union of segments of slope $\pm 1$ running from edge to edge of the rectangle $I \times J$, and the four surviving pictures give the four cases.

He states the lemma only for arc-length parametrizations, and says so: a merely monotone transition would leave the constant and the shape of the reparametrization free.

Proved in Lean

Let $M$ be a Hausdorff topological space charted on $H$, with $I : \mathrm{ModelWithCorners}\ \mathbb R\ E\ H$ over an arbitrary real normed space $E$; let $L$ be a length element, a density indexed not by charts but by local real parameters, so that it can be read along a chart and along an arc with no layer between; and let $A$, $A'$ be $C^\infty$ arcs of unit speed for $L$ — homeomorphisms from open subsets of $M$ onto bounded intervals of $\mathbb R$, smooth in both directions, whose coordinate advances at unit rate. Write $S$ for the overlap, the set of $A$-parameters whose point lies in $A'$ as well, and take the components of $S$ to be its maximal subintervals. Then: on each component the transition $s \mapsto A'(A^{-1}(s))$ is $s \mapsto \varepsilon s + k$ with $\varepsilon = \pm 1$; $S$ has at most two components; and two distinct components reach opposite ends of $A$'s interval, one running down to its infimum while the other runs up to its supremum. There is no dimension hypothesis, no manifold instance, no compactness and no boundarylessness: only Hausdorffness, which the overlap API needs to identify two limits. The second and third clauses use no smoothness whatever. The extension clause is separate and strong: the glued arc is again a smooth arc of unit speed, its source is the union of the two sources, and its coordinate equals the old one on the whole of the old source.

What differs

  • Milnor's $M$ is a smooth 1-manifold in a Euclidean space and his parametrizations are by Euclidean arc length. The formal statement asks only that $M$ be Hausdorff and charted on a model with corners over an arbitrary real normed space, with unit speed taken against a length element; it carries no dimension hypothesis, no manifold instance, no compactness and no boundarylessness, and it holds for a model whose range is a compact interval, which is a one-manifold with corners.
  • The lemma splits, and only one half needs unit speed. That the overlap has at most two components, and that two components reach opposite ends, hold for arcs that are merely homeomorphisms from open subsets of a Hausdorff space onto bounded intervals — no smoothness and no arc length anywhere. What genuinely needs unit speed is the affine form $s \mapsto \pm s + k$ of the transition, which comes in one line from the cocycle rule of the length element: read in a unit-speed parameter the density is $1$, so $1 = 1 \cdot |\tau'|$, and a continuous nowhere-zero $\tau'$ cannot change sign on a component.
  • The two-component case is counted, not excluded. The topological Appendix (V.A) avoids it by normalizing the first arc to start at a boundary point, which rules out the circle by fiat; here there is no hypothesis on where either arc starts, and the case that forces the circle appears as one of the alternatives. The device the earlier file lacked is the inner component, the one both of whose ends are interior to the first arc's interval: it runs off both ends of the other arc at once, so the transition carries it onto that arc's whole interval, and injectivity then makes it the only component. Two byproducts: the inner case is exactly the case where one arc sits inside the other, and an inner component forces the other arc to contain no boundary point at all.
  • Milnor's normalization turns out to have been doing nothing essential, and his third clause — two components force $S^1$ — is not proved here as a statement about $M$; it is the two-component alternative, and the circle is identified in V.A2 by a different route, through unit-speed curves of the whole line.
  • The extension clause is strong in a way the topological theorem cannot be. Because the transition is $s \mapsto +s + k$ with the constant pinned down from either side, the old parameter is the new parameter on the old source, so the glued arc genuinely extends the first rather than merely containing its image up to reparametrization; the topological theorem's conclusion is an existential that forgets the witness's coordinate, from which the transition could be recovered only as an increasing homeomorphism fixing $0$ — it could be $s \mapsto s^2$. About seventy-five lines of the topological proof then disappear: both one-sided limit computations at the junction and the trichotomy proofs of continuity there, because with unit-speed arcs the two branches are the same affine function on the whole overlap and there is no seam to be continuous at.
V.A2

The classification of one-manifolds: Milnor's Appendix theorem

hypotheses explicit
Hausdorff and $\sigma$-compact, both neededexactly one: all six exclusions provedthe long line is the counterexample

Milnor Appendix, Theorem · p. 54

Every smooth connected 1-manifold, with or without boundary, is diffeomorphic to the circle $S^1$ or to an interval of real numbers — open, closed or half-open, bounded or not — so that up to diffeomorphism there are exactly four of them. Milnor proves it by taking a maximal parametrization by arc length, Lemma 1 supplying the overlap picture at each step; the reduction of an arbitrary interval to a normal form he leaves to a parenthesis, an affine map for a bounded interval and $t \mapsto \tanh t$ for an unbounded one; and the distinctness of the four he asserts without proof. No countability hypothesis is stated, his manifolds being subsets of $\mathbb R^k$.

Chapters 2, 4, 5 and 6 quote only the consequence, the even boundary count, and the footnote on p. 14 points here for the proof.

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ be a real normed space with $\dim E = 1$, let $I : \mathrm{ModelWithCorners}\ \mathbb R\ E\ H$, and let $M$ be a topological space charted on $H$ which is Hausdorff, $\sigma$-compact, connected — hence nonempty — and a $C^\infty$ manifold over $I$. Then $M$ is $C^\infty$-diffeomorphic to the circle, to $[0, 1]$, to $[0, \infty)$ or to $\mathbb R$, each with its own model with corners, and to exactly one of them. A sharper form keys the four alternatives to two invariants: compact with empty boundary gives the circle, compact with a boundary point $[0, 1]$, non-compact with a boundary point $[0, \infty)$, non-compact with empty boundary $\mathbb R$. Exclusivity is proved, all six pairs: compactness settles four of them, and the number of boundary points — a diffeomorphism invariant, because a diffeomorphism carries boundary onto boundary — has the values $0$, $2$, $1$, $0$ and settles the remaining one in each compactness class. The boundary is Mathlib's, and no compatibility hypothesis on the charts is assumed: the chart-independence of boundary points is proved. Nothing is assumed about the boundary, about orientability or about second countability. Nine stages and some nine thousand three hundred lines: the one-dimensional setup, with the model classified into the line, a closed half-line and a compact interval, the last a one-manifold with corners kept alive throughout; smooth arcs and their transitions; the length element, which is where Hausdorffness and $\sigma$-compactness are spent, and unit-speed arcs; the affine transition and the extension theorem (V.A1); and the four cases — the compact one with boundary by exhausting a component by arcs, the circle by a unit-speed curve of the whole line with a propagation lemma in place of an exhaustion, and the two non-compact ones by explicit parametrizations of the six shapes of open interval and half-line, with no inverse function theorem anywhere.

What differs

  • Milnor states no countability hypothesis and the theorem is false without one: the long line — two copies of the closed long ray $\omega_1 \times [0, 1)$ glued along their least elements — is a connected Hausdorff smooth one-manifold without boundary diffeomorphic to none of the four, not being second countable. So the formal statement assumes $\sigma$-compactness, which Milnor has for free, his manifolds sitting in $\mathbb R^k$, and which he uses silently: the Euclidean metric he restricts to $M$ is a Riemannian metric, and a manifold carrying one is metrizable. It is spent in exactly one place in the whole development, the existence of the length element, and only the two non-compact cases need it; in the compact cases compact plus Hausdorff plus a finite-dimensional model already gives second countability.
  • "Exactly four" is asserted in the book and proved here. All six exclusions are theorems: four by compactness, two by the number of boundary points, which is a diffeomorphism invariant because a diffeomorphism carries the boundary onto the boundary. The boundary of the model half-line had to be computed by hand, Mathlib having no half-line manifold; $[0, \infty)$ was given its structure here, as a one-chart manifold with boundary whose chart is a homeomorphism onto the whole model half-space.
  • Milnor's "an interval of real numbers" becomes the three normal forms $[0, 1]$, $[0, \infty)$ and $\mathbb R$, which is his own parenthesis carried out, and each model carries its own model with corners. Hausdorffness is assumed rather than inherited from an ambient $\mathbb R^k$, and the model space is an arbitrary real normed space of dimension one rather than $\mathbb R$ itself.
  • Where the design pass was wrong, and this is the substantive correction of the programme: the exhaustion of a manifold by arcs has no boundaryless analogue. Every arc of a boundaryless manifold has an open interval for its target, while the extension theorem needs the first arc's target to be half-open, that is, to start at a boundary point. The circle is proved instead with unit-speed curves of the whole line — maps agreeing near each time with the arc-length parametrization of some arc — whose engine is a propagation lemma, that two such curves agreeing near one time agree on the whole interval, proved by a connectedness argument run in the parameter of one arc rather than in the time, because the set of times of local agreement need not be closed, and the obstruction is exactly that an overlap can have two components.
  • Three bridges had to be built which the book does not need. The connected components of a smooth one-manifold are open, hence manifolds in their own right, which is what lets a theorem about connected manifolds reach a disconnected one, and is the piece the design pass never mentions. The boundary of the half-line model is a single point. And the boundary count for a possibly disconnected compact one-manifold is proved from scratch, its finiteness being the harder half, because V.A wants a compatibility hypothesis available only when the model range is a half-line.
  • A byproduct closes a hypothesis of V.A. For a smooth one-manifold whose model range is a half-line, Mathlib's boundary satisfies the compatibility condition that the topological theorem has to assume — every chart of the atlas, not only the chart at a point, sends exactly the boundary points to coordinate $0$ — with no side condition at all, and $C^1$ suffices. So V.A applies to smooth one-manifolds unconditionally.
V.A3

The even boundary count, derived from the classification

stronger
derived from the classificationeach interval gives exactly two pointsthe pairing: the two ends of one arc

Milnor Appendix, Theorem, last clause · p. 54

The last sentence of the Appendix theorem: a compact 1-manifold with boundary has an even number of boundary points. Milnor derives it from the classification — a compact manifold has finitely many components, since the components are open and form a cover with no proper subcover, and each is a circle with no boundary points or a closed interval with two. This is the only consequence of the Appendix the book uses: it is quoted on p. 14 in the proof of Lemma 5, again in the homotopy lemma of §4, in Lemma 1 of §5 and in §6, each time with a footnote pointing to the Appendix.

The pairing behind the parity is the two ends of one arc; Milnor leaves it in the picture and counts components instead.

Proved in Lean

The hypotheses of V.A′, and four conclusions read off from the classification. Let $M$ be a Hausdorff $C^\infty$ manifold over any model with corners, its model space an arbitrary real normed space; let $Z$ be a normed space with a single point; and let $X \subseteq M$ be a compact half-slice submanifold with boundary $B \subseteq X$ — that is, a compact 1-manifold with boundary in the sense of Chapter 2. Then every connected component of $X$, which is open and so a manifold in its own right, is $C^\infty$-diffeomorphic to the circle or to $[0, 1]$. The components meeting $B$ are exactly the ones diffeomorphic to $[0, 1]$, and the ones missing $B$ exactly the circles, both stated as equivalences. Each component that meets $B$ meets it in exactly two points. And every boundary point $p$ has exactly one partner: a boundary point $p' \ne p$ with $B \cap (\text{component of } p) = \{p, p'\}$, which is the pairing, realised by the two ends of one arc-length parametrization of that component. Hence $B$ is finite with an even number of points — the conclusion of V.A′, with a different proof: the old one counts a fixed-point-free involution built from the topological Appendix's arc extension, this one runs the classification on each component. V.A′ is left in place, is still what Chapters 2 and 4 cite, and is not used here; the only thing borrowed from the topological file is the combinatorial lemma that a fixed-point-free involution of a finite set has even cardinality.

What differs

  • The conclusion is V.A′'s and the proof is the book's, where V.A′ argues by parity alone: the components are identified and counted, rather than an involution being exhibited on the boundary of a topological manifold. Both theorems stand, V.A′ is what Chapters 2 and 4 cite, and this row uses nothing of it beyond the combinatorics of a fixed-point-free involution.
  • More is proved than the book states. Milnor says a compact one-manifold is a union of finitely many circles and arcs; the formal statement says which components are which, by an equivalence in each direction — a component meets the boundary exactly when it is diffeomorphic to $[0, 1]$, and misses it exactly when it is a circle — gives each component of the second kind exactly two boundary points, and names the partner of each boundary point, which is the pairing the parity comes from.
  • Finiteness of the boundary is the harder half for a possibly disconnected compact one-manifold, and it could not be taken over from V.A: that theorem assumes the chart compatibility condition, which is available only when the model range is a half-line. It is proved here for the abstract manifold first and then transported.
  • Hypotheses as in V.A′ and no more: only $X$ is compact, $M$ is merely Hausdorff, its model space is an arbitrary real normed space, and being a one-manifold is expressed by the half-space model $\mathbb R \times Z$ with $Z$ a single point. Unlike V.A′, the smooth structure of $X$ is not discarded — it is what the classification runs on — and $\sigma$-compactness is free, $X$ being compact.
  • That the components of a one-manifold are open, hence manifolds in their own right, is what lets a theorem about connected manifolds reach a disconnected one. It needs only that the model space is one-dimensional: every point lies in the source of an arc, and the source of an arc is preconnected. No compactness, no Hausdorffness and no length element.
V.A4

Connected one-manifolds are orientable

not in the book
the book has no use for the statementboundaryless model, and only the sourceMathlib's $[0,1]$ has two charts

Milnor Appendix · p. 54

The book never states this and never needs it. Orientability as a property of a manifold is nowhere defined (V7.18), and for one-manifolds the question does not arise: Milnor's one-manifolds are the level sets $f^{-1}(y)$ in a Euclidean space, where a tangent line is a line in one fixed $\mathbb R^k$, and the signs §5 attaches to the two ends of an arc are ambient, read off from the ambient orientation rather than from an orientation of the arc. The classification of the Appendix would make the statement immediate, the circle and every interval being orientable, but Milnor draws no such conclusion, having no use for one: his remark of p. 50 restricts itself to a non-orientable $M$ and says nothing about why such an $M$ cannot be one-dimensional.

This row exists to remove a hypothesis, not to record a result of the book: it is what makes $2 \le m$ derivable in V7.26.

Proved in Lean

Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional real normed space, $I : \mathrm{ModelWithCorners}\ \mathbb R\ E\ H$ with $I$ boundaryless, and $M$ a Hausdorff, $\sigma$-compact, connected $C^\infty$ manifold charted on $H$. If $\dim E = 1$ then $M$ is orientable in the sense of V7.18. The classification (V.A2) makes $M$ diffeomorphic to the circle if it is compact and to $\mathbb R$ if it is not — the two interval models are excluded because the model is boundaryless, so the boundary of $M$ is empty — and orientability travels back along the diffeomorphism. Hence, with $\dim E = m$: a connected non-orientable $M$ has $2 \le m$, dimension $0$ being orientable because in dimension $0$ every linear map preserves every orientation, the only tuple to test being the empty one, and dimension $1$ by the previous statement. The same in the vocabulary of Chapters 7 and 8 — a compact connected non-orientable slice submanifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ charted on $\mathbb R^m$ has $2 \le m$ — where every hypothesis comes free: $M$ is Hausdorff as a subspace of a metric space, $\sigma$-compact because compact, and its model is boundaryless, so its boundary is empty. On the way: the pullback of an orientation along an invertible linear map and its uniqueness, a space of dimension $n$ having exactly two orientations; the transport of an orientation along any smooth map of manifolds of the same dimension whose derivative is everywhere invertible, hence in particular along a diffeomorphism; and all four models of the classification oriented. The file sits in Chapter 7, where the statement is used.

What differs

  • No counterpart in the book, and none is wanted there: Milnor's one-manifolds arise as level sets in a Euclidean space and carry no orientation of their own, orientability as a property of a manifold being undefined in the book (V7.18). The row is here to remove a hypothesis, and V7.26 is the only thing that uses it.
  • Transport of an orientation is proved along any smooth map between manifolds of the same dimension whose derivative is everywhere invertible: neither injectivity, nor surjectivity, nor being a local diffeomorphism is needed. It assumes boundarylessness of the source only, the target being allowed a boundary, and that single restriction is why the row is stated for boundaryless models — a chart target of the source has to be open in the model space for the map read in charts to have a genuine derivative there, continuous in the point. Transport into a manifold whose model has a boundary is not proved, is not needed, and is where the file stops.
  • All four models of the classification are oriented, and one of them is not free. Mathlib's manifold structure on $[0, 1]$ has two charts, $z \mapsto z$ on $[0, 1)$ and $z \mapsto 1 - z$ on $(0, 1]$, whose transition reverses orientation, so a constant family is not an orientation there and the orientation has to flip at the right endpoint; that one transition derivative is computed by factoring the chart at $1$ through the chart at an interior point and the reflection of the model space. The circle was oriented not through its own charts but through the unit circle $S^1 \subseteq \mathbb R^2$, which Chapter 8 orients as the boundary of the disk, the classification supplying the diffeomorphism; that is shorter than either direct route, the sphere being oriented already.
  • What the row buys is exactly the deletion of the dimension hypothesis from V7.26: non-orientability of a connected manifold now implies $2 \le m$, so the hypothesis the punctured stage needs (V7.22) is derived rather than assumed. Nothing else in the project depends on it.

Not formalized

What the book states and the formalization does not prove, and what it does instead

G.1

The index sum is the Euler characteristic

not formalized

Milnor §6, pp. 35 and 40

Milnor states the Poincaré–Hopf theorem in the form that the index sum equals the Euler number $\chi(M) = \sum_i (-1)^i \operatorname{rank} H_i(M)$, and refers to Hopf and to the literature for the identification; p. 40 sketches the three further steps it would take.

In the formalization

What is proved is the integer, not its name. V6.9 produces an integer that is the index sum of every smooth tangent field with finitely many zeros on a compact submanifold of Euclidean space, and identifies it with the degree of the Gauss map, which is the form Milnor proves. V6.10 and V6.14 make it computable, as a sum of signs of determinants, and V6.15 computes it for the sphere: it is $1 + (-1)^n$, and it is pinned down, any integer with the Poincaré–Hopf property being that one. What is missing is the name. The identification of that integer with the Euler characteristic is not formalized, and the statement cannot even be written down: Mathlib has no Euler characteristic of a topological space, no Betti numbers and no singular or cellular homology with ranks to alternate, so $\chi(S^n) = 1 + (-1)^n$ is not a sentence of the library. Supplying it would mean building a homology theory and computing it for manifolds — the work of a library rather than of a chapter — and Milnor does not prove it either, referring to Hopf's theorem. It is therefore not planned, and it is the one place where this edition is further from the book than a count of lines suggests. Out of reach for a related reason: the Poincaré–Hopf theorem for a manifold not presented as a submanifold of Euclidean space, which would need Whitney's embedding theorem, Milnor working with $N \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ as well.
